15.2 Multiplier-ideal envelopes and I-goodness

Definitions, results, and proofs on this subject.

16 Multiplier-ideal envelopes and -goodness

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Throughout this chapter, $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n$ and $\theta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, which need not be closed. We fix a smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm{d}V$ on $X$ and take $L^1$ norms with respect to it. For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$ denotes the extended non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ of theorem 6.1.2; its total mass may be infinite. We write $u\preceq v$ and $u\sim v$ for the ordinary singularity order and equivalence of definition 1.12.1, and $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}v$ and $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$ for inclusion and equality of the multiplier ideals $\mathcal I(ku)$ and $\mathcal I(kv)$ at every weight $k>0$, as in section 1.12. Finally, $P_\theta [u]$ and $P_{\theta ,F}[u]$ denote the ordinary singularity envelopes of definition 1.19.4, below zero and below a smooth obstacle $F$, and $V_\theta =P_\theta (0)$ is the potential with minimal singularities of section 1.19.

Multiplier ideals record weighted holomorphic integrability. Two potentials can have the same multiplier ideals at every weight although their ordinary envelopes differ, so passing from the ordinary singularity type of a potential to its multiplier-ideal class loses information. Analytic approximation makes this loss visible: along dyadic Demailly approximations, the envelopes converge to the greatest nonpositive representative of the multiplier-ideal class, which may exceed the ordinary envelope of the original potential.

In the Kähler theory, equality of these two envelopes is also characterized by a mass condition. In a nonclosed background the equality remains meaningful even when the potential has infinite mass, and we therefore study both envelopes along analytic approximations. Examples on curves show that this still does not give continuity of mass or completeness, because diffuse polar curvature can disappear in the limit. The same examples explain why the obstacle has to be taken into account when the background changes.

16.1 The Kähler characterization

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

First suppose that $X$ is Kähler, that $\theta $ is a smooth closed big real $(1,1)$-form, and that $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has positive non-pluripolar mass. Let $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ be the multiplier-ideal envelope of definition 16.2.1 below, and let $d_ S$ be the singularity pseudometric of [Section 6.2, SGPT]. The characterization of $\mathcal I$-good singularities in [Theorem 7.1.1, SGPT] states that the following three conditions are equivalent:

\[ \begin{gathered} u\text{ lies in the }d_ S\text{-closure of analytic singularities},\\ \int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u) =\int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\theta (P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}),\\ P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}. \end{gathered} \]

The last condition involves only envelopes. It therefore offers a starting point for the Hermitian problem even where the metric and the mass characterizations are unavailable.

The integral on the right of the middle condition is the multiplier-ideal volume of [SGPT]. The upper and lower volumes of definition 13.3.1, by contrast, vary the potential within its ordinary singularity type. The distinction matters in a nonclosed background, where even a bounded change of the potential can alter its total mass.

16.2 Ideal projection

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to the general setting fixed at the beginning of the chapter. The multiplier-ideal envelope is the greatest nonpositive potential in a multiplier-ideal class. Taking a supremum over the class could a priori enlarge the multiplier ideals; the increasing-limit form of strong openness rules this out, and the envelope is then a projection onto the class.

Definition 16.2.1. For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ define

\[ P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I} :=\left(\sup \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):v\leq 0, \ v\sim _{\mathcal I}u\} \right)^*. \]

As for ordinary envelopes, the members of the defining family are called competitors. The family is nonempty because it contains $u-\sup _ Xu$; bounded perturbations do not change multiplier ideals. Since it is bounded above by zero, its regularized supremum is a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function. The envelope depends only on the multiplier-ideal class of $u$.

Theorem 16.2.2. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}\sim _{\mathcal I}u,\qquad P_\theta [P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}]_{\mathcal I}=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}. \]

The regularization in definition 16.2.1 does not change the pointwise supremum. Moreover,

\[ \sup _ X P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}=0. \]

Proof. Write $q=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$. By Choquet's lemma, some sequence from the defining family has regularized supremum $q$. Replacing its terms by successive finite maxima, we obtain by lemma 1.16.4 an increasing sequence $v_ j$ that remains in the family and converges to $q$ almost everywhere.

Fix $x\in X$ and $\lambda >0$. On a coordinate ball about $x$, choose one smooth function $r$ such that every $v_ j+r$ and $q+r$ is plurisubharmonic; this is possible because all these functions have complex Hessian bounded below by the same smooth form $-\theta $. The increasing-limit assertion of theorem 1.16.3 gives

\[ \mathcal I(\lambda (q+r))_ x =\mathcal I(\lambda (v_ j+r))_ x =\mathcal I(\lambda u)_ x \]

for all large $j$; the second equality holds because smooth additions do not change multiplier ideals. Hence $q\sim _{\mathcal I}u$.

Since $q\leq 0$, it belongs to its own defining family. The pointwise supremum is therefore at least $q$, and it is at most its regularization $q$; this proves the assertion about regularization. The defining families for $P_\theta [q]_{\mathcal I}$ and $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ coincide, which proves idempotence. Finally, if $\sup _ Xq<0$, then $q-\sup _ Xq$ would be a larger competitor, which is impossible. $\square$

Ideal projection commutes with pullback by a modification because both the potential class and the all-weight ideal class descend.

Proposition 16.2.3. Let $\mu :Y\to X$ be a modification of connected compact complex manifolds. For every smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ on $X$ and every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ P_{\mu ^*\theta }[u\circ \mu ]_{\mathcal I} =P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}\circ \mu . \]

Proof. By proposition 1.22.12, pullback identifies the potentials in the two backgrounds. Surjectivity of $\mu $ identifies their nonpositivity constraints, and lemma 1.22.17 identifies their all-weight multiplier-ideal classes. Hence pullback gives a bijection of the complete competitor families. The ideal envelope is itself a competitor and is their greatest member by theorem 16.2.2. Its pullback is therefore the greatest competitor upstairs, which proves the identity. $\square$

Proposition 16.2.4. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ u-\sup _ Xu\leq P_\theta [u]\leq P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}\leq V_\theta . \]

Moreover,

\[ \begin{gathered} P_\theta [u]\sim _{\mathcal I}u,\qquad P_\theta [P_\theta [u]]_{\mathcal I}=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I},\\ P_\theta [P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}]=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}. \end{gathered} \]

Proof. Let $h\leq 0$ be a competitor for the ordinary envelope, so that $h\preceq u$. The function $\max (h,u-\sup _ Xu)$ is nonpositive, ordinarily equivalent to $u$, and dominates $h$. Since ordinarily equivalent potentials have the same multiplier ideals, it is a competitor for the ideal envelope. Taking regularized suprema gives $P_\theta [u]\leq P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$; the two outer inequalities follow directly from the defining families.

Projection and monotonicity of local integrability now give, for every $\lambda >0$,

\[ \mathcal I(\lambda u) \subseteq \mathcal I(\lambda P_\theta [u]) \subseteq \mathcal I(\lambda P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}) =\mathcal I(\lambda u). \]

Thus the ordinary envelope preserves all multiplier ideals, and it has the same ideal envelope as $u$. For $q=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$, the inequalities already proved and idempotence give $q\leq P_\theta [q]\leq P_\theta [q]_{\mathcal I}=q$. $\square$

We call $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ normalized if $\sup _ Xu=0$. Recall from definition 1.19.4 that $u$ is an ordinary model, or envelope fixed point, if $P_\theta [u]=u$; we call $u$ an ideal model if $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}=u$. Models of either kind are normalized, and by the last identity in proposition 16.2.4 every ideal model is an ordinary model. The map $u\mapsto P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ is called ideal projection.

Corollary 16.2.5. If $\theta $ is semipositive and big, then $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ has finite extended Monge–Ampère mass for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Proof. By the last identity in corollary 16.2.4, $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ is an ordinary model, that is, an envelope fixed point. The mass estimate for envelope fixed points in proposition 13.1.6 applies. $\square$

Proposition 16.2.6. For $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ u\preceq _{\mathcal I}v\quad \Longrightarrow \quad P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}\leq P_\theta [v]_{\mathcal I}. \]

For $a>0$ one has $P_{a\theta }[a u]_{\mathcal I}=aP_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$. For smooth real $(1,1)$-forms $\theta _ i$ and potentials $u_ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ i)$, $i=1,2$,

\[ P_{\theta _1}[u_1]_{\mathcal I}+P_{\theta _2}[u_2]_{\mathcal I} \leq P_{\theta _1+\theta _2}[u_1+u_2]_{\mathcal I}. \]

Proof. Let $h$ be a competitor for $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$. Since $h\sim _{\mathcal I}u\preceq _{\mathcal I}v$, the Lelong-number criterion in theorem 1.16.3 shows that $\max (h,v-\sup _ Xv)$ is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $v$. This function is nonpositive and dominates $h$, so it is a competitor for $P_\theta [v]_{\mathcal I}$; taking the supremum over $h$ proves that the envelope preserves the order. Multiplication by $a$ is a bijection between the defining families in the scaling identity. For the sum, $P_{\theta _1}[u_1]_{\mathcal I}+P_{\theta _2}[u_2]_{\mathcal I}$ is nonpositive and $(\theta _1+\theta _2)$-plurisubharmonic, and it is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $u_1+u_2$ by projection and lemma 1.16.4. It is therefore a competitor for the envelope on the right. $\square$

16.2.1 Analytic and divisorial singularities

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For a potential $a$ with analytic singularities, the multiplier-ideal order controls the ordinary order: by lemma 1.17.10, every quasi-plurisubharmonic $v$ with $v\preceq _{\mathcal I}a$ satisfies $v\preceq a$. The ideal envelope of $a$ is then a competitor for its ordinary envelope, and the two envelopes coincide. This extends [Proposition 3.2.10, SGPT] to smooth backgrounds.

Proposition 16.2.7. Let $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ have analytic singularities. Then

\[ a\sim P_\theta [a]=P_\theta [a]_{\mathcal I}. \]

In particular the common envelope has analytic singularities with the same coefficient and ideal as $a$, up to a bounded remainder.

Proof. Set $q=P_\theta [a]_{\mathcal I}$. Projection gives $q\sim _{\mathcal I}a$, so lemma 1.17.10 gives $q\preceq a$. Since $q\leq 0$, it is a competitor for $P_\theta [a]$. Together with proposition 16.2.4, this yields

\[ a-\sup _ Xa\leq P_\theta [a]\leq q\leq P_\theta [a]. \]

Hence the two envelopes are equal and ordinarily equivalent to $a$. Since they differ from $a$ by a bounded function, they inherit its analytic presentation. $\square$

The same argument applies to the potential of a countable divisorial current of finite mass, with lemma 1.17.4 in place of lemma 1.17.10.

Corollary 16.2.8. Let $S$ be a closed positive divisorial current of finite mass on a connected compact complex manifold $Z$, and choose a smooth residual decomposition $S=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ as in corollary 1.17.5. For every Hermitian form $\Omega $ with $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Z,\Omega )$,

\[ P_\Omega [v]=P_\Omega [v]_{\mathcal I}\sim v. \]

Proof. Set $q=P_\Omega [v]_{\mathcal I}$. Projection gives $q\sim _{\mathcal I}v$, and lemma 1.17.4 gives $q\preceq v$. Thus $q$ is a competitor for $P_\Omega [v]$. Together with proposition 16.2.4, this yields $v-\sup _ Zv\leq P_\Omega [v]=q\preceq v$. $\square$

16.3 Changes of background and envelope mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A smooth change of representative $(\theta ,u)\mapsto (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f,u-f)$ preserves the current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ but moves the zero obstacle. The resulting envelopes remain ordinarily equivalent after transport. In a nonclosed background, however, their masses can differ.

Proposition 16.3.1. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $f\in C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$. Set $\theta '=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$, $u'=u-f$, $m=\min _ Xf$ and $M=\max _ Xf$. Then

\[ P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}+m \leq P_{\theta '}[u']_{\mathcal I}+f \leq P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}+M. \]

The same inequalities hold for the ordinary envelopes.

Proof. If $v$ is a competitor for $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$, then $v-f+m$ is $\theta '$-plurisubharmonic, nonpositive and multiplier-ideal equivalent to $u'$. Therefore $v\leq P_{\theta '}[u']_{\mathcal I}+f-m$, and taking the envelope gives the first inequality. Conversely, a competitor $v'$ in the new background gives the competitor $v'+f-M$ in the old one, which proves the second inequality. For the ordinary envelopes, the same calculation applies with the ordinary singularity order in place of multiplier-ideal equivalence. $\square$

For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, put

\[ W_\theta (u)=\int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\theta (P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I})\in [0,+\infty ]. \]

This quantity depends on the decomposition of the current, even for smooth potentials.

Proposition 16.3.2. There are two smooth decompositions of the same positive current for which the corresponding values of $W$ are different.

Proof. Use the Hermitian form $\omega $ and the smooth function $g=u_ t$ of example 2.3.1, with $t\ne 0$ sufficiently small. Both $\omega $ and $\omega '=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$ are Hermitian, and

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}0=\omega '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(-g). \]

For a smooth potential in a Hermitian background, all multiplier ideals are trivial and zero is a competitor for its multiplier-ideal envelope, so that envelope is zero. Consequently

\[ W_\omega (0)=\int _ X\omega ^2,\qquad W_{\omega '}(-g)=\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g)^2. \]

By the explicit formula (2.3), the two integrals differ. $\square$

The underlying torus is Kähler; the dependence comes from the chosen nonclosed background. In the closed theory, the invariance of mass under bounded changes of the potential gives the missing step after proposition 16.3.1.

Singularity volumes, on the other hand, range over the entire ordinary type, so the bijection of competitors in the gauge change preserves them. Under bounded mass, proposition 30.9.1 accordingly transports ordinary models of positive volume and preserves their upper-volume comparison.

16.3.1 Envelope equality and infinite mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For each $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the equality

16.1
\begin{equation} P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I} \end{equation}

is meaningful. We call it envelope goodness in the fixed background. Its relation to mass differs from the Kähler characterization: it can hold when the potential has infinite mass while its envelope has finite mass.

Proposition 16.3.3. If a Hermitian metric $\omega $ fails the bounded mass property of definition 6.5.1, there exists $U\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ such that

\[ P_\omega [U]=P_\omega [U]_{\mathcal I}=0, \qquad U\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\omega ,0), \qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (U)(X)=+\infty , \]

where $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\omega ,0)$ is the relative envelope class of definition 13.4.1.

Proof. Take the potential $U$ of proposition 13.6.1, which has the stated ordinary envelope, class membership and infinite mass. Since $P_\omega [U]=V_\omega =0$, proposition 16.2.4 gives $P_\omega [U]_{\mathcal I}=0$. $\square$

16.4 geometry of ideal classes

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Ideal projection selects a canonical representative of each multiplier-ideal class, so the $L^1$ distance between these representatives defines a metric on the classes. Since the representatives have supremum zero, normalized compactness makes this metric totally bounded and its completion compact. The points added in the completion, however, need not be ideal models. On a curve this happens as badly as possible: approximating curvature measures by finite atomic measures shows that the completion contains every normalized potential.

Let

\[ \mathfrak S_{\mathcal I}(X,\theta ) =\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )/{\sim _{\mathcal I}},\qquad \mathfrak M_{\mathcal I}(X,\theta ) =\{ q\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):P_\theta [q]_{\mathcal I}=q\} \]

be the set of multiplier-ideal classes and the set of ideal models, and write $[u]_{\mathcal I}$ for the multiplier-ideal class of $u$. By theorem 16.2.2, the map $[u]_{\mathcal I}\mapsto P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ identifies these two sets, and every ideal envelope has supremum zero.

Proposition 16.4.1. The formula

16.2
\begin{equation} d_{\mathcal I,\theta ,\mathrm{d}V}([u]_{\mathcal I},[v]_{\mathcal I}) =\int _ X|P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}-P_\theta [v]_{\mathcal I}|\, \mathrm{d}V \end{equation}

defines a finite metric on $\mathfrak S_{\mathcal I}(X,\theta )$. This metric space is totally bounded. Its metric completion is canonically the $L^1$ closure of $\mathfrak M_{\mathcal I}(X,\theta )$ inside the compact space $\mathcal K_\theta $ of normalized $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions in theorem 1.4.2. Changing $\mathrm{d}V$ to another smooth positive probability volume form gives a bi-Lipschitz equivalent metric.

Proof. The ideal envelopes are integrable and depend only on the ideal classes, so the formula is well defined and finite; symmetry and the triangle inequality are those of the $L^1$ norm. If the distance is zero, the two envelopes agree almost everywhere, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3, and projection shows that the two ideal classes coincide. Conversely, equal ideal classes have the same envelope.

By the normalized compactness theorem, $\mathfrak M_{\mathcal I}$ lies in the compact space $\mathcal K_\theta $. Every subset of a compact metric space is totally bounded, and its completion is its closure in that space. Finally, the ratio of two smooth positive volume forms on the compact manifold $X$ is bounded above and below by positive constants, and multiplying the integral by these bounds proves the last assertion. $\square$

16.4.1 Completion on a curve

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a curve, a normalized potential is determined by its curvature measure. Replacing that measure by finite atomic approximations produces ideal models converging to the potential, even when the potential itself is not an ideal model.

Theorem 16.4.2. Let $Y$ be a compact connected Riemann surface and $\theta $ a smooth positive form of total mass one. Fix a smooth positive probability volume form on $Y$, again denoted by $\mathrm{d}V$, and use $\mathcal K_\theta $, the envelopes and the ideal metric on $Y$ in place of $X$. Then the ideal models with finitely many logarithmic poles and zero non-pluripolar mass are dense in $\mathcal K_\theta $ for the $L^1$ topology. The logarithmic coefficients can be chosen rational, so these approximants have analytic singularities in the convention of [SGPT]. Consequently the completion in proposition 16.4.1 is all of $\mathcal K_\theta $, and the metric on ideal classes is not complete.

Proof. We first show that the normalized Green potentials of finite atomic probability measures are ideal models, and then approximate the curvature measure of an arbitrary $u\in \mathcal K_\theta $ by such measures.

Let $\delta _ p$ denote the Dirac mass at $p$. For distinct points $p_1,\ldots ,p_ N\in Y$, consider a finitely supported probability measure

\[ \nu =\sum _{j=1}^ N a_ j\delta _{p_ j},\qquad a_ j>0,\quad \sum _ j a_ j=1. \]

The Green-kernel construction gives a potential $q_\nu $ with

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_\nu =\nu ,\qquad \sup _ Yq_\nu =0. \]

Indeed, choose a kernel with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ xG(x,p)=\delta _ p-\theta $. The sum $\sum _ j a_ jG(x,p_ j)$ has curvature $\nu $, and subtracting its finite supremum gives $q_\nu $. In a coordinate $z$ centered at $p_ j$, the function $q_\nu $ is $a_ j\log |z|^2$ plus a smooth function, and it is smooth away from the finite support of $\nu $. Since all of its curvature lies on its pole set, its non-pluripolar measure is zero.

We claim that $P_\theta [q_\nu ]_{\mathcal I}=q_\nu $. Let $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\theta )$ satisfy $w\sim _{\mathcal I}q_\nu $. The Lelong-number criterion of theorem 1.16.3 gives $\nu (w,p_ j)=a_ j$, where $\nu (w,p_ j)$ is the Lelong number of $w$ at $p_ j$. On a curve, this Lelong number is exactly the mass of the atom at $p_ j$ of the positive measure $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$. By distributional Stokes, that measure has total mass one, so the prescribed atoms already exhaust its mass and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w=\nu $. Thus $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w-q_\nu )=0$, and the difference is a harmonic distribution, hence a smooth function that is constant on the compact connected curve. Among these translates, the greatest nonpositive one is $q_\nu $, which proves the claim. By proposition 16.2.4, every such $q_\nu $ is also an ordinary model.

Now let $u\in \mathcal K_\theta $. Its curvature $\nu =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is a positive probability measure. Choose finitely supported probability measures $\nu _ j$ converging weakly to $\nu $. For instance, partition the compact metric space $Y$ into finitely many Borel sets of diameter tending to zero, place the mass of each set at a chosen point of it, and omit the sets of mass zero; uniform continuity of each continuous test function gives weak convergence.

The weights can be made rational. For a partition with weights $a_1,\ldots ,a_ N$, choose a large integer $M$, replace $a_ i$ by $b_ i=\lfloor Ma_ i\rfloor /M$ for $i<N$, put $b_ N=1-\sum _{i<N}b_ i$, and discard zero weights. The total absolute change is at most $2(N-1)/M$, which can be made to tend to zero along the partitions, so weak convergence is preserved. Writing $b_ i=m_ i/M$, the corresponding Green potential has the analytic type

\[ \left(\frac1M,\mathcal O_ Y\left(-\sum _ i m_ i p_ i\right)\right) \]

with smooth local remainders.

Let $q_ j=q_{\nu _ j}$. By normalized compactness, every subsequence has a further subsequence converging in $L^1$ to some $q\in \mathcal K_\theta $. Passing to the limit in the curvature identity in the sense of distributions gives

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q=\nu =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u. \]

By the same harmonic argument, $q-u$ is constant, and the constant is zero because both suprema vanish. Every cluster value is therefore $u$, so the whole sequence converges to $u$ in $L^1$. This proves the density assertion and identifies the completion.

To see incompleteness concretely, choose a nonconstant smooth real function $f$. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$, one has $\theta +\varepsilon \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f>0$, so $u=\varepsilon f-\sup _ Y(\varepsilon f)$ is a smooth, normalized and nonconstant element of $\mathcal K_\theta $. Its multiplier ideals are all trivial, so zero is a competitor and $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}=0$. Thus $u\notin \mathfrak M_{\mathcal I}(Y,\theta )$, although $u$ lies in the completion. The set of ideal models is therefore not closed in its completion, and the metric is not complete. $\square$

16.4.2 Mass and capacity convergence

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The approximation in theorem 16.4.2 loses all non-pluripolar mass. For the nonconstant smooth $u$ at the end of its proof, the approximants $q_ j$ have zero non-pluripolar measure, whereas $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$.

After normalization to mean zero, the same sequence converges in the Gauduchon trace metric of corollary 5.2.1. Nevertheless, the sequence $q_ j$ does not converge to $u$ in capacity. Otherwise, since $q_ j,u\leq 0$, theorem 7.3.1 would give

\[ 0=\mathrm{e}^{q_ j}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (q_ j) \rightharpoonup \mathrm{e}^{u}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u), \]

contradicting the strict positivity of the limiting measure. Thus weak convergence of traces and $L^1$ convergence do not imply convergence in capacity, even on a curve.

The same sequence shows that ideal projection is not continuous for the $L^1$ topology: $P_\theta [q_ j]_{\mathcal I}=q_ j\to u$, whereas $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}=0$. The completion therefore distinguishes smooth normalized potentials that the quotient by multiplier-ideal equivalence identifies.

The loss of mass also occurs along sequences of fixed positive mass.

Proposition 16.4.3. In the curve setting above, for every rational $0<\tau <1$ there are ideal models $r_ j$ with analytic singularities such that

\[ \begin{gathered} d_{\mathcal I,\theta ,\mathrm{d}V}([r_ j]_{\mathcal I},[0]_{\mathcal I})\longrightarrow 0,\\ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (r_ j)(Y)=\tau , \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (r_ j)\rightharpoonup \tau \theta . \end{gathered} \]

In particular, non-pluripolar mass is not continuous for this metric, even on a family whose masses have a fixed positive lower bound.

Proof. Choose finitely supported probability measures $\nu _ j$ with rational weights converging weakly to $\theta $, and let $q_ j$ be their normalized Green potentials; the preceding proof gives $q_ j\to 0$ in $L^1$. Put $a=1-\tau $ and $r_ j=P_\theta [a q_ j]_{\mathcal I}$. The scaled potential lies in the same background, since

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(aq_ j)=(1-a)\theta +a\nu _ j\geq 0. \]

Since $aq_ j$ is a competitor, $aq_ j\leq r_ j\leq 0$, and hence $r_ j\to 0$ in $L^1$. By projection, $r_ j$ is an ideal model. By proposition 16.2.7, it has exactly the logarithmic coefficients of $aq_ j$, which are rational, with bounded local remainders, and it is locally bounded away from the finite pole set. On the curve, removing the polar part of the curvature therefore gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (r_ j)=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r_ j-a\nu _ j. \]

By distributional Stokes, this measure has total mass $1-a=\tau $. The positive curvature measures $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r_ j$ have mass one and converge as distributions, hence weakly, to $\theta $. Since $\nu _ j\rightharpoonup \theta $, the last identity gives $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (r_ j)\rightharpoonup \tau \theta $. Finally, $P_\theta [0]_{\mathcal I}=0$, so the distance in the statement is $\int _ Y|r_ j|\, \mathrm{d}V$, which tends to zero. The limiting potential $0$ has non-pluripolar measure $\theta $, of mass one. $\square$

For comparison, let $d_{S,\theta }$ denote the singularity pseudometric $d_ S$ recalled at the beginning of the chapter, computed in the background $\theta $. Since $r_ j\leq 0$, the ordered-distance formula of [Lemma 6.2.2, SGPT] applies and gives

\[ d_{S,\theta }(r_ j,0) =\frac12\left(\int _ Y\theta -\int _ Y\mathrm{MA}_\theta (r_ j)\right) =\frac{1-\tau }{2}>0. \]

Thus $d_{S,\theta }$ keeps the fixed mass gap that disappears in the elementary ideal metric.

16.4.3 Comparison with scalar normalization

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to $X$ of arbitrary dimension, with the fixed volume form $\mathrm{d}V$. The scalar pseudometric $\Delta $ of (15.28) compares the normalization scalars $B_\varphi $ of section 15.1 uniformly over all continuous logarithmic densities. When an analytic singularity is shrunk to zero, the estimate in a smaller background makes this scalar distance tend to zero. At a fixed mass, however, the normalization scalar in a closed background does not see the location of the poles.

Example 16.4.4. Let $\theta $ be semipositive and big, and let $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ have analytic singularities with $a\leq 0$. Use the normalization scalars $B_\varphi $ and the sets $\mathcal A_\varphi (G)$ of supporting probabilities from section 15.1, section 15.2, defined with the fixed volume form $\mathrm{d}V$. Set

\[ \varphi _ t=P_\theta [ta],\qquad t\in \mathbb Q\cap (0,1). \]

By proposition 16.2.7, $\varphi _ t\sim ta$; since the ordinary envelope depends only on the ordinary type, $P_\theta [\varphi _ t]=\varphi _ t$. Moreover $ta\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,t\theta )$ and $ta\leq \varphi _ t$, so the envelope $P_{t\theta }(\varphi _ t)$ in the smaller background is not identically $-\infty $. By proposition 15.7.1,

\[ \sup _{G\in C(X)}|B_{\varphi _ t}(G)-B_0(G)| \leq -n\log (1-t)\longrightarrow 0 \qquad (t\downarrow 0). \]

When $\theta $ is Hermitian and $G$ is fixed and smooth, every choice in $\mathcal A_{\varphi _ t}(G)$ converges weakly in $L^1$ to the smooth Gauduchon adjoint probability, by corollary 15.7.4, theorem 15.2.5.

By contrast, the projective references in example 15.10.2 have equal mass and identical normalization functionals, although their poles and multiplier ideals differ.

16.5 Limits of analytic envelopes

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By the curve construction of theorem 16.4.2, convergence of potentials alone does not determine the limit of their ideal envelopes. Two further conditions do: a common lower bound in the multiplier-ideal order, and convergence of all divisorial Lelong numbers. Together they force every cluster value of the envelopes to have the multiplier ideals of the limiting potential, and maximality of the envelope then identifies the cluster value. The dyadic analytic approximants satisfy both conditions.

In this section, $\theta $ is again an arbitrary smooth real $(1,1)$-form. We fix $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and a Hermitian form $h$ on $X$, which will absorb the curvature errors of the approximants. Recall from section 1.17 that a local divisor $E$ over $X$ is a prime divisor on a smooth modification $\pi $ of a coordinate neighborhood in $X$, and that $\nu _ E(u)=\nu (u\circ \pi ,E)$ is the generic Lelong number of the pullback of $u$ along $E$.

Proposition 16.5.1. Let $\epsilon _ j\geq 0$ tend to zero, and let $a_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\epsilon _ jh)$ satisfy

\[ u\preceq _{\mathcal I}a_ j,\qquad \nu _ E(a_ j)\longrightarrow \nu _ E(u) \quad \text{for every local divisor }E\text{ over }X. \]

Then

\[ P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[a_ j]_{\mathcal I} \longrightarrow P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I} \quad \text{in }L^1. \]

If the $a_ j$ have analytic singularities, their ordinary envelopes converge to $P_\theta [u]$ in $L^1$ if and only if $P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$.

Proof. Set $p=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ and $q_ j=P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[a_ j]_{\mathcal I}$. Projection and the ideal order give

\[ p\leq P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[p]_{\mathcal I}\leq q_ j\leq 0. \]

The first inequality holds because $p$ is a competitor in the larger background, and the second follows from proposition 16.2.6, since $p\sim _{\mathcal I}u\preceq _{\mathcal I}a_ j$. All $q_ j$ have supremum zero and belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +Ch)$ for a fixed $C\geq \sup _ j\epsilon _ j$. By normalized compactness, every subsequence therefore has a further subsequence converging in $L^1$. Let $q$ be such a limit. Passing to the limit in the current inequalities gives $q\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and lemma 1.3.3 gives $p\leq q\leq 0$.

Fix a local modification $\pi :Z\to U$ and a prime divisor $E\subset Z$. By lemma 1.22.13, the chosen subsequence still converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ after pullback. Since $q_ j\sim _{\mathcal I}a_ j$, the two functions have the same divisorial Lelong numbers, and the pointwise semicontinuity in lemma 1.17.6 yields

\[ \nu _ E(q)\geq \limsup _ j\nu _ E(q_ j) =\lim _ j\nu _ E(a_ j)=\nu _ E(u). \]

Indeed, at every point of $E$ the pullback of each $q_ j$ has Lelong number at least $\nu _ E(q_ j)$; it remains to apply the pointwise inequality at a point of $E$ where the pullback of $q$ has Lelong number $\nu _ E(q)$. On the other hand, $p\leq q$ gives $\nu _ E(q)\leq \nu _ E(p)=\nu _ E(u)$. Thus $q$ and $u$ have the same Lelong numbers along all local divisors. Blowing up a point of any local modification recovers the pointwise Lelong number there as a generic divisorial number; on a curve the point is already a divisor. By theorem 1.16.3, $q\sim _{\mathcal I}u$.

Consequently $q$ is a competitor for $p$, so $q\leq p$. Every cluster value is therefore $p$, which proves convergence of the whole sequence. For analytic $a_ j$, proposition 16.2.7 identifies the ordinary and ideal envelopes, and the last assertion follows from uniqueness of $L^1$ limits and lemma 1.3.3. $\square$

The divisorial convergence follows from inclusions of multiplier ideals at fixed weights. The local Bergman estimate bounds the error in terms of the log discrepancy, and this error tends to zero after division by the weight.

Corollary 16.5.2. Let $\epsilon _ j\geq 0$ tend to zero, and let $a_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\epsilon _ jh)$ satisfy $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}a_ j$. Suppose that, for every integer $m\geq 1$ and every $\delta >0$,

\[ \mathcal I((1+\delta )m a_ j)\subseteq \mathcal I(mu) \quad \text{for all sufficiently large }j. \]

Then the conclusions of proposition 16.5.1 hold.

Proof. Fix a local divisor $E$ over a coordinate neighborhood $U$, and write $A=A_ U(E)$ for its log discrepancy. By lemma 1.17.8, the assumed inclusion implies

\[ (1+\delta )m\nu _ E(a_ j) \geq \operatorname {ord}_ E\mathcal I((1+\delta )m a_ j) \geq \operatorname {ord}_ E\mathcal I(mu) \geq m\nu _ E(u)-A \]

for all large $j$. Taking the lower limit in $j$ first, and then letting $m\to \infty $ and $\delta \downarrow 0$, we obtain $\liminf _ j\nu _ E(a_ j)\geq \nu _ E(u)$. The opposite inequality $\nu _ E(a_ j)\leq \nu _ E(u)$ holds for every $j$ because $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}a_ j$. Hence proposition 16.5.1 applies. $\square$

The common ideal bound cannot be omitted, even when all divisorial Lelong numbers converge. In the atomic approximation of theorem 16.4.2, every individual atom can tend to zero while the limiting potential is not an ideal model.

Example 16.5.3. On a compact connected curve $Y$ with a positive form $\theta $ of total mass one, there are normalized $a_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\theta )$ with analytic singularities and a smooth $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\theta )$ such that

\[ a_ j\longrightarrow u\text{ in }L^1,\qquad \nu _ E(a_ j)\longrightarrow \nu _ E(u)\text{ for every local divisor }E, \]

but $P_\theta [a_ j]=P_\theta [a_ j]_{\mathcal I}\to u\ne P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ in $L^1$.

Proof. Choose $u$ smooth, nonconstant and normalized, with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$, and apply the construction in theorem 16.4.2 to the smooth probability measure $\nu =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Since $\nu $ has no atoms, the largest mass of a cell tends to zero along partitions of vanishing diameter. Choose the rational approximations of the weights so that their total absolute error tends to zero. The resulting atomic probability measures $\nu _ j$ converge weakly to $\nu $, and their largest atoms tend to zero.

By the same theorem, their normalized Green potentials $a_ j$ have analytic singularities, satisfy $P_\theta [a_ j]=P_\theta [a_ j]_{\mathcal I}=a_ j$, and converge to $u$ in $L^1$. At every point $y\in Y$,

\[ 0\leq \nu (a_ j,y)=\nu _ j(\{ y\} ) \leq \max _{z\in Y}\nu _ j(\{ z\} )\longrightarrow 0=\nu (u,y). \]

Since local modifications of a smooth curve are isomorphisms, this gives all the required divisorial limits. As $u$ is bounded and $\theta >0$, both of its envelopes are zero, and since $u$ is not constant, the envelopes do not converge to them. Each $a_ j$ has a logarithmic pole, so $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}a_ j$ fails. $\square$

16.5.1 Dyadic regularization

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Demailly regularization provides the common ideal bound, since its approximants lie above $u$, and the Bergman estimate gives convergence of each divisorial Lelong number. Along dyadic indices, subadditivity of multiplier ideals also orders the ordinary singularity types of the approximants, which makes their envelopes decrease pointwise. This is the mechanism used in [Theorem 7.1.1, proof of $(3)\Rightarrow (1)$, Step 1, SGPT].

Apply theorem 1.15.1 to $u$, with $\gamma =-\theta $, along the indices $m_ j=2^ j$, starting at a sufficiently large index. Denote the resulting potentials by $a_ j$, and choose numbers $\epsilon _ j>0$ decreasing to zero such that

16.3
\begin{equation} a_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\epsilon _ jh),\qquad a_ j\geq u,\qquad a_ j\text{ has type }(1/m_ j,\mathcal I(m_ ju)). \end{equation}

These approximants satisfy $a_ j\to u$ in $L^1$. Their values need not decrease, but their ordinary singularity types do: the subadditivity property in (1.41) gives

\[ \mathcal I(2m_ ju)\subseteq \mathcal I(m_ ju)^2, \qquad a_{j+1}\preceq a_ j, \]

where the ordinary comparison follows by taking logarithms of local generators, with a constant that may depend on $j$.

Theorem 16.5.4. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and every choice in (16.3), put

\[ q_ j=P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[a_ j]. \]

Then $q_ j$ has analytic singularities, and

\[ q_ j=P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[a_ j]_{\mathcal I} \searrow P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I} \]

pointwise and in $L^1$. The limit is independent of the choice of the approximants $a_ j$, of the error bounds $\epsilon _ j$ and of the metric $h$.

Proof. By proposition 16.2.7, $q_ j=P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[a_ j]_{\mathcal I}\sim a_ j$. Since the backgrounds decrease and $a_{j+1}\preceq a_ j$, the function $q_{j+1}$ is a competitor for $P_{\theta +\epsilon _ jh}[a_ j]$. Hence $q_{j+1}\leq q_ j$.

The inequality $a_ j\geq u$ gives $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}a_ j$. For every local divisor $E$, lemma 1.17.8 gives

\[ \nu _ E(a_ j)=\frac1{m_ j}\operatorname {ord}_ E\mathcal I(m_ ju) \longrightarrow \nu _ E(u). \]

By proposition 16.5.1, $q_ j\to p:=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ in $L^1$, and $p\leq q_ j\leq 0$. The decreasing limit of the $q_ j$ is therefore bounded below by $p$; it is a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function that agrees with $p$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere. This identifies the limit in terms of $\theta $ and the multiplier-ideal class of $u$ alone, which proves the asserted independence. $\square$

Corollary 16.5.5. For the sequence above, the following are equivalent:

\[ P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I},\qquad q_ j\longrightarrow P_\theta [u]\text{ in }L^1, \qquad q_ j\searrow P_\theta [u]\text{ pointwise}. \]

They hold for one choice in (16.3) if and only if they hold for every such choice. For the fixed volume form $\mathrm{d}V$,

\[ \lim _ j\int _ X(q_ j-P_\theta [u])\, \mathrm{d}V =\int _ X(P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}-P_\theta [u])\, \mathrm{d}V\geq 0, \]

and the vanishing of this limit is a further equivalent condition.

Proof. The theorem identifies both limits of the $q_ j$ as $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ for every such choice, and its $L^1$ convergence gives the integral formula. By proposition 16.2.4, the limiting difference is nonnegative. Its integral therefore vanishes exactly when the two envelopes agree almost everywhere, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3. This proves all the equivalences. $\square$

16.5.2 Absorbing the curvature error

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When $\theta =\omega $ is Hermitian, we can take $h=\omega $, and dividing the approximants by $1+\epsilon _ j$ absorbs the curvature error. If the error bounds are rational, the analytic coefficients remain rational, so the whole approximation takes place in the fixed class $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$.

Corollary 16.5.6. Let $\omega $ be Hermitian and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. There are $b_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with analytic singularities and decreasing ordinary singularity types, such that $b_ j\to u$ in $L^1$ and

\[ P_\omega [b_ j]=P_\omega [b_ j]_{\mathcal I}\searrow P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}. \]

Consequently the classes of potentials with analytic singularities are dense in the whole metric space of ideal classes of proposition 16.4.1. The equality $P_\omega [u]=P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}$ is equivalent to convergence of these ordinary envelopes to $P_\omega [u]$ in $L^1$.

Proof. Use $h=\omega $ in (16.3). Enlarge the error bounds to positive rational numbers $\epsilon _ j\downarrow 0$, and set

\[ c_ j=\frac1{1+\epsilon _ j},\qquad b_ j=c_ ja_ j. \]

Then $c_ j\uparrow 1$ is rational and

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ j =c_ j\bigl ((1+\epsilon _ j)\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j\bigr )\geq 0. \]

The analytic coefficient of $b_ j$ is $c_ j/m_ j$. Moreover $b_ j\to u$ in $L^1$, since $a_ j\to u$ there and the sequence has bounded $L^1$ norm. Because $a_{j+1}\preceq a_ j$, $c_{j+1}\geq c_ j$, and each $a_ j$ is bounded above, one has $b_{j+1}\preceq b_ j$.

Put $q_ j=P_{(1+\epsilon _ j)\omega }[a_ j]=P_{(1+\epsilon _ j)\omega }[a_ j]_{\mathcal I}$. The preceding theorem gives $q_ j\searrow P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}$ pointwise and in $L^1$. Since $b_ j$ has analytic singularities, proposition 16.2.7 and the homogeneity in proposition 16.2.6 give

\[ r_ j:=P_\omega [b_ j]=P_\omega [b_ j]_{\mathcal I}=c_ jq_ j. \]

Here $q_ j\leq 0$ decreases and $c_ j\uparrow 1$, so $r_ j$ also decreases, with pointwise limit $P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}$. Moreover

\[ P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}\leq q_ j\leq r_ j\leq 0. \]

Since the lower bound is integrable, dominated convergence gives convergence in $L^1$.

The distance in proposition 16.4.1 from $[b_ j]_{\mathcal I}$ to $[u]_{\mathcal I}$ is exactly the $L^1$ distance from $r_ j$ to $P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}$, so it tends to zero. The last assertion follows because the limit of the $r_ j$ has been identified. $\square$

16.6 A topology on ordinary singularity types

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The ordinary and the ideal envelope of a potential record different parts of its singularity. If a topology on ordinary singularity types requires both envelopes to converge, their equality becomes a closed condition, which every limit of analytic singularity types satisfies. For the converse, the dyadic approximants of a potential with equal envelopes must converge to it. We therefore add coordinates that these approximants control in a fixed Hermitian background: controlled inclusions of multiplier ideals, and the weighted total variation of divisorial Lelong numbers on each fixed modification. The closure of the analytic types is then exactly the set of types with equal envelopes, on the whole space of ordinary types.

This topology depends on the zero obstacle hidden in the envelopes, and a smooth change of background moves that obstacle. Testing all smooth obstacles removes this dependence, and raises the question whether equality of the envelopes below zero implies equality below every obstacle. Examples of zero volume built from divisors in fibrations and products over curves answer this question on explicit classes of potentials. On these classes, polar curvature on the curve factors also gives complete metrics, which we compare with the complete topologies known in positive volume at the end of the section. From 16.6.3 on, several constructions use the volume comparison of ordinary models and the curve models of chapter 30; those results do not depend on the present section, and the reader may consult them as they are cited.

Fix a Hermitian form $\omega $ on $X$ and write

\[ \mathfrak S(X,\omega )=\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )/{\sim }. \]

In this section $[u]$ denotes the ordinary class of $u$, that is, its class modulo bounded differences. For each smooth compact modification $\pi :Y\to X$, fix a Hermitian form $\gamma _\pi $ on $Y$. For a prime divisor $E\subset Y$, set $w_ E=\int _ E\gamma _\pi ^{n-1}$, and let $\nu _ E(u)=\nu (u\circ \pi ,E)$ be the generic Lelong number of section 1.17.

Lemma 16.6.1. For $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, the sum

16.4
\begin{equation} d_\pi ([u],[v])=\sum _{E\subset Y\text{ prime}}w_ E|\nu _ E(u)-\nu _ E(v)| \end{equation}

is finite and defines a pseudometric on $\mathfrak S(X,\omega )$. Changing $\gamma _\pi $ gives an equivalent pseudometric for the fixed modification $\pi $.

Proof. The current $T=\pi ^*\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u\circ \pi )$ is positive. Divisorial removal in lemma 1.17.1 gives, for every finite family of prime divisors,

\[ \sum _ Ew_ E\nu _ E(u)\leq \int _ YT\wedge \gamma _\pi ^{n-1}<\infty . \]

Taking the supremum over finite families shows that the sum converges and that only countably many coefficients are positive. The same bound holds for $v$. Bounded changes of $u$ or $v$ do not affect the coefficients, and the triangle inequality holds term by term. Finally, two Hermitian forms on the compact manifold $Y$ bound one another by fixed positive multiples, and so do the corresponding divisor weights. $\square$

For $t>0$ and $a>1$, let

16.5
\begin{equation} \mathcal U(t,a)=\{ ([u],[v]):\mathcal I(atu)\subseteq \mathcal I(tv),\quad \mathcal I(atv)\subseteq \mathcal I(tu)\} . \end{equation}

These are relations between ordinary classes, since a bounded change leaves every multiplier ideal unchanged. Let $d_ I$ be the pullback of the metric (16.2), in background $\omega $, under $[u]\mapsto [u]_{\mathcal I}$, and put

\[ d_ O([u],[v])=\| P_\omega [u]-P_\omega [v]\| _{L^1(\mathrm dV)}. \]

Both are pseudometrics on ordinary classes: $d_ I$ compares the ideal envelopes, whereas $d_ O$ also detects differences of ordinary envelopes within a single multiplier-ideal class.

Proposition 16.6.2. Finite intersections of the relations $d_ O<r$, $d_ I<r$, $d_\pi <r$ and $\mathcal U(t,a)$, with $r,t>0$ and $a>1$, form a base of entourages for a uniformity on $\mathfrak S(X,\omega )$. Denote its topology by $\tau _{\omega ,0}$, where the subscript $0$ records the zero obstacle in the envelopes. A net $[u_ j]$ converges to $[u]$ if and only if both envelopes converge in $L^1$, $d_\pi ([u_ j],[u])\to 0$ for every fixed $\pi $, and $([u_ j],[u])\in \mathcal U(t,a)$ eventually for every fixed $t,a$. Smooth positive choices of $\mathrm dV$ and of the $\gamma _\pi $ do not change the uniformity.

Proof. All generators are symmetric and contain the diagonal. Multiplier ideals decrease as the weight increases, as one sees from local integrability after subtracting a local upper bound from the potential. For $b=\sqrt a$, put $\mathcal V=\mathcal U(t,b)\cap \mathcal U(bt,b)$. If $([x],[y]),([y],[z])\in \mathcal V$, then

\[ \mathcal I(atx)\subseteq \mathcal I(bty)\subseteq \mathcal I(tz), \]

and the same argument with $x$ and $z$ exchanged shows that $\mathcal V\circ \mathcal V\subseteq \mathcal U(t,a)$. For the pseudometric relations, halving the radius gives the corresponding refinement under composition. Taking finite intersections of these refinements proves the axioms of a uniformity, and convergence in it means eventual membership in each generating entourage. Bounded ratios of smooth positive volume forms and lemma 16.6.1 show that the auxiliary choices do not matter. $\square$

Each entourage involves only finitely many fixed modifications. The definition therefore requires neither a countable cofinal family of modifications nor convergence that is uniform over all modifications.

16.6.1 Analytic closure

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now show that the dyadic approximants of corollary 16.5.6 converge in $\tau _{\omega ,0}$ exactly when the two envelopes agree. Let $\mathcal A_\omega $ be the set of ordinary classes with analytic singularities, with rational coefficients as in section 1.13; the rational curvature correction in corollary 16.5.6 keeps the approximants in this set. In the controlled ideal relations, the scale factor of that correction has to be taken into account.

Theorem 16.6.3. The closure and the sequential closure of $\mathcal A_\omega $ in $\tau _{\omega ,0}$ are both equal to

16.6
\begin{equation} \mathcal G_\omega =\{ [u]:P_\omega [u]=P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}\} . \end{equation}

For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, the dyadic sequence $b_ j$ of corollary 16.5.6 satisfies

\[ [b_ j]\longrightarrow [u]\text{ in }\tau _{\omega ,0} \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad [u]\in \mathcal G_\omega . \]

The approximants may be chosen with one positive rational analytic coefficient on all charts.

Proof. We use the notation of corollary 16.5.6: $m_ j=2^ j$, $a_ j$ has type $(1/m_ j,\mathcal I(m_ ju))$, $c_ j=(1+\epsilon _ j)^{-1}\uparrow 1$ is rational, and $b_ j=c_ ja_ j$. The ordinary and ideal envelopes of $b_ j$ agree and decrease to $P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}$. Thus the two envelope coordinates of $[b_ j]$ converge to those of $[u]$ exactly when $[u]\in \mathcal G_\omega $. It remains to show that the other coordinates always converge.

Fix $t>0$ and $a>1$, and choose $1<s<a$. For large $j$, one has $ac_ j>s$ and $m_ j\geq st/(s-1)$, so lemma 1.14.2 gives

\[ \mathcal I(atb_ j)=\mathcal I(atc_ ja_ j) \subseteq \mathcal I(sta_ j)\subseteq \mathcal I(tu). \]

Since $u\preceq a_ j\preceq b_ j$, the reverse relation $\mathcal I(atu)\subseteq \mathcal I(tb_ j)$ holds for every $j$. The factor $c_ j$ enters the weight in the first inclusion, which is why we chose $s<a$.

For a divisor $E$ on a fixed modification $\pi $, the Bergman divisorial estimate gives

\[ c_ j\max \left\{ \nu _ E(u)-\frac{A(E)}{m_ j},0\right\} \leq \nu _ E(b_ j)\leq c_ j\nu _ E(u)\leq \nu _ E(u), \]

where $A(E)$ is the log discrepancy, computed over a coordinate neighborhood of the center of $E$. Each coefficient therefore converges to $\nu _ E(u)$, and the summability in lemma 16.6.1 gives $d_\pi ([b_ j],[u])\to 0$ by dominated convergence. This proves convergence of all the remaining coordinates.

Finally, analytic classes have equal envelopes by proposition 16.2.7. The map $[u]\mapsto (P_\omega [u],P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I})$ is continuous into $L^1\times L^1$, whose diagonal is closed. Thus every limit of a net of analytic classes lies in $\mathcal G_\omega $. The sequence $b_ j$ gives the converse and the sequential assertion. $\square$

16.6.2 Separation and mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The topology $\tau _{\omega ,0}$ sees an ordinary class only through its envelopes, its multiplier ideals and its divisorial Lelong numbers. Distinct ordinary types can have the same ordinary envelope, and such types cannot be separated.

Proposition 16.6.4. Two classes have the same $\tau _{\omega ,0}$ neighborhoods if and only if their ordinary envelopes coincide. This topology is not $T_0$ and cannot be induced by a metric on $\mathfrak S(X,\omega )$.

Proof. The coordinate $d_ O$ distinguishes different ordinary envelopes. Conversely, potentials with equal ordinary envelopes have equal multiplier ideals at every weight by corollary 16.2.4. Their ideal envelopes and all their divisorial coefficients therefore agree, so every generating entourage has identical sections at the two classes.

To produce distinct classes of this kind, choose a cutoff logarithmic pole $w\leq 0$ supported in a coordinate ball, plurisubharmonic near its pole and smooth on the annulus where the cutoff varies. After multiplication by a small positive constant, $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. Put $\chi (s)=1-\sqrt{1-s}$ for $s\leq 0$ and $u=\chi (w)$. The inequalities $0<\chi '\leq 1/2$ and $\chi ''\geq 0$, applied first to bounded truncations, give $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. In local coordinates $z$ centered at the pole, $u$ has size comparable to $\sqrt{-\log |z|^2}$, so it is unbounded and $\mathrm e^{-tu}$ is integrable for every $t>0$. Hence all its multiplier ideals are trivial and $P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}=0$.

For every $0<\delta \leq 1$, the function $\delta s-\chi (s)$ is bounded above on $(-\infty ,0]$. Hence $\delta w\preceq u$, and $\delta w$ is a competitor for the ordinary envelope of $u$. Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ off the pole gives $P_\omega [u]=0$. Thus $[u]$ and $[0]$ are distinct classes with the same neighborhoods. $\square$

By theorem 6.1.2, the two envelopes have the extended masses

\[ \int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\omega (P_\omega [u]),\qquad \int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\omega (P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}). \]

In the present positive background, both are at most $\int _ X\omega ^ n$. For the ideal envelope this follows from corollary 16.2.4, proposition 13.1.6. For the ordinary envelope, normalize $u\leq 0$ and put $p_ j=P_\omega (u+j,0)$ and $p=P_\omega [u]$, with the obstacle envelopes of section 1.19. Then $p_ j\to p$ in capacity by lemma 1.8.12. For $k>0$, set $\eta _ k=\min \{ 1,(u+k)_+\} $. If $j>k$, the contact set of $p_ j$ with $u+j$ lies outside $\{ \eta _ k>0\} $, so proposition 13.1.5 gives $\eta _ k\mathrm{MA}_\omega (p_ j)\leq \eta _ k\omega ^ n$. Testing against a nonnegative continuous function and applying lemma 6.3.1, we obtain the same inequality for $p$. Letting $k\to \infty $ proves $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (p)\leq \omega ^ n$, since the remaining set $\{ u=-\infty \} $ is pluripolar.

The potential itself can nevertheless have infinite mass: proposition 16.3.3 gives such a $u$ whose two envelopes are both zero. Its ordinary class belongs to $\mathcal G_\omega $. Thus the topology and its analytic-closure criterion need no mass condition on the representative, whereas the two canonical envelopes never have infinite mass in a Hermitian background.

16.6.3 Transport with an obstacle

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A smooth change of background $\omega '=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $ identifies $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega ')$ by $u\mapsto u-\chi $, but it moves the zero obstacle to $-\chi $. To compare the topologies in different backgrounds, we therefore need envelopes below an arbitrary smooth obstacle.

For a smooth real obstacle $F$, we use the ordinary envelope $P_{\omega ,F}[u]$ of definition 1.19.4, and we write $P_{\omega ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}$ for the ideal envelope defined with $v\leq F$ in place of $v\leq 0$. The competitor family of the latter contains a translate of $u$. Replacing a competitor by its maximum with this translate shows that the ideal envelope may equivalently be defined with the condition $v\preceq _{\mathcal I}u$. The same notation applies to any smooth real background that admits $u$. Define $\tau _{\omega ,F}$ by replacing the two envelope coordinates in proposition 16.6.2 by these obstacle envelopes.

Proposition 16.6.5. Let $\chi \in C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$ and $\omega '=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi >0$. The map $[u]\mapsto [u-\chi ]$ is a uniform isomorphism

\[ (\mathfrak S(X,\omega ),\tau _{\omega ,F}) \longrightarrow (\mathfrak S(X,\omega '),\tau _{\omega ',F-\chi }). \]

For $s>0$, $[u]\mapsto [su]$ is a uniform isomorphism from $\tau _{\omega ,F}$ to $\tau _{s\omega ,sF}$. Both maps transport the ordinary and the ideal envelopes exactly.

Proof. For either envelope operator $P$, the correspondence of competitors gives

\[ P_{\omega ',F-\chi }[u-\chi ]=P_{\omega ,F}[u]-\chi , \qquad P_{s\omega ,sF}[su]=sP_{\omega ,F}[u]. \]

Smooth additions leave all multiplier ideals and divisorial Lelong numbers unchanged. Scaling multiplies the divisorial and envelope distances by $s$ and changes the ideal weights by $t\mapsto st$. Each map and its inverse therefore transport the generating entourages to generating entourages. $\square$

For two smooth obstacles, translation of competitors gives

\[ P_{\omega ,F}[u]+\min _ X(G-F) \leq P_{\omega ,G}[u] \leq P_{\omega ,F}[u]+\max _ X(G-F), \]

and the same inequalities hold for the ideal envelopes. By the local construction in theorem 6.1.2, the extended measures satisfy $\mathrm{MA}_{\omega '}(u-\chi )=\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)$ and $\mathrm{MA}_{s\omega }(su)=s^ n\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)$, also when the total mass is infinite. If instead the zero obstacle is kept in both backgrounds, the transport gives $P_{\omega ',0}[u-\chi ]=P_{\omega ,\chi }[u]-\chi $, so the change of background compares the obstacles $0$ and $\chi $. The bounded comparison of obstacles does not make this change continuous, even on a curve.

Proposition 16.6.6. Let $Y$ be a compact connected Riemann surface and $\theta >0$ a smooth form of mass one. If $F$ is a nonconstant smooth function with $\theta '=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F>0$, then the bijection

\[ \Gamma _ F:(\mathfrak S(Y,\theta ),\tau _{\theta ,0}) \longrightarrow (\mathfrak S(Y,\theta '),\tau _{\theta ',0}), \qquad [u]\longmapsto [u-F], \]

and its inverse are both discontinuous. Equivalently, $\tau _{\theta ,0}$ and $\tau _{\theta ,F}$ are incomparable topologies on the same space of ordinary types.

Proof. The smooth function $\theta '/\theta $ has integral one against the probability measure $\theta $, and it is not constant: otherwise $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F=0$, and the harmonic function $F$ would be constant on $Y$. Choose $c$ with $\min _ Y(\theta '/\theta )<c<1$, so that $\theta '\not\geq c\theta $.

Next choose probability measures $\mu _ j$ without atoms, each carried by a polar set, that converge weakly to $\theta $. To construct them, start with finite atomic probability measures converging weakly to $\theta $, as in theorem 16.4.2, and replace each atom by a nonatomic probability measure carried by a polar Cantor set in a small coordinate disk about that atom, keeping its weight; the construction in example 30.12.7 provides such measures in every disk. If the diameters of the disks tend to zero uniformly, uniform continuity of continuous test functions preserves weak convergence, and the finite union of the polar carriers is polar.

Let $q_ j$ be the normalized Green potentials with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_ j=\mu _ j$ and $\sup _ Yq_ j=0$. By normalized compactness, the sequence has $L^1$ cluster values, and each of them has curvature $\theta $ and supremum zero, hence vanishes. Thus $q_ j\to 0$ in $L^1$. Put $u_ j=cq_ j$. Then

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j=(1-c)\theta +c\mu _ j,\qquad u_ j\leq P_\theta [u_ j]\leq 0. \]

Since $\mu _ j$ has no atoms, all point Lelong numbers of $u_ j$ vanish. Because modifications of a smooth curve are isomorphisms, theorem 1.16.3 gives trivial multiplier ideals at every weight. Thus $P_\theta [u_ j]_{\mathcal I}=0$, all controlled ideal relations with $[0]$ hold, and every divisorial distance to $[0]$ vanishes. The bounds above give convergence of the ordinary envelopes to zero in $L^1$. Consequently $[u_ j]\to [0]$ in $\tau _{\theta ,0}$.

Set $v_ j=P_{\theta ,F}[u_ j]$. Every competitor $h\preceq u_ j$ satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h\geq c\mu _ j$, by corollary 1.6.8. The family of competitors below $F$ contains a translate of $u_ j$ and is stable under finite maxima, so Choquet selection and distributional convergence of increasing maxima show that $v_ j$ satisfies the same inequality. If $[u_ j]\to [0]$ in $\tau _{\theta ,F}$, the ordinary-envelope coordinate would give $v_ j\to P_{\theta ,F}[0]=F$ in $L^1$, because $F$ is a bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic competitor. Passing to the limit in the current inequality would then give $\theta '\geq c\theta $, a contradiction.

By proposition 16.6.5, $[u]\mapsto [u-F]$ is a uniform isomorphism from $\tau _{\theta ,F}$ onto $\tau _{\theta ',0}$, so the sequence above shows that $\Gamma _ F$ is discontinuous. The same argument applied to $\theta '$ and $-F$ shows that the inverse is discontinuous. Under this transport, continuity of $\Gamma _ F$ and of its inverse are precisely the two comparisons between $\tau _{\theta ,0}$ and $\tau _{\theta ,F}$, which proves incomparability. $\square$

The sequence $u_ j$ has trivial multiplier ideals, and its polar curvature in the sense of section 1.6.1, that is, the restriction $\mathbf1_{\{ u_ j=-\infty \} }(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)$ of its curvature to its pole set, is $c\mu _ j$. By the classification of curve models through their polar curvature in lemma 30.12.1 and the bounded obstacle comparison, its ordinary envelopes in both backgrounds have non-pluripolar mass $1-c$. The discontinuity therefore persists among models whose masses are bounded below by a fixed positive constant. On the same curve, the total variation of polar curvature is a complete metric on ordinary models, by theorem 30.12.2, and after ordinary projection it is invariant under smooth changes of background; it keeps the diffuse curvature that the sequence above loses.

16.6.4 All smooth obstacles

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to the Hermitian background $\omega $ on $X$. By proposition 16.6.6, a smooth change of background need not preserve convergence when the obstacle is kept at zero. Requiring convergence below every smooth obstacle gives a topology that is stable under such changes. The dyadic approximation still determines its analytic closure, which is now described by equality of the two envelopes below every obstacle.

Let $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $ be the topology of the uniformity generated by all the uniformities $\tau _{\omega ,F}$ with $F\in C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$. Thus an entourage involves finitely many distances between obstacle envelopes, together with finitely many of the controlled ideal relations and divisorial distances defined above.

Proposition 16.6.7. A countable family $\{ F_ k\} _{k\geq 1}\subset C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$ that is dense for the uniform norm suffices for the obstacle coordinates of $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $. For smooth $\chi $ with $\omega '=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi >0$, the map $[u]\mapsto [u-\chi ]$ is a uniform isomorphism between $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $ and $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_{\omega '}$. For $s>0$, positive scaling gives the corresponding uniform isomorphism onto $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_{s\omega }$. This topology is not $T_0$ on ordinary classes.

Proof. For either envelope operator, the obstacle comparison gives

\[ \| P_{\omega ,F}[u]-P_{\omega ,G}[u]\| _{L^1(\mathrm dV)} \leq \| F-G\| _\infty . \]

Hence the two envelope distances for a pair $[u],[v]$ differ by at most $2\| F-G\| _\infty $, uniformly in the pair. The space $C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$ contains a countable subset that is dense for the uniform norm: approximate each member of a countable dense subset of $C(X)$ by smooth functions with errors $1/m$, $m\geq 1$. Given an entourage, we approximate each of its finitely many obstacles by a member of the family and shrink the radii accordingly; this proves that the family generates the same uniformity. The divisorial coordinates still range over all fixed compact modifications.

The transport in proposition 16.6.5 relabels the entire family of smooth obstacles by $F\mapsto F-\chi $, or by $F\mapsto sF$. These are bijections of the family, which proves both isomorphisms.

For nonseparation, use the unbounded function $u=1-\sqrt{1-w}$ of proposition 16.6.4. It has trivial multiplier ideals and zero divisorial Lelong numbers, and $\delta w\preceq u$ for $0<\delta \leq 1$. Fix $F$ and put $q=P_\omega (F)$, the envelope of the obstacle $F$ in the sense of section 1.19. Since $\omega >0$, the constant $\min _ XF$ is a competitor, so $q$ is bounded and $q\leq F$. The function

\[ h_\delta =(1-\delta )q+\delta w-\delta \| F\| _\infty \]

is $\omega $-plurisubharmonic, lies below $F$, and satisfies $h_\delta \preceq u$. Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ off the pole of $w$ gives $P_{\omega ,F}[u]=q=P_{\omega ,F}[0]$. Since $u$ and $0$ have the same multiplier ideals, their ideal envelopes below $F$ also agree. Every generating entourage therefore has identical sections at $[u]$ and $[0]$, although these ordinary classes differ. $\square$

Below a smooth obstacle, the ideal envelope is the ordinary envelope of the ideal model obtained with the zero obstacle. Equality of the two envelopes below every obstacle therefore reduces to a question about ordinary envelopes alone.

Proposition 16.6.8. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and put $q=P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$. For every smooth real $F$,

\[ P_{\theta ,F}[q]=P_{\theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}\sim q. \]

If $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\theta ,q)$, then also

\[ P_{\theta ,F}[u]=P_{\theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} \quad \text{for every smooth real }F. \]

Proof. Set $q_ F=P_{\theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}$. Translation of competitors gives

\[ q+\min _ XF\leq q_ F\leq q+\max _ XF, \]

so $q_ F\sim q$. The identity $q_ F-F=P_{\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F}[u-F]_{\mathcal I}$ and theorem 16.2.2 give $q_ F\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. Since $q_ F\leq F$ and $q_ F\sim q$, it is a competitor for $P_{\theta ,F}[q]$. Conversely, the maximum of a competitor for $P_{\theta ,F}[q]$ with a sufficiently low translate of $q$ is a competitor for $P_{\theta ,F}[q]_{\mathcal I}$, and this ideal envelope equals $q_ F$ because $q\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. These bounds give

\[ q_ F\leq P_{\theta ,F}[q]\leq P_{\theta ,F}[q]_{\mathcal I}=q_ F. \]

The last assertion follows from proposition 13.4.5, which gives $P_{\theta ,F}[u]=P_{\theta ,F}[q]$. $\square$

Theorem 16.6.9. The closure and the sequential closure of $\mathcal A_\omega $ in $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $ are both equal to

\[ \mathcal G^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega =\{ [u]:P_{\omega ,F}[u]=P_{\omega ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} \text{ for every smooth }F\} . \]

It suffices to test equality on the countable dense family above. The dyadic sequence $b_ j$ of corollary 16.5.6 converges to $[u]$ in this topology if and only if $[u]\in \mathcal G^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $.

Proof. Fix a smooth obstacle $F$. As in the preceding proof, transport of the obstacle and theorem 16.2.2 give $P_{\omega ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. For $a$ with analytic singularities, lemma 1.17.10 therefore makes this ideal envelope a competitor for the ordinary envelope, so $P_{\omega ,F}[a]=P_{\omega ,F}[a]_{\mathcal I}$.

Write $p=P_{\omega ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}$ and $q_ j=P_{\omega ,F}[b_ j]_{\mathcal I}$. The dyadic construction gives $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}b_ j$, decreasing ordinary types, and convergence of every local divisorial Lelong number of $b_ j$ to that of $u$. The ideal envelope preserves the ideal order, as one sees by taking maxima with a translate of $b_ j$ below $F$, so $p\leq q_ j\leq F$. Since $q_ j=P_{\omega ,F}[b_ j]$ and the ordinary types decrease, the sequence $q_ j$ decreases. Its limit $q$ is bounded below by $p$, so it is $\omega $-plurisubharmonic, and $p\leq q\leq F$; the same bounds give convergence in $L^1$.

Convergence after pullback and semicontinuity of Lelong numbers, as in proposition 16.5.1, give $\nu _ E(q)\geq \nu _ E(u)$ for every local divisor $E$, and $p\leq q$ gives the reverse inequality. The Lelong-number criterion of theorem 1.16.3 yields $q\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. Since $q\leq F$, it is a competitor for $p$, which proves

\[ P_{\omega ,F}[b_ j]=P_{\omega ,F}[b_ j]_{\mathcal I} \searrow P_{\omega ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}. \]

The controlled ideal relations and all divisorial distances on fixed modifications converge by the proof of theorem 16.6.3; they do not depend on $F$. Consequently this one dyadic sequence converges in $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $ exactly when the stated envelope equalities hold.

For each $F$, equality of the two $L^1$ envelope coordinates is a closed condition. Analytic classes satisfy all these equalities, so the closure of the analytic classes under nets lies in $\mathcal G^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $, and the dyadic sequence proves the converse. Finally, the uniform obstacle bound in the proof of proposition 16.6.7 extends equality from a dense family to every smooth obstacle. $\square$

Before turning to examples, we record how the envelopes and the relative envelope classes behave under finite unramified coverings; this will be used for fibrations with disconnected fibers. Recall from definition 13.4.1 that $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\theta ,\varphi )$ means that $u\preceq \varphi $ and that, for every $a\in (0,1)$, there is a stretching competitor $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $a\varphi +(1-a)h\leq u$; here $a=1-b^{-1}$ in the notation of that definition. Pullback of competitors and the maximum over the fibers of the covering identify the envelopes upstairs and downstairs, and the same fiber maximum transfers stretching competitors from the covering space to the base.

Proposition 16.6.10. Let $r:Y\to X$ be a finite unramified holomorphic covering between connected compact complex manifolds. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, let $u,\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and put $\theta '=r^*\theta $ and $u'=u\circ r$. For every $F\in C(X,\mathbb R)$,

\[ \begin{split} P_{\theta ',F\circ r}[u']& =P_{\theta ,F}[u]\circ r,\\ P_{\theta ',F\circ r}[u']_{\mathcal I} & =P_{\theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}\circ r. \end{split} \]

Moreover,

\[ u'\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\theta ',\varphi \circ r) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\theta ,\varphi ). \]

Proof. For $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\theta ')$, define

\[ v_{\max }(x)=\max _{y\in r^{-1}(x)}v(y). \]

Over a sufficiently small ball in $X$, this is the finite maximum of the functions obtained by composing $v$ with the local inverse branches of $r$. Each of them is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, so $v_{\max }\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ by lemma 1.3.1. If $v\preceq u'$, then $v_{\max }\preceq u$. If $v\sim _{\mathcal I}u'$, a local biholomorphic change of variables makes each branch multiplier-ideal equivalent to $u$, and the maximum rule in lemma 1.16.4 gives $v_{\max }\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. The obstacle bound $v\leq F\circ r$ gives $v_{\max }\leq F$.

Thus every competitor upstairs is dominated by the pullback of a corresponding competitor downstairs. Conversely, pullback of a competitor downstairs preserves the background inequality, the ordinary order or multiplier-ideal equivalence, and the obstacle bound. These two correspondences between the competitor families give the envelope identities; upper regularization commutes with $r$, since $r$ is locally biholomorphic.

Since $r$ is surjective, it reflects the ordinary order: $u'\preceq \varphi \circ r$ if and only if $u\preceq \varphi $. Fix $a\in (0,1)$. Pullback takes any stretching competitor $h$ with $a\varphi +(1-a)h\leq u$ to a stretching competitor upstairs. Conversely, if $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\theta ')$ satisfies

\[ a\varphi \circ r+(1-a)v\leq u', \]

then taking the maximum over the fibers gives $a\varphi +(1-a)v_{\max }\leq u$; this also holds at poles, because the convex sums are defined there. Applying the definition of the relative class for every $a$ proves the last assertion. $\square$

Taking $F=0$ gives $\mathcal G^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega \subseteq \mathcal G_\omega $. For the reverse inclusion, let $q=P_\omega [u]_{\mathcal I}$. By proposition 16.6.8, it suffices to show that $P_{\omega ,F}[u]=P_{\omega ,F}[q]$ for every $F$, and membership $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\omega ,q)$ is sufficient for this. Under bounded mass and positive lower volume of $q$, this membership is equivalent to equality of the envelopes below zero, by proposition 30.8.2. On curves the two closures coincide even at zero mass, by proposition 30.12.10. We do not know whether the reverse inclusion holds in general. The following subsections prove equality for explicit classes of potentials in higher dimension, including types of zero volume. The reduction along a divisor in a fibration gives such classes over curves, and one example of this kind has a base potential in the required relative envelope class and an ambient background with unbounded mass (example 16.6.18). Although $\tau ^{\mathrm{all}}_\omega $ removes the dependence on a fixed obstacle, it still has incomplete curve families and is strictly weaker than polar total variation on ordinary curve models, by proposition 30.12.12. On the product and fibration model loci constructed below, complete metrics come instead from polar curvature.

16.6.5 Countable poles and all obstacles

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A fixed potential that is more singular than every finite stretch of a given potential supplies ordinary competitors below all continuous obstacles. We construct such a pair with poles on any prescribed countable set. The potentials have trivial multiplier ideals even when their poles are dense, so both envelopes lose this ordinary singularity information.

Let $h$ be a Hermitian form on the connected compact complex manifold $X$ of dimension $n\geq 1$, and fix a smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm dV$. Below a continuous obstacle we use the same competitor definitions as in definition 1.19.4, definition 16.2.1, with the bound $v\leq F$.

Theorem 16.6.11. For every nonempty finite or countably infinite subset $\{ x_ j\} \subset X$, there are nonpositive $u,w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ such that

\[ h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u,\ h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\geq \tfrac 34h, \qquad u(x_ j)=w(x_ j)=-\infty . \]

The same $w$ satisfies $tw\preceq u$ for every $0<t\leq 1$, and

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,h,0),\qquad \mathrm e^{-su},\ \mathrm e^{-sw}\in L^1(X,\mathrm dV) \quad (s>0). \]

Thus $\mathcal I(su)=\mathcal I(sw)=\mathcal O_ X$ for every $s>0$. If the prescribed set is dense, both potentials are unbounded on every nonempty open set.

Proof. Fix $0<\beta <\beta '<1$. Choose a finite atlas of relatively compact charts with larger coordinate neighborhoods, on which the coefficients of $\mathrm dV$ are uniformly bounded. For each $x_ j$, choose a coordinate ball of radius $r_ j\leq 1$ centered at $z_ j$, contained in one of the larger charts. Let $\chi $ be a smooth cutoff supported inside the unit ball, with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ and $\chi =1$ near zero. On this ball set

\[ \chi _ j(z)=\chi ((z-z_ j)/r_ j),\qquad L_ j=1-\log (|z-z_ j|^2/r_ j^2). \]

The functions $-\chi _ jL_ j^\sigma $, for $\sigma =\beta ,\beta '$, extend by zero outside the ball. Near the center they are plurisubharmonic: for $s=\log (|z-z_ j|^2/r_ j^2)$ the profile $q_\sigma (s)=-(1-s)^\sigma $ has

\[ q_\sigma '=\sigma (1-s)^{\sigma -1}>0, \qquad q_\sigma ''=\sigma (1-\sigma )(1-s)^{\sigma -2}>0. \]

To justify the extension across the center, take a smooth nondecreasing $\zeta $ equal to zero on $(-\infty ,-2]$ and one on $[-1,+\infty )$. For large $A$ put

\[ q_{\sigma ,A}'(s)=q_\sigma '(s)\zeta (s/A),\qquad q_{\sigma ,A}(s)=q_\sigma (0)-\int _ s^0q_{\sigma ,A}'(v)\, \mathrm dv. \]

These profiles are convex and increasing, constant near the center, and agree with $q_\sigma $ on $s\geq -A$. As $A$ increases they decrease to $q_\sigma $, including its value $-\infty $ at the center. Radial integrability gives convergence in $L^1$. The cutoff annulus is unchanged for large $A$, and its Hessian has a finite lower bound. After a fixed local quadratic correction, the decreasing-limit theorem gives the canonical quasi-plurisubharmonic extension; its current bound passes to the limit.

Put $e_ j=2^{-j-2}$. Scale both profiles by the same $0<\delta _ j\leq 2^{-j}$ so that the nonpositive functions

16.9
\begin{equation} a_ j=-\delta _ j\chi _ jL_ j^\beta , \qquad c_ j=-\delta _ j\chi _ jL_ j^{\beta '}, \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j,\ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}c_ j\geq -e_ jh \end{equation}

have these lower bounds globally. This is possible because only the smooth cutoff annulus can contribute a negative Hessian. Coordinate rescaling and the finite atlas give

\[ \| a_ j\| _{L^1}+\| c_ j\| _{L^1}\leq C\delta _ j. \]

Consequently the nonpositive partial sums decrease with uniformly bounded $L^1$ norms. By corollary 1.4.3, they define potentials

\[ u=\sum _ j a_ j,\qquad w=\sum _ j c_ j \]

with the stated canonical values and with currents at least $(1-\sum _ j e_ j)h\geq 3h/4$. Each prescribed point is a pole of its own summand. This also proves the assertion when the prescribed set is dense.

For $b\geq 1$ let

\[ S=\sum _ j\delta _ j>0,\qquad M_ b=\max \{ 0,\sup _{L\geq 1}(bL^\beta -L^{\beta '})\} <\infty . \]

Multiplying the defining inequality by $\delta _ j\chi _ j$ and summing gives

16.10
\begin{equation} w\leq bu+M_ bS. \end{equation}

The inequality first holds for finite sums and then for their decreasing limits, including common poles. Taking $b=1/t$ gives $tw\preceq u$. Since $h>0$, convexity with zero makes $tw$ $h$-plurisubharmonic. Also $w-M_ bS\leq bu$ is an $h$-plurisubharmonic stretching competitor for every $b\geq 1$, so definition 13.4.1 gives $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,h,0)$.

It remains to prove integrability at every positive exponential scale. Fix $s>0$ and use Jensen's inequality with weights $\delta _ j/S$, assigning the unused weight in each finite sum to zero:

\[ \mathrm e^{-s\sum _{j\leq N}a_ j} \leq 1-\sum _{j\leq N}\frac{\delta _ j}{S} +\sum _{j\leq N}\frac{\delta _ j}{S} \mathrm e^{sS\chi _ jL_ j^\beta }. \]

The integrals on the right are bounded uniformly in $j$, since coordinate rescaling reduces the contribution inside the ball to a constant times

\[ \int _0^1\exp \bigl (sS(1-\log R^2)^\beta \bigr )R^{2n-1}\, \mathrm dR. \]

With $x=-\log R^2$, its exponent is $-nx+sS(1+x)^\beta $, which is at most $-nx/2$ for large $x$. Integrating the Jensen inequality and using monotone convergence proves $\mathrm e^{-su}\in L^1$. The same calculation with $\beta '$ proves the assertion for $w$. Every holomorphic germ is bounded on a smaller coordinate neighborhood, so definition 1.12.3 gives the stated trivial ideals. $\square$

Proposition 16.6.12. For the potentials of theorem 16.6.11 and every $F\in C(X,\mathbb R)$,

\[ P_{h,F}[u]=P_{h,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}=P_ h(F). \]

In particular, $P_ h[u]=P_ h[u]_{\mathcal I}=0$.

Proof. Put $Q=P_ h(F)$. The constant $\min _ XF$ is a competitor. The envelope construction of section 1.19, which preserves the exact lower current bound for the nonclosed form $h$, gives $Q\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and

\[ \min _ XF\leq Q\leq F\leq \max _ XF. \]

Since $Q$ is bounded, all its multiplier ideals are trivial. Thus $Q\sim _{\mathcal I}u$, making $Q$ itself a competitor for the ideal envelope below $F$. Every ideal competitor is an unrestricted competitor for $P_ h(F)$, so $P_{h,F}[u]_{\mathcal I}=Q$.

For $0<t<1$ set

\[ A_ t=(1-t)Q+tw-t\| F\| _\infty . \]

Convexity gives $A_ t\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$, and $w\leq 0$ implies $A_ t\leq (1-t)F-t\| F\| _\infty \leq F$. Boundedness of $Q$ and (16.10) with $b=1/t$ give $A_ t\preceq u$. Hence the ordinary envelope $O=P_{h,F}[u]$ has a competitor and satisfies $A_ t\leq O\leq Q$. Its regularized supremum is $h$-plurisubharmonic by the same envelope construction. Where $w$ is finite, $A_ t\to Q$ as $t\downarrow 0$. Thus $O=Q$ almost everywhere, and lemma 1.3.3 gives equality everywhere. For $F=0$, zero is an unrestricted competitor, so $P_ h(0)=0$. $\square$

For a dense prescribed set, the ordinary type of $u$ differs from the bounded type, although their envelopes agree below every continuous obstacle. The construction applies to every positive Hermitian background, including one with unbounded upper mass or zero lower mass.
