It does not decide whether equality below zero alone implies all-obstacle equality for an arbitrary potential and an admissible smooth background.

16.6.6 Relative divisors and fiber obstacles

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

To test equality of the envelopes below all obstacles beyond curves, we need potentials whose envelopes can be computed. Consider a holomorphic submersion with compact connected fibers, and a potential that is the sum of a logarithmic potential $\Psi $ with fixed weights along some divisors and the pullback of a potential $w$ on the base. Every competitor has at least the poles of $\Psi $, and removing them leaves a current pulled back from the base. Since the fibers are compact and connected, the remaining potential descends to the base, and minimizing the obstacle over the fibers reduces both envelopes to envelopes on the base. Over a fiber contained in the divisor the induced obstacle is infinite, but normalized compactness still bounds the competitors. Because the remaining current comes from a base of smaller dimension, all these potentials have zero top-degree mass. The reduction gives criteria on the base for membership in relative envelope classes and for equality of the envelopes below all obstacles. The divisors may be singular or ramified over the base; only the explicit product formula for the multiplier ideals requires relative normal crossings.

Let $p:M\to Y$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion between connected compact complex manifolds, with connected fibers, and put $d=\dim Y$ and $n=\dim M$, where $1\leq d<n$. Let $H_1,\ldots ,H_ s$ be a nonempty finite family of distinct prime divisors on $M$ and put $H=\bigcup _ jH_ j$. For positive real weights $\lambda _ j$, choose smooth Hermitian metrics $h_ j$ on $\mathcal O_ M(H_ j)$, with canonical sections $s_ j$ and Chern curvatures $\kappa _ j$. Set

\[ A=\sum _{j=1}^ s\lambda _ jH_ j,\qquad \Psi =\sum _{j=1}^ s\lambda _ j\log |s_ j|_{h_ j}^2,\qquad \kappa _ A=\sum _{j=1}^ s\lambda _ j\kappa _ j. \]

Thus $\kappa _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi =[A]=\sum _ j\lambda _ j[H_ j]$. For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ on $Y$ and $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$, put

\[ \Theta =p^*\beta +\kappa _ A,\qquad u=\Psi +w\circ p. \]

The form $\beta $ need not be closed or positive. Envelopes below continuous real obstacles are defined by the same competitor families as for smooth obstacles. Let

\[ Z=\{ y\in Y:p^{-1}(y)\subset H\} \]

be the set of points over which the whole fiber lies in $H$.

Proposition 16.6.13. For every $F\in C(M,\mathbb R)$, the function

\[ \widehat F(y)=\inf _{x\in p^{-1}(y)\setminus H}\{ F(x)-\Psi (x)\} , \qquad \inf \varnothing =+\infty , \]

is continuous in the extended real sense, finite on $Y\setminus Z$ and equal to $+\infty $ on $Z$. Defining the base envelopes below this extended-real obstacle by the usual competitor families, one has

16.11
\begin{equation} \begin{split} P_{\Theta ,F}[u]& =\Psi +P_{\beta ,\widehat F}[w]\circ p,\\ P_{\Theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} & =\Psi +P_{\beta ,\widehat F}[w]_{\mathcal I}\circ p. \end{split} \end{equation}

Every representative of the ordinary type of $u$ or of $P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ has zero extended mass. Consequently both singularity volumes of these types are zero.

If the ordinary and ideal envelopes of $w$ agree below every smooth obstacle on $Y$, then those of $u$ agree below every smooth obstacle on $M$.

Proof. We first study the induced obstacle and bound the base competitors, then describe all competitors upstairs by divisorial removal and descent, and finally prove the mass assertion.

Step 1: the induced obstacle. Since $p$ is open, $Y\setminus Z=p(M\setminus H)$ is open and nonempty. The function $F-\Psi $ is bounded below by $\min _ MF-\sup _ M\Psi $ and tends to $+\infty $ near $H$, because $\Psi \to -\infty $ there. On every fiber that meets $M\setminus H$, its infimum is therefore attained outside $H$. A local holomorphic section through a minimizer gives upper semicontinuity of $\widehat F$. Conversely, let $y_ j\to y$ with bounded fiber minima. By compactness, a subsequence of the minimizers converges, and its limit lies outside $H$ because $F-\Psi $ remains bounded along it; this gives lower semicontinuity. If $y\in Z$, the same limit would lie in $H$, a contradiction. Thus $\widehat F\to +\infty $ at $Z$, and $\widehat F$ is continuous in the extended sense.

Step 2: a uniform bound for the base competitors. Choose a relatively compact nonempty ball $U$ in the base and a holomorphic section $s$ of $p$ on a neighborhood of $\overline U$ with image outside $H$. There is a finite $C_ U$ with $\widehat F\leq F\circ s-\Psi \circ s\leq C_ U$ on $U$. Fix a smooth probability volume form $\mathrm dV$ on $Y$. For every $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ below $\widehat F$, normalized compactness in theorem 1.4.2 gives

\[ -C_\beta \leq \int _ U(b-\sup _ Yb)\, \mathrm dV \leq (C_ U-\sup _ Yb)\int _ U\mathrm dV. \]

Hence every such $b$ satisfies $\sup _ Yb\leq K_ F=C_ U+C_\beta /\int _ U\mathrm dV$. A sufficiently low translate of $w$ belongs to both base competitor families. Their regularized suprema are therefore $\beta $-plurisubharmonic functions, not identically $-\infty $, and by the extended continuity of $\widehat F$ they still lie below $\widehat F$.

Step 3: the competitors upstairs. The pullback of $w$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, so its generic Lelong numbers are nonnegative, and $\nu (u,H_ j)=\lambda _ j+\nu (w\circ p,H_ j)\geq \lambda _ j$. Let $v$ be an ordinary competitor for $u$, or an ideal competitor with $v\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. By the ordinary order or by theorem 1.16.3, its generic Lelong number along $H_ j$ is at least $\lambda _ j$. Divisorial removal in lemma 1.17.1 gives

\[ \Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v-[A]\geq 0. \]

Hence $v-\Psi $ has a quasi-plurisubharmonic representative $a$ satisfying $p^*\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\geq 0$. Descent along the compact connected fibers, proposition 1.22.3, gives $a=b\circ p$ for a unique $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$, and $v=\Psi +b\circ p$ for the canonical representatives. Conversely, every potential of this form is $\Theta $-plurisubharmonic.

For such $v$, one has $v\preceq u$ if and only if $b\preceq w$. Indeed, canceling $\Psi $ outside $H$ gives the base inequality almost everywhere, by Fubini's theorem in product charts, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3; the reverse implication follows by pullback and addition. Similarly, $v\sim _{\mathcal I}u$ if and only if $b\sim _{\mathcal I}w$: lemma 1.16.4 permits both addition and cancellation of $\Psi $, the pullback formula in lemma 1.16.6 then gives one direction, and restriction of the extended ideals along any local section gives the other. This comparison of ideals does not need a section outside $H$.

If the divisors have relative simple normal crossings, the ideals have an explicit form. Here this means that near every point there are product coordinates $(y,z)$ with $p(y,z)=y$ in which the components through the point are distinct coordinate hyperplanes in the fiber variables. Then

16.14
\begin{equation} \mathcal I\bigl (t(\Psi +b\circ p)\bigr ) =\mathcal O_ M\! \left(-\sum _ j\lfloor t\lambda _ j\rfloor H_ j\right) \bigl (\mathcal I(tb)\mathcal O_ M\bigr ) \qquad (t>0). \end{equation}

To see this, work in such coordinates. Up to a smooth correction, $\Psi $ is the sum of the weights $\lambda _ j\log |z_ j|^2$ over the components through the point. Since $b$ is locally bounded above, integrability forces vanishing to order at least $\lfloor t\lambda _ j\rfloor $ along each hyperplane. After this product of coordinate powers is factored out, the remaining fiber weight is

\[ \prod _ j|z_ j|^{-2(t\lambda _ j-\lfloor t\lambda _ j\rfloor )}. \]

It is integrable and bounded below by a positive constant on a small polydisk. Integrability of the quotient against this weight therefore implies integrability against the pullback of $\mathrm e^{-tb}$ alone, and lemma 1.16.6 puts the quotient in the extension of $\mathcal I(tb)$. Conversely, local generators of that ideal are weighted-integrable against the integrable product weight, by Fubini's theorem. Outside $H$ the formula is the same pullback lemma. For one smooth divisor of weight one transverse to the fibers, the first factor is the ideal sheaf of the divisor raised to the power $\lfloor t\rfloor $.

Step 4: the envelope identities. The bound $v\leq F$ is equivalent to $b\leq \widehat F$, with no constraint over $Z$. The competitor families upstairs and on the base therefore correspond to each other, which gives (16.11) outside $H$, since regularization commutes with pullback in product coordinates there. On $H$ both sides equal $-\infty $: every competitor is at most $\Psi +K_ F$, by the bound $b\leq K_ F$, and this upper semicontinuous function equals $-\infty $ on $H$, so regularization does not change the values there.

Step 5: equality below all obstacles. Suppose that the two base envelopes agree below every smooth obstacle. Uniform obstacle comparison extends this equality to continuous real obstacles. The functions $G_ j=\min (\widehat F,j)$ are continuous and real-valued. Every base competitor below $\widehat F$ has finite supremum and hence lies below $G_ j$ for every sufficiently large integer $j$. Each full competitor family is thus the union of its subfamilies below the $G_ j$, and equality for all $G_ j$ gives equality below $\widehat F$ after taking regularized suprema. By the reduction, the envelopes upstairs agree below every smooth obstacle.

Step 6: zero mass. Take any $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ and write $a=\Psi +b\circ p$. On a relatively compact chart outside $H$, the functions $b_ j=\max (b,-j)$ are locally bounded and quasi-plurisubharmonic, and there

\[ \Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Psi +b_ j\circ p)=p^*(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ j). \]

Since $n>d$, the $n$th bounded product of this current is zero by lemma 1.7.3. This identity does not require $b_ j$ to be $\beta $-plurisubharmonic: proposition 1.7.1 defines the products for locally bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic potentials. By plurifine locality, this zero product agrees with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\Theta (a)$ on $\{ b\circ p>-j\} $ in the chart. These sets cover the locus where $b\circ p$ is finite, while the remaining pole set and $H$ receive no extended mass. Thus $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\Theta (a)=0$.

By divisorial removal and descent, every representative ordinarily equivalent to $u$ has this form. By the identity already proved, the ideal model is also of this form, with base potential $P_{\beta ,\widehat0}[w]_{\mathcal I}$, where $\widehat0$ is the induced obstacle for $F=0$. The same removal and descent apply to every representative of its ordinary type. All these masses are zero, which proves both volume assertions. $\square$

Proposition 16.6.14. For $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ put $v=\Psi +\varphi \circ p$. Then

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,v) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad w\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\beta ,\varphi ). \]

In particular, for $q_ M=P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ and $q_ Y=P_\beta [w]_{\mathcal I}$,

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,q_ M) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad w\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\beta ,q_ Y). \]

Every ordinary representative of $u$ or $q_ M$ has zero extended mass, so both singularity volumes of these types vanish.

Proof. The ordinary order $u\preceq v$ is equivalent to $w\preceq \varphi $. One implication follows by addition and pullback. Conversely, cancel $\Psi $ outside $H$, which has smooth volume zero; Fubini's theorem in product charts gives the base inequality almost everywhere, and lemma 1.3.3 makes it hold everywhere. Membership in the relative envelope class requires, for every $a\in (0,1)$, a stretching competitor $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(M,\Theta )$ with

\[ av+(1-a)h\leq u. \]

A base competitor $c\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ with $a\varphi +(1-a)c\leq w$ gives such an $h$, namely $h=\Psi +c\circ p$.

Conversely, put $\rho _ j=\nu (w\circ p,H_ j)$ and $\sigma _ j=\nu (\varphi \circ p,H_ j)$. The ordinary order $w\preceq \varphi $ gives $\rho _ j\geq \sigma _ j\geq 0$. Comparing generic Lelong numbers along $H_ j$ in the inequality above gives

\[ a(\lambda _ j+\sigma _ j)+(1-a)\nu (h,H_ j) \geq \lambda _ j+\rho _ j. \]

Consequently $\nu (h,H_ j)\geq \lambda _ j+(\rho _ j-a\sigma _ j)/(1-a)\geq \lambda _ j$. Divisorial removal and descent along the fibers give $h=\Psi +c\circ p$ with $c\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$. Canceling $\Psi $ outside $H$ and the pole sets, and applying Fubini's theorem, we obtain $a\varphi +(1-a)c\leq w$ almost everywhere on the base, and hence on all of $Y$ by lemma 1.3.3. This proves the first equivalence.

For the ideal reference, theorem 16.2.2 gives $q_ M\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. The generic divisorial coefficients of $q_ M$ are therefore at least $\lambda _ j$, and removal and descent give

\[ q_ M=\Psi +b\circ p,\qquad b\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta ). \]

Cancellation in lemma 1.16.4 gives $b\circ p\sim _{\mathcal I}w\circ p$. By lemma 1.16.6, these ideals are extensions of the base ideals, so restriction along any local section gives $b\sim _{\mathcal I}w$, even over a fiber contained in $H$.

Put $C=\max \{ 0,\sup _ M\Psi \} $ and $K=\max \{ 0,\sup _ Yb\} $. The potential $\Psi +q_ Y\circ p-C$ is nonpositive and multiplier-ideal equivalent to $u$, so it is a competitor for $q_ M$. Likewise, $b-K$ is a competitor for $q_ Y$. Canceling $\Psi $ in the first inequality almost everywhere and extending as above gives

\[ q_ Y-C\leq b\leq q_ Y+K. \]

Thus $b\sim q_ Y$. The first equivalence with reference $b$, together with the invariance of the class under ordinarily equivalent references in proposition 13.4.2, proves the second equivalence. This argument works with the ideal model itself and does not require the induced fiber obstacle to be finite. If $w$ belongs to the base relative class, proposition 16.6.8 gives equality of the two envelopes upstairs below every smooth obstacle.

For any ordinary representative of $u$ or $q_ M$, removal and descent give the form $\Psi +c\circ p$ with $c\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$. Outside $H$, replace $c$ locally by $\max (c,-k)$ and keep $\Psi $. The resulting bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic potentials have currents pulled back from the $d$-dimensional base, so their $n$th products vanish by lemma 1.7.3, since $n>d$. By plurifine locality, this product agrees with the original extended measure on $\{ c\circ p>-k\} $. These sets cover the locus where $c\circ p$ is finite, and the pole sets and $H$ receive no mass. This proves the mass and volume assertions. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.15. The ordinary and ideal envelopes of $u$ agree below every smooth obstacle on $M$ if and only if those of $w$ agree below every smooth obstacle on $Y$.

Put $q=P_\beta [w]_{\mathcal I}$. If $w\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\beta ,q)$, then

\[ P_{\Theta ,F}[u]=P_{\Theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} \quad \text{for every smooth real }F. \]

If $Y$ is a curve, write

\[ T_ w=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w,\qquad \mu _ w=\mathbf1_{\{ w=-\infty \} }T_ w,\qquad D_ w=\sum _{y\in Y}\nu (w,y)\delta _ y \]

for the curvature measure of $w$, its polar part and its atomic part. Then equality of the two envelopes of $u$ below zero, the equality $\mu _ w=D_ w$, and equality of the two envelopes of $u$ below every smooth obstacle are equivalent. They are also equivalent to

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}). \]

These equivalences also hold when $\int _ Y\beta =0$.

Proof. The forward implication is part of proposition 16.6.13. For the converse, we approximate a smooth obstacle on the base by the induced obstacles of continuous obstacles upstairs. Suppose that the envelopes upstairs agree below every smooth obstacle. Uniform obstacle comparison extends this equality to continuous real obstacles. Fix a smooth real function $B$ on $Y$ and put $G=\widehat0$. The continuous obstacles

\[ F_ j=B\circ p+\max (\Psi ,-j)\qquad (j\geq 1) \]

have induced base obstacles

\[ G_ j=B+\max (G-j,0), \]

with value $+\infty $ on $Z$. By the reduction and the assumed equality,

\[ q_ j=P_{\beta ,G_ j}[w]_{\mathcal I}=P_{\beta ,G_ j}[w]. \]

Write $q_ B=P_{\beta ,B}[w]_{\mathcal I}$. Then $q_ B\leq q_ j\leq q_1$, and the $q_ j$ decrease to a $\beta $-plurisubharmonic potential $r$, not identically $-\infty $, with convergence in $L^1$; indeed, their negative parts are bounded by that of $q_ B$, and corollary 1.4.3 applies. The set $Z$ has smooth volume zero: in each product chart of the submersion, the preimage of $Z$ lies in $H$, which has smooth volume zero, and Fubini's theorem gives the assertion on the base. Off $Z$, the bounds $q_ j\leq G_ j\downarrow B$ give $r\leq B$, and almost-everywhere order in lemma 1.3.3 extends the inequality to all of $Y$.

Each $q_ j$ is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $w$. To see this without assuming $G_ j$ finite, put $Q_0=P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ and $Q_ j=P_{\Theta ,F_ j}[u]_{\mathcal I}$. Translation of the competitor families gives

\[ Q_0+\min _ MF_ j\leq Q_ j\leq Q_0+\max _ MF_ j. \]

Thus $Q_ j\sim Q_0\sim _{\mathcal I}u$ by theorem 16.2.2. Since $Q_ j=\Psi +q_ j\circ p$, cancellation and reflection of multiplier ideals under submersions, lemma 1.16.4, lemma 1.16.6, give $q_ j\sim _{\mathcal I}w$. The same bounded comparison with $P_\beta [w]_{\mathcal I}$ gives $q_ B\sim _{\mathcal I}w$.

Let $\pi $ be any smooth local modification of a coordinate neighborhood in the base. The bounds $q_ B\circ \pi \leq q_ j\circ \pi \leq q_1\circ \pi $ give decreasing local $L^1$ convergence to $r\circ \pi $. At every point $x$ of this modification, lemma 1.17.6 and the ordinary order give

\[ \nu (w\circ \pi ,x) \leq \nu (r\circ \pi ,x) \leq \nu (q_ B\circ \pi ,x)=\nu (w\circ \pi ,x). \]

All these Lelong numbers are therefore equal, and theorem 1.16.3 gives $r\sim _{\mathcal I}w$. Together with $r\leq B$, this makes $r$ a competitor for $q_ B$. Hence $r=q_ B$.

Put $\epsilon _ j=\max \{ 0,\sup _ Y(q_ j-B)\} $. The moving-point estimate in lemma 1.4.1 gives $\epsilon _ j\to 0$: otherwise a subsequence of maximizers of $q_ j-B$ converges to some $y$, where that estimate contradicts $q_ B(y)\leq B(y)$. Every ordinary competitor $b$ below $G_ j$ satisfies $b\leq q_ j$, so $b-\epsilon _ j\leq B$ and $b-\epsilon _ j\preceq w$. Taking regularized suprema yields

\[ q_ j-\epsilon _ j\leq P_{\beta ,B}[w]. \]

Since $q_ B\leq q_ j$, letting $j\to \infty $ proves $q_ B\leq P_{\beta ,B}[w]$. The reverse inequality always holds, so the two envelopes agree below $B$. Since $B$ was arbitrary, this proves the converse.

Under the relative-class hypothesis, proposition 16.6.8 gives equality of the two base envelopes below every smooth obstacle, and the equivalence just proved gives the stated equality upstairs.

In the curve case, put $G=\widehat0$ and $L=-\sup _ M\Psi $. The uniform bound in Step 2 of the proof of proposition 16.6.13 gives a finite $K$ with $\sup _ Yb\leq K$ for every $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ below $G$. Since $G\geq L$, translation of the two competitor families gives

\[ \begin{split} P_\beta [w]+L& \leq P_{\beta ,G}[w] \leq P_\beta [w]+K,\\ q+L& \leq P_{\beta ,G}[w]_{\mathcal I} \leq q+K. \end{split} \]

By proposition 30.13.1, corollary 1.6.8, the polar curvatures of these two base envelopes are $\mu _ w$ and $D_ w$, respectively. Thus equality of the envelopes upstairs below zero forces $\mu _ w=D_ w$. Conversely, under this equality of measures, corollary 30.13.2 gives equality below every smooth obstacle on the base. As in Step 5 of the proof of proposition 16.6.13, this extends to every induced obstacle $\widehat F$, and the reduction gives equality upstairs below every smooth obstacle. Taking $F=0$ proves the remaining implication. The same curve criterion identifies the equality of measures with membership of $w$ in the base relative envelope class of $q$, and the equivalence for ideal references in proposition 16.6.14 gives the last assertion upstairs. $\square$

The normal-crossing hypothesis is needed only for the explicit formula (16.14). The reduction of the envelopes holds without it.

Example 16.6.16. On $M=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, let $p,r$ be the projections and let $\sigma $ be the probability Fubini–Study form. With homogeneous coordinates $[y_0:y_1]$ and $[z_0:z_1]$, the divisor

\[ H=\{ y_0z_0^2+y_1z_1^2=0\} \]

is smooth, meets every fiber properly and ramifies over the first factor. Give $\mathcal O_ M(H)=\mathcal O_ M(1,2)$ its product metric, with logarithmic divisor potential $\Psi $ and curvature $\kappa _ H=p^*\sigma +2r^*\sigma $. Put $\Theta =2p^*\sigma +2r^*\sigma $ and choose $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^1,\sigma )$ with $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w=\delta _{[0:1]}$. Both obstacle envelopes of $u=\Psi +w\circ p$ reduce to the base, but the product formula for its multiplier ideals fails at a ramification point.

Proof. For each $[z_0:z_1]$ there is a unique point $[y_0:y_1]=[-z_1^2:z_0^2]$ on $H$, so $H$ is a smooth graph over the second factor, and its projection to the base has degree two. In particular no fiber of $p$ lies in $H$. Since $\Theta =p^*\sigma +\kappa _ H$, proposition 16.6.13 gives both envelope identities.

Near $([0:1],[1:0])$, use $y=y_0/y_1$ and $z=z_1/z_0$. Up to smooth additions,

\[ \Psi =\log |y+z^2|^2,\qquad w=\log |y|^2, \qquad u=\log |y(y+z^2)|^2. \]

At $t=3/4$, each of the two weights separately has trivial multiplier ideal, so the right side of (16.14) would be $\mathcal O_ M$. However, $1\notin \mathcal I(3u/4)$ at this point. Indeed, consider the region where $y=cz^2$ with $|c-1|<1/4$ and $0<|z|<\epsilon $; for fixed $z$, it has area comparable to $|z|^4$ in the $y$ variable. The weighted integral of $1$ is therefore bounded below by a positive constant times

\[ \int _{0<|z|<\epsilon }|z|^{-6}|z|^4\, \mathrm d\lambda _ z =2\pi \int _0^\epsilon \frac{\mathrm dr}{r}=+\infty , \]

where $\mathrm d\lambda _ z$ is planar area. Thus the multiplier ideal is proper. The envelope identities follow instead from the behavior of multiplier-ideal equivalence under addition, and do not require the product formula. $\square$

If the divisor contains an entire fiber, the induced obstacle is infinite over that fiber. The next example still admits an explicit model for the zero obstacle.

Example 16.6.17. Let $M=\mathbb P^2\times \mathbb P^1$, with projections $p,r$, and let $\sigma _ j$ be the standard Fubini–Study form on $\mathbb P^ j$. In the positive closed background

\[ \Theta =2p^*\sigma _2+r^*\sigma _1, \]

there is a logarithmic potential $\Psi $ of a smooth prime divisor containing the fiber over $E=[0:0:1]$. Its fiber infimum for the zero obstacle is finite and continuous off $E$ and tends to $+\infty $ at $E$. Nevertheless,

\[ P_\Theta [\Psi ]=P_\Theta [\Psi ]_{\mathcal I}=\Psi +q\circ p, \]

where $q\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^2,\sigma _2)$ is bounded, $0\leq q\leq \log 4$ and $q(E)=\log 4$. Every ordinary representative of this model has zero extended mass, so both singularity volumes vanish. The two envelopes also agree below every smooth obstacle.

Proof. Use homogeneous coordinates $[y_0:y_1:y_2]$ and $[z_0:z_1]$, and take

\[ H=\{ y_0z_0+y_1z_1=0\} ,\qquad \Psi =\log \frac{|y_0z_0+y_1z_1|^2}{(|y_0|^2+|y_1|^2+|y_2|^2)(|z_0|^2+|z_1|^2)}. \]

Outside $E$, the divisor is the graph $[z_0:z_1]=[-y_1:y_0]$. Its closure contains the whole fiber over $E$, so $H$ is prime. It is smooth outside that fiber by the graph description, and smooth along it because the derivatives in the $y_0$ and $y_1$ directions are $z_0$ and $z_1$, which cannot both vanish. Its product metric has curvature $p^*\sigma _2+r^*\sigma _1$, hence

\[ \Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi =p^*\sigma _2+[H]. \]

Writing $S=|y_0|^2+|y_1|^2$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives the fiber obstacle

\[ G(y)=-\max _{z\in \mathbb P^1}\Psi (y,z) =\log \frac{S+|y_2|^2}{S},\qquad G(E)=+\infty . \]

It has no finite continuous extension at $E$.

We claim that the largest $\sigma _2$-plurisubharmonic potential below $G$ is

\[ q(y)=\begin{cases} \displaystyle \log \frac{4|y_2|^2}{S+|y_2|^2},& S\leq |y_2|^2,\\[6pt] \displaystyle \log \frac{S+|y_2|^2}{S},& S\geq |y_2|^2. \end{cases} \]

To see that $q$ is $\sigma _2$-plurisubharmonic, work on $y_2\ne 0$ with $x=(y_0/y_2,y_1/y_2)$ and $t=\log |x|^2$. Adding the Fubini–Study potential $L(x)=\log (1+|x|^2)$ gives

\[ q+L=\begin{cases} \log 4,& t\leq 0,\\ 2\log (1+\mathrm e^ t)-t,& t\geq 0. \end{cases} \]

This function and its first derivative are continuous at $t=0$. For $t>0$, its first two derivatives are $(\mathrm e^ t-1)/(\mathrm e^ t+1)\geq 0$ and $2\mathrm e^ t/(1+\mathrm e^ t)^2>0$. It is therefore a convex nondecreasing function of $t$, hence plurisubharmonic in $x$, across the unit sphere as well. The expression is constant near $x=0$, and $q=G$ is smooth near $y_2=0$, so $q$ is globally $\sigma _2$-plurisubharmonic and bounded.

If $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^2,\sigma _2)$ lies below $G$, then $b+L\leq \log 4$ on $|x|=1$, and the maximum principle on the unit ball gives $b\leq q$ inside it. Outside the ball, the obstacle already gives $b\leq G=q$. For $0<R=|x|^2\leq 1$, the inequality $q\leq G$ reads $4R\leq (1+R)^2$, and at $E$ it is automatic. The formula for $q$ also gives the asserted bounds, which proves the claim.

For every ordinary or ideal competitor $v$ for $\Psi $, the ordinary order or theorem 1.16.3 gives $\nu (v,H)\geq 1$. Divisorial removal and descent along the fibers, lemma 1.17.1, proposition 1.22.3, give $v=\Psi +b\circ p$ with $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^2,\sigma _2)$. The bound $v\leq 0$ gives $b\leq G$ off $E$, hence everywhere with the extended value at $E$. Thus $v\leq \Psi +q\circ p$. Conversely, this upper bound is a competitor for both envelopes, since it is nonpositive and differs boundedly from $\Psi $. This proves the envelope identities directly.

On $M\setminus H$, bounded local truncations of $b$ have products pulled back from a two-dimensional base, so their third products vanish by lemma 1.7.3, and plurifine locality gives zero extended mass. Divisorial removal puts every ordinary representative of the model in this form, which proves both volume assertions. Finally, since the model is ordinarily equivalent to $\Psi $, proposition 13.4.2 gives $\Psi \in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,P_\Theta [\Psi ]_{\mathcal I})$, and proposition 16.6.8 gives equality below every smooth obstacle. $\square$

Example 16.6.18. There is a Hermitian background $\Theta $ on a compact fourfold that fails the bounded mass property and admits a potential $u$ without an analytic ordinary type such that

\[ P_\Theta [u]=P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}=\Psi , \qquad u\not\sim \Psi . \]

Both envelopes agree below every smooth obstacle, and their common model for the zero obstacle has both singularity volumes zero.

Proof. Let $(Y,\omega )$ be the Hopf threefold of theorem 8.0.1. On $\mathbb P^1$, let $\sigma $ be the probability Fubini–Study form, choose a point $D$ and a logarithmic potential $\psi $ with $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$ and $\sup \psi =0$. On $M=Y\times \mathbb P^1$, with projections $p,r$, put

\[ \Theta =p^*\omega +r^*\sigma ,\qquad H=Y\times D,\qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r. \]

Take the potential $U\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\omega ,0)$ of infinite mass from proposition 13.6.1, which satisfies $P_\omega [U]=0$. Its multiplier ideals are trivial, so also $P_\omega [U]_{\mathcal I}=0$. Set $u=\Psi +U\circ p$. Here $\widehat0=0$, so proposition 16.6.13, corollary 16.6.15 give the envelope identities, the equality below all obstacles and the vanishing of the singularity volumes. Moreover $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,\Psi )$ by proposition 16.6.14. Since $U$ has infinite extended mass, it is unbounded; restriction to a section outside $H$ therefore proves $u\not\sim \Psi $. If a potential $a$ with analytic singularities were ordinarily equivalent to $u$, proposition 16.2.7 would give $u\sim a\sim P_\Theta [a]=P_\Theta [u]=\Psi $, a contradiction.

Finally, let $V_ j$ be the smooth potentials of theorem 8.0.1. Their pullbacks are bounded and $\Theta $-plurisubharmonic, and proposition 19.9.1 gives

\[ \int _ M\mathrm{MA}_\Theta (V_ j\circ p) =4\int _ Y\mathrm{MA}_\omega (V_ j)\longrightarrow +\infty . \]

Thus $\Theta $ itself fails the bounded mass property. $\square$

The descent of potentials uses that the fibers are connected. Otherwise the residual potential can take independent values on different components of a fiber, as a finite isogeny shows.

Example 16.6.19. There is a surjective holomorphic submersion $p:M\to Y$ from a connected compact Kähler surface to an elliptic curve, with four components in every fiber, for which both identities in (16.11) fail. The pole divisor is smooth and maps onto $Y$, and the obstacle upstairs is smooth.

Proof. Let $E=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm i\mathbb Z)$, let $\beta $ be its flat probability form, and let $q:E\to E$ be multiplication by two. On $M=E\times \mathbb P^1$, denote the projections by $a,r$ and put $p=q\circ a$. Then $q$ is an unramified covering of degree four and $q^*\beta =4\beta $. The manifold $M$ is connected, whereas every fiber of $p$ is the disjoint union of four projective lines.

On $\mathbb P^1$ take the probability Fubini–Study form $\sigma $, a point $D$ and a logarithmic potential $\psi $ with $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$ and $\sup \psi =0$. Put

\[ H=E\times D,\qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r,\qquad \Theta =p^*\beta +r^*\sigma =4a^*\beta +r^*\sigma . \]

Thus $\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi =p^*\beta +[H]$, which corresponds to the base potential $w=0$. Choose $\epsilon >0$ so small that

\[ f(\zeta )=\epsilon \cos (2\pi \operatorname {Re}\zeta ) \in \mathrm{PSH}(E,4\beta ), \]

and set $F=f\circ a$. The function $f$ is well defined and smooth on $E$. Since it changes sign under translation by $1/2$, it is not constant on the fibers of $q$.

Apply proposition 16.6.13 to the projection $a$, which has connected fibers, with base background $4\beta $ and base potential zero. Its induced obstacle is $f$, since $\sup \psi =0$. The bounded potential $f$ is a competitor for both base envelopes and equals their obstacle, so

\[ P_{\Theta ,F}[\Psi ]=P_{\Theta ,F}[\Psi ]_{\mathcal I} =\Psi +f\circ a. \]

For the map $p$, the fiber obstacle of proposition 16.6.13 would instead be

\[ \widehat F(y)=\min _{q(\zeta )=y}f(\zeta ). \]

Every expression $\Psi +b\circ p$ is invariant under translation of the first factor by points of the kernel of $q$. The computed envelope is not invariant under translation by $1/2$, because $\Psi $ is unchanged and $f$ changes sign. It therefore cannot equal either right side of (16.11) for $p$. $\square$

The correct formula takes the fiber minimum over each connected component of a fiber separately. These components are the points of the finite covering of the base in lemma 1.22.2.

Corollary 16.6.20. Let $p:M\to Y$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion between connected compact complex manifolds, with $1\leq \dim Y<\dim M$; its fibers may be disconnected. Use the weighted divisor data $A,H,\Psi ,\kappa _ A$ above, let $\beta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $Y$, and let $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$. Put

\[ \Theta =p^*\beta +\kappa _ A,\qquad u=\Psi +w\circ p. \]

For the factorization $p=r\circ \pi $ of lemma 1.22.2, set $\beta '=r^*\beta $ and $w'=w\circ r$. For $F\in C(M,\mathbb R)$ define

\[ \widehat F_{\mathrm{comp}}(y') =\inf _{x\in \pi ^{-1}(y')\setminus H}\{ F(x)-\Psi (x)\} , \qquad \inf \varnothing =+\infty . \]

This obstacle is continuous in the extended real sense on $Y'$, and

\[ \begin{split} P_{\Theta ,F}[u] & =\Psi +P_{\beta ',\widehat F_{\mathrm{comp}}}[w']\circ \pi ,\\ P_{\Theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} & =\Psi +P_{\beta ',\widehat F_{\mathrm{comp}}}[w']_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi . \end{split} \]

Every representative of the ordinary type of $u$ or of $P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ has zero extended mass, and both singularity volumes of these types are zero. The ordinary and ideal envelopes of $u$ agree below every smooth obstacle on $M$ if and only if those of $w'$ agree below every smooth obstacle on $Y'$.

For $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$, put $v=\Psi +\varphi \circ p$. Then

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,v) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad w\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\beta ,\varphi ). \]

For $q_ M=P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ and $q_ Y=P_\beta [w]_{\mathcal I}$, one also has

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,q_ M) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad w\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\beta ,q_ Y). \]

If $Y$ is a curve, let $\mu _ w,D_ w$ be the polar and atomic measures of corollary 16.6.15. Then equality of the envelopes of $u$ below zero, the equality $\mu _ w=D_ w$, and equality of the envelopes of $u$ below every smooth obstacle are equivalent. They are also equivalent to $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta ,q_ M)$.

Proof. The covering map $r$ preserves the curvature inequality, so $w'\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y',\beta ')$. Moreover $\dim Y'=\dim Y<\dim M$, and

\[ \Theta =\pi ^*\beta '+\kappa _ A,\qquad u=\Psi +w'\circ \pi . \]

Applying proposition 16.6.13 to the submersion $\pi $, which has connected fibers, gives the obstacle identities and the mass assertions, and corollary 16.6.15 for the same submersion gives the equivalence for all obstacles. In example 16.6.19, this factorization has $Y'=E$, $r=q$ and $\pi =a$, so it recovers the computed envelope.

For the assertions on relative classes, apply proposition 16.6.14 to $\pi $, with reference $\varphi \circ r$ on $Y'$, and then transfer membership through $r$ by proposition 16.6.10. That proposition, applied with the zero obstacle, also gives

\[ P_{\beta '}[w']_{\mathcal I}=q_ Y\circ r. \]

Applying the same two equivalences with these ideal references proves the assertion for $q_ M$ and $q_ Y$.

In the curve case, $Y'$ is a curve. The covering is locally biholomorphic, so curvature measures and point Lelong numbers pull back without multiplicities. The pole set of $w'$ is $r^{-1}\{ w=-\infty \} $, and therefore

\[ \mu _{w'}=r^*\mu _ w,\qquad D_{w'}=r^*D_ w, \]

where the pullback of a measure is defined on each sheet through the local inverse of $r$. Such pullbacks reflect equality: over every sufficiently small ball in $Y$, the restriction of a measure is recovered from any sheet over that ball. Hence $\mu _{w'}=D_{w'}$ if and only if $\mu _ w=D_ w$. The curve case of corollary 16.6.15, applied to $\pi $, identifies the former equality with the equality of the envelopes upstairs below zero, with their equality below every smooth obstacle and with membership in the relative envelope class upstairs. This proves the last assertion. $\square$

16.6.7 Divisorial products and zero volume

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The simplest case of the fibration reduction is a product of a curve $C$ with a compact Kähler manifold $Z$, with the poles of $\Psi $ along $C\times D$ for a smooth divisor $D\subset Z$. If the divisor potential on $Z$ has supremum zero, the induced zero obstacle on $C$ is zero, so the envelopes of $\Psi +w\circ p$ are determined by those of $w$ on the curve. Projective products then give potentials without analytic ordinary type whose two envelopes agree, as well as distinct models with the same zero volumes. Since top-degree mass vanishes on all these models, volumes cannot distinguish them. Polar curvature on the curve can, and it gives a complete metric on the resulting class of product models.

Let $C$ be a compact connected curve with a smooth positive form $\eta $, and let $Z$ be a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $k\geq 1$. Suppose that $D\subset Z$ is a nonempty smooth reduced divisor, $\kappa $ is a Kähler form on $Z$, and $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Z,\kappa )$ is smooth off $D$ and satisfies

\[ \kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D],\qquad \sup _ Z\psi =0. \]

Thus, near $D$, $\psi $ is $\log |z_1|^2$ plus a smooth function, where $z_1$ is a local equation of $D$. On $M=C\times Z$, write $p:M\to C$ and $r:M\to Z$ for the projections, and put

\[ \Theta =p^*\eta +r^*\kappa ,\qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r,\qquad H=C\times D. \]

For $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\eta )$ set $u=\Psi +w\circ p$.

On the curve $(C,\eta )$, we use the description of models given in section 30.12. Recall from section 1.6.1 that the polar curvature of $q\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\eta )$ is the restriction of the curvature measure $\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q$ to the pole set $\{ q=-\infty \} $; it is a positive measure carried by a polar set, of mass at most $\int _ C\eta $. By lemma 30.12.1, every such measure is the polar curvature of exactly one ordinary model, and the ordinary envelope of any potential is the model with the same polar curvature. Ideal projection replaces the polar curvature by its atomic part, by lemma 19.5.2, proposition 30.12.6. Finally, the total variation of the difference of polar curvatures is a complete geodesic metric on ordinary models, which coincides with the upper-volume comparison $d_\eta $ of definition 30.6.1, by theorem 30.12.2, corollary 30.12.3.

Proposition 16.6.21. For $F\in C^\infty (M,\mathbb R)$, the function

\[ \widehat F(y)=\min _{z\in Z\setminus D}\{ F(y,z)-\psi (z)\} \qquad (y\in C) \]

is finite and continuous. The two envelopes satisfy

16.23
\begin{equation} \begin{split} P_{\Theta ,F}[u]& =\Psi +P_{\eta ,\widehat F}[w]\circ p,\\ P_{\Theta ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} & =\Psi +P_{\eta ,\widehat F}[w]_{\mathcal I}\circ p. \end{split} \end{equation}

Proof. Apply proposition 16.6.13 to $p:M\to C$, with $H=C\times D$, $\beta =\eta $ and $\Psi =\psi \circ r$. The local logarithmic form of $\psi $ defines a smooth metric on $\mathcal O_ M(H)$ with curvature $r^*\kappa $. Thus $\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi =p^*\eta +[H]$, and $H$ is smooth and transverse to the fibers. The induced fiber minimum is the function $\widehat F$ above. For $F=0$, the normalization $\sup _ Z\psi =0$ gives $\widehat0=0$. $\square$

Example 16.6.22. Take $C=\mathbb P^1$ and $Z=\mathbb P^ k$, $k\geq 1$, with their standard Fubini–Study forms, and let $D$ be a hyperplane. In this product setting there is a potential $u$ without an analytic ordinary type such that

\[ P_\Theta [u]=P_\Theta [u]_{\mathcal I}=\Psi , \qquad u\not\sim \Psi , \]

and the two envelopes of $u$ agree below every smooth obstacle. Their common model for the zero obstacle has zero singularity volume.

Proof. Use the unbounded curve potential $w$ constructed in the proof of proposition 16.6.4, whose two envelopes are zero. A logarithmic potential of a hyperplane on $Z$ gives $\psi $ with $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$ and supremum zero. Set $u=\Psi +w\circ p$ and apply proposition 16.6.21, corollary 16.6.15. Fixing $z\notin D$ shows that $u-\Psi =w\circ p$ is unbounded, so $u\not\sim \Psi $. If a potential $a$ with analytic singularities were ordinarily equivalent to $u$, proposition 16.2.7 would give $u\sim a\sim P_\Theta [a]=P_\Theta [u]=\Psi $, a contradiction. $\square$

Vanishing of the volumes does not force the two envelopes to agree.

Example 16.6.23. In the product setting, take $C=\mathbb P^1$ and $Z=\mathbb P^ k$, $k\geq 1$, with probability Fubini–Study forms and a hyperplane $D$. The positive closed background $\Theta $ admits normalized ordinary models $a\leq b$ with

\[ \begin{gathered} P_\Theta [a]=a,\qquad P_\Theta [a]_{\mathcal I}=P_\Theta [b]=P_\Theta [b]_{\mathcal I}=b,\\ a\sim _{\mathcal I}b,\qquad a\not\sim b,\\ \underline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (a) =\overline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (a) =\underline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (b) =\overline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (b)=0. \end{gathered} \]

Thus equal singularity volumes of a type and of its ideal model do not imply equality of the envelopes at zero volume.

Proof. Use the normalized diffuse polar curve model $w$ of example 30.12.7 on $\mathbb P^1$ with its probability Fubini–Study form. It satisfies $P_\eta [w]=w$ and $P_\eta [w]_{\mathcal I}=0$, and it is unbounded. Take the normalized hyperplane potential on $\mathbb P^ k$ and put

\[ a=\Psi +w\circ p,\qquad b=\Psi . \]

The identities for the zero obstacle in proposition 16.6.21 give all four envelope identities. Both functions have supremum zero, and $w\leq 0$ gives $a\leq b$. Ideal projection preserves the ideal type by theorem 16.2.2, so $a\sim _{\mathcal I}b$. Restriction to $\mathbb P^1\times \{ z\} $, for any $z\notin D$, gives the unbounded difference $w$, so $a\not\sim b$. Finally, proposition 16.6.13 gives zero extended mass for every ordinary representative of either model, which proves all four volume identities. $\square$

The same failure occurs in a nonclosed background once a second curve factor is added.

Example 16.6.24. In every complex dimension $n\geq 3$, there is a projective manifold with the bounded mass property that admits a smooth positive nonclosed background $\Theta $ and normalized ordinary models $a\leq b$ such that

\[ \begin{gathered} P_\Theta [a]=a,\qquad P_\Theta [a]_{\mathcal I}=P_\Theta [b]=P_\Theta [b]_{\mathcal I}=b,\\ a\sim _{\mathcal I}b,\qquad a\not\sim b,\\ \underline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (a) =\overline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (a) =\underline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (b) =\overline{\operatorname {vol}}_\Theta (b)=0. \end{gathered} \]

In particular, equality of the upper volumes of a type and of its ideal model does not force equality of the envelopes when the ideal model has zero lower volume.

Proof. Let $Y=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, with projections $\pi _1,\pi _2$, and let $\sigma $ be the probability Fubini–Study form on $\mathbb P^1$. Choose a positive nonconstant smooth function $f$ on the first factor and put

\[ \beta =\pi _1^*\sigma +(f\circ \pi _1)\pi _2^*\sigma . \]

This form is positive, and $\mathrm d\beta =\pi _1^*(\mathrm df)\wedge \pi _2^*\sigma \ne 0$. Let $w_0$ be the normalized diffuse polar model of example 30.12.7, and put $w=w_0\circ \pi _1$. Then $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ and

\[ P_\beta [w]=w,\qquad P_\beta [w]_{\mathcal I}=0. \]

Indeed, replace an ordinary competitor $v\leq 0$ by $\max (v,w)$. On every horizontal section $y\mapsto (y,z)$, where $\beta $ restricts to $\sigma $, this maximum is a $\sigma $-plurisubharmonic function, not identically $-\infty $ since it dominates $w_0$, and it is at least as singular as $w_0$. Its restriction is therefore at most $P_\sigma [w_0]=w_0$. This proves $v\leq w$ on $Y$; the reverse inequality for the envelope holds because $w$ is itself a competitor. All multiplier ideals of $w_0$ are trivial, so lemma 1.16.6 shows that the same holds for $w$. Since $\beta $ is positive, zero is a competitor for the ideal envelope of $w$, which proves the second identity.

Set $k=n-2$ and $M=Y\times \mathbb P^ k$, with projections $p,r$. On the second factor take the probability Fubini–Study form $\kappa $, a hyperplane $D$, and its normalized potential $\psi $ with $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$. Put

\[ \Theta =p^*\beta +r^*\kappa ,\qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r,\qquad a=\Psi +w\circ p,\qquad b=\Psi . \]

The form $\Theta $ is positive and not closed. A sufficiently large multiple of the closed positive product form dominates $\Theta $, so the bounded mass property follows from Stokes' theorem for that closed form and the metric independence in proposition 6.5.2.

Apply proposition 16.6.13 to $p:M\to Y$ and the divisor $Y\times D$. The induced zero obstacle is zero because $\sup \psi =0$. The two identities on the base give all four identities upstairs, and both functions have supremum zero. Ideal projection preserves the ideal type, so $a\sim _{\mathcal I}b$. Moreover $a\leq b$, while their difference restricts to the unbounded function $w$ on any section outside the divisor, so $a\not\sim b$. Since $\dim Y=2<n$, the same proposition gives zero extended mass for every ordinary representative of $a$ or $b$, which proves the volume assertions. $\square$

We return to the product $M=C\times Z$ and its fixed divisor potential $\Psi $. All the models $\Psi +q\circ p$ have zero top-degree volumes, but the polar curvature of $q$ on $C$ still distinguishes them.

Definition 16.6.25. For a normalized ordinary model $q$ in background $\eta $ on $C$, put

\[ \Phi (q)=\Psi +q\circ p,\qquad \mathcal S_\Psi =\{ \Phi (q):P_\eta [q]=q\} . \]

Write $\mu _ q=\mathbf1_{\{ q=-\infty \} }(\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)$ for the polar curvature of $q$, and define

16.26
\begin{equation} \rho _\Psi (\Phi (q_1),\Phi (q_2)) =\| \mu _{q_1}-\mu _{q_2}\| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \end{equation}

Proposition 16.6.26. The set $\mathcal S_\Psi $ consists of normalized ordinary models in background $\Theta $, and $(\mathcal S_\Psi ,\rho _\Psi )$ is a complete geodesic metric space. The upper-volume comparison $d_\Theta $ of definition 30.6.1, which is formed from the upper singularity volumes of $a$, $b$ and $\max (a,b)$, vanishes identically on this set:

\[ d_\Theta (a,b)=0\qquad (a,b\in \mathcal S_\Psi ). \]

The ideal models in $\mathcal S_\Psi $ form a closed geodesically convex subspace, equal to the $\rho _\Psi $-closure of the models with analytic singularities. Ideal projection is a $1$-Lipschitz retraction onto this subspace and selects the unique nearest ideal model. For $a=\Phi (q)$, the distance from $a$ to this subspace is the mass of the nonatomic part of $\mu _ q$. If $\rho _\Psi (a_ j,a)\to 0$, then $a_ j\to a$ in $L^1(M)$ for every smooth positive volume form.

Proof. The identities for the zero obstacle in proposition 16.6.21 give

\[ P_\Theta [\Phi (q)]=\Phi (q),\qquad P_\Theta [\Phi (q)]_{\mathcal I} =\Phi (P_\eta [q]_{\mathcal I}). \]

Both summands of $\Phi (q)$ have supremum zero on their respective factors, so $\Phi (q)$ is normalized. Restriction to any section outside $D$ shows that $\Phi $ is injective. The classification in lemma 30.12.1 and the distance formula in theorem 30.12.2 therefore make $\Phi $ an isometry from the space of curve models onto $\mathcal S_\Psi $. Completeness and the existence of geodesics follow from corollary 30.12.3.

By proposition 16.6.13, every ordinary representative of $\Phi (q)$ has zero extended mass. Moreover,

\[ \max (\Phi (q_1),\Phi (q_2)) =\Psi +\max (q_1,q_2)\circ p, \]

and the same proposition, applied to the curve potential $\max (q_1,q_2)$, gives zero upper volume for the maximum. Thus all three upper volumes in the definition of $d_\Theta $ vanish.

Under $\Phi $, ideal projection corresponds to taking the atomic part of $\mu _ q$. The assertions on closedness, convexity and nearest points therefore follow from corollary 30.12.3, proposition 30.12.6. To prove analytic closure upstairs, approximate an atomic measure by finite atomic measures with rational coefficients and mass less than $\int _ C\eta $. By lemma 19.5.2, their curve models have the corresponding logarithmic poles up to bounded remainders. Adding $\Psi $ preserves an analytic presentation: in the local product monomial, clear the denominators of the rational pole coefficients together with the unit coefficient of $D$. The resulting models in $\mathcal S_\Psi $ converge for $\rho _\Psi $. Conversely, by proposition 16.2.7, every ordinary model with analytic singularities in this locus is equal to its ideal projection, so the closure of these models lies in the closed ideal subspace.

Finally, if $\rho _\Psi (\Phi (q_ j),\Phi (q))\to 0$, then theorem 30.12.4 gives $q_ j\to q$ in $L^1(C)$. Off the pole sets, which have smooth volume zero, the difference of the two product potentials is $(q_ j-q)\circ p$. For a smooth positive volume form $\mathrm dV$ on $M$, integration along the compact fibers makes $p_*\mathrm dV$ a smooth positive density on $C$, and Fubini's theorem gives

\[ \int _ M|\Phi (q_ j)-\Phi (q)|\, \mathrm dV \leq C_{\mathrm dV}\| q_ j-q\| _{L^1(C,\eta )}\longrightarrow 0, \]

where $C_{\mathrm dV}$ bounds the density of $p_*\mathrm dV$ with respect to $\eta $. $\square$

The same complete metric exists on the corresponding product locus in the nonclosed background of example 16.6.24.

Corollary 16.6.27. Let $M=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^ k$, $k\geq 1$, with projections $h,s,r$. Let $\sigma $ and $\kappa $ be the probability Fubini–Study forms on $\mathbb P^1$ and $\mathbb P^ k$, respectively, and let $f$ be a positive nonconstant smooth real function on the first factor. Set

\[ \Theta =h^*\sigma +(f\circ h)s^*\sigma +r^*\kappa , \qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r, \]

where $\psi $ is the normalized hyperplane potential with $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$. For every normalized ordinary model $q$ on $(\mathbb P^1,\sigma )$, put $\Phi _ f(q)=\Psi +q\circ h$. Then

\[ P_\Theta [\Phi _ f(q)]=\Phi _ f(q),\qquad P_\Theta [\Phi _ f(q)]_{\mathcal I} =\Phi _ f(P_\sigma [q]_{\mathcal I}). \]

On $\mathcal T_\Psi =\{ \Phi _ f(q):P_\sigma [q]=q\} $, define

\[ \begin{gathered} \rho _{\Psi ,f}(\Phi _ f(q_1),\Phi _ f(q_2)) =\| \mu _{q_1}-\mu _{q_2}\| _{\mathrm{TV}},\\ \mu _ q=\mathbf1_{\{ q=-\infty \} }(\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q). \end{gathered} \]

All the conclusions of proposition 16.6.26 hold on this locus: completeness and geodesics, vanishing of the upper-volume comparison, a closed ideal subspace in which analytic models are dense, ideal projection as the nearest-point retraction, and $L^1$ convergence upstairs.

Proof. On $Y=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, write $\pi _1,\pi _2$ for the projections and put $\beta =\pi _1^*\sigma +(f\circ \pi _1)\pi _2^*\sigma $. The restriction argument of example 16.6.24 applies to every normalized ordinary curve model and gives

\[ P_\beta [q\circ \pi _1]=q\circ \pi _1. \]

Indeed, the maximum of an ordinary competitor with $q\circ \pi _1$ restricts on every horizontal section to a competitor for $P_\sigma [q]=q$ that is $\sigma $-plurisubharmonic and not identically $-\infty $.

Put $q_{\mathcal I}=P_\sigma [q]_{\mathcal I}$ and let $E$ be the atomic part of $\mu _ q$. The Green potential $w_ E$ of lemma 19.5.2 satisfies $q_{\mathcal I}\sim w_ E$ and $q\sim _{\mathcal I}w_ E$. Its pullback satisfies

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w_ E\circ \pi _1) =\sum _ xE(\{ x\} )[\{ x\} \times \mathbb P^1] -E(\mathbb P^1)\pi _1^*\sigma . \]

The divisor current is closed and has finite mass, and $w_ E\circ \pi _1$ is $\beta $-plurisubharmonic because $E(\mathbb P^1)\leq 1$. By corollary 16.2.8, its ordinary and ideal envelopes are equal. Multiplier ideals pull back by lemma 1.16.6, so ordinary equivalence and the ordinary identity just proved give

\[ P_\beta [q\circ \pi _1]_{\mathcal I} =P_\beta [w_ E\circ \pi _1]_{\mathcal I} =P_\beta [w_ E\circ \pi _1] =q_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi _1. \]

This includes the cases $E=0$ and $E$ with countably many atoms.

Apply proposition 16.6.13 to $M\to Y$ and the divisor $Y\times D$, whose induced zero obstacle is zero. The two identities on the base give the asserted identities upstairs. Thus $\Phi _ f$ identifies the locus isometrically with the whole space of curve models, and ideal projection again takes the atomic part. The metric, nearest-point and analytic-closure arguments of proposition 16.6.26 apply unchanged; rational atomic coefficients and the unit coefficient of the hyperplane give local analytic monomials. Each pairwise maximum still has the form $\Psi +\max (q_1,q_2)\circ h$, so the fibration proposition makes all three upper volumes zero. Finally, integration of a smooth volume form along the compact fibers of $h$ transfers $L^1$ convergence on the curve to $M$, as in that proposition. The background is positive and not closed, and, as in example 16.6.24, a dominating closed product form gives the bounded mass property. $\square$

The locus and its metric persist when the coefficient $f$ of the second curve factor is allowed to vanish.

Corollary 16.6.28. Keep $M,h,s,r,\sigma ,\kappa ,D,\Psi $ from corollary 16.6.27. For any smooth real function $f\geq 0$ on the first curve, put

\[ \Theta _ f=h^*\sigma +(f\circ h)s^*\sigma +r^*\kappa , \qquad \Phi (q)=\Psi +q\circ h. \]

For every normalized ordinary curve model $q$, one has

\[ P_{\Theta _ f}[\Phi (q)]=\Phi (q),\qquad P_{\Theta _ f}[\Phi (q)]_{\mathcal I} =\Phi (P_\sigma [q]_{\mathcal I}). \]

All the metric conclusions of corollary 16.6.27 hold on the same set $\mathcal T_\Psi $, with the same distance given by total variation of polar curvature. The identity map of this set is an isometry between any two of the backgrounds $\Theta _ f$ and commutes with ideal projection. This includes uniformly convergent families $f_ j\to 0$, whose limiting background $\Theta _0$ is not big.

Proof. On $Y=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, put $\beta _ f=\pi _1^*\sigma +(f\circ \pi _1)\pi _2^*\sigma $. This form is semipositive, and the argument with horizontal sections in corollary 16.6.27 still gives

\[ P_{\beta _ f}[q\circ \pi _1]=q\circ \pi _1. \]

Indeed, the pullback of $q$ is $\beta _ f$-plurisubharmonic, and every horizontal section has background $\sigma $, also where $f$ vanishes.

Write $q_{\mathcal I}=P_\sigma [q]_{\mathcal I}$, let $E$ be the atomic part of $\mu _ q$, and set $g=w_ E\circ \pi _1$, with the Green potential of the preceding proof. Then $g$ is $\beta _ f$-plurisubharmonic, $q\circ \pi _1\sim _{\mathcal I}g$, and $g\sim q_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi _1$. Its complex Hessian is the same countable divisor current of finite mass minus $E(\mathbb P^1)\pi _1^*\sigma $. For every ideal competitor $v\leq 0$ for $q\circ \pi _1$, lemma 1.17.4 gives $v\preceq g$. Therefore

\[ v\leq P_{\beta _ f}[g] =P_{\beta _ f}[q_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi _1] =q_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi _1. \]

The last equality uses the ordinary identity just proved, since $q_{\mathcal I}$ is an ordinary curve model. Conversely, $q_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi _1$ is a normalized $\beta _ f$-plurisubharmonic ideal competitor, because ideal projection preserves the ideal type on the curve and multiplier ideals pull back. This proves the ideal identity on the base, including the cases $E=0$ and $E$ with countably many atoms.

Proposition 16.6.13 allows this semipositive background on the base and gives both identities upstairs. Normalization follows because the factors are independent. The metric space is the same isometric copy of the whole space of curve models, and ideal projection, analytic closure, geodesics and $L^1$ convergence upstairs follow as in the preceding proof. Since the relevant currents are still pulled back from the surface $Y$, the same proposition gives zero upper volumes, so the upper-volume comparison vanishes. Both $\Phi (q)$ and its polar measure are independent of $f$, so the stated identifications and the compatibility with ideal projection hold exactly, including at the limiting background. Analytic singularities depend only on the functions, so their closure is also unchanged.

To see that $\Theta _0$ is not big, let $n=k+2$, and take the smooth closed positive $(n-1,n-1)$-form and the Hermitian form

\[ T=h^*\sigma \wedge r^*(\kappa ^ k),\qquad \Omega =h^*\sigma +s^*\sigma +r^*\kappa . \]

One has $\int _ M\Theta _0\wedge T=0$ and $\int _ M\Omega \wedge T=1$. If $\Theta _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \varepsilon \Omega $ for some $u\in \mathrm{QPSH}(M)$ and $\varepsilon >0$, pairing with $T$ and using Stokes' theorem would give $0\geq \varepsilon $. Thus $\Theta _0$ is not big, although the model locus keeps its complete metric. $\square$

16.6.8 Several curve factors

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A single curve factor produces a model space isometric to the space of models on one curve. Products of several curves give larger complete model spaces at zero volume. The envelopes of a sum of curve models are again computed coordinatewise: the supremum of a competitor over all factors but one is a potential on the remaining curve, and restriction to slices treats the other factors by induction. The sum of the polar distances of the factors is then a complete metric on the induced product models.

Let $C_1,\ldots ,C_ m$ be connected compact curves with smooth positive forms $\eta _ i$, where $m\geq 2$, and put $A_ i=\int _{C_ i}\eta _ i$. Set

\[ Y=\prod _{i=1}^ m C_ i,\qquad \beta =\sum _{i=1}^ m\pi _ i^*\eta _ i, \]

where $\pi _ i:Y\to C_ i$ are the projections. For normalized ordinary models $q_ i$ on $(C_ i,\eta _ i)$, write

\[ w=\sum _{i=1}^ m q_ i\circ \pi _ i,\qquad q_{i,\mathcal I}=P_{\eta _ i}[q_ i]_{\mathcal I}. \]

Lemma 16.6.29. The function $w$ is a normalized ordinary model on $(Y,\beta )$, and

\[ P_\beta [w]_{\mathcal I} =\sum _{i=1}^ m q_{i,\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i. \]

Proof. The function $w$ is $\beta $-plurisubharmonic and has supremum zero. We prove $P_\beta [w]=w$ by induction on the number of factors, the case of one factor being the identity $P_{\eta _1}[q_1]=q_1$. Let $v\leq 0$ be an ordinary competitor, and replace it by $\max (v,w)$. This preserves $v\preceq w$ and ensures that the restrictions of $v$ to slices are not identically $-\infty $ wherever the corresponding curve potentials are finite.

Write $Y=Y'\times C_ m$, and take the regularized partial supremum

\[ a(t)=\left(\sup _{y'\in Y'}v(y',t)\right)^*. \]

The restrictions of $v$ to the slices $\{ y'\} \times C_ m$ are $\eta _ m$-plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $. They are bounded above by zero, and at least one of them is not identically $-\infty $, since $v\geq w$. The local upper-envelope theorem from chapter 1 therefore makes $a$ an $\eta _ m$-plurisubharmonic function. The global inequality $v\leq w+C$ gives $a\leq q_ m+C$, because the other normalized potentials have supremum zero. Thus $a$ is a competitor for $P_{\eta _ m}[q_ m]$, and $a\leq P_{\eta _ m}[q_ m]=q_ m$.

For $t$ with $q_ m(t)$ finite, the function $v(\cdot ,t)-q_ m(t)$ is a potential in the product background on $Y'$, not identically $-\infty $; it is nonpositive and at least as singular as $\sum _{i<m}q_ i\circ \pi _ i$. The induction hypothesis bounds it by that sum. Hence $v\leq w$ off the poles of $q_ m$. At those poles the original bound $v\leq w+C$ gives the same inequality. Taking the envelope proves the ordinary identity.

For the ideal identity, let $E_ i$ be the atomic part of the polar curvature of $q_ i$, and take the Green potentials $g_ i$ of lemma 19.5.2. They satisfy

\[ q_ i\sim _{\mathcal I}g_ i,\qquad q_{i,\mathcal I}\sim g_ i,\qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ i=E_ i-\frac{E_ i(C_ i)}{A_ i}\eta _ i. \]

By lemma 1.16.6, lemma 1.16.4, $g=\sum _ i g_ i\circ \pi _ i$ is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $w$. Its complex Hessian is a closed countable sum of the divisors $\pi _ i^{-1}(x)$ minus the smooth form $\sum _ i E_ i(C_ i)\pi _ i^*\eta _ i/A_ i$. Each coordinate fiber has constant mass for the product metric, so the divisor part has finite mass. Moreover $g$ is $\beta $-plurisubharmonic since $E_ i(C_ i)\leq A_ i$. Therefore corollary 16.2.8 gives $P_\beta [g]_{\mathcal I}=P_\beta [g]$. Since $g$ is ordinarily equivalent to $\sum _ iq_{i,\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i$, the ordinary identity, applied to the models $q_{i,\mathcal I}$, shows that this envelope is $\sum _ iq_{i,\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i$. As $w\sim _{\mathcal I}g$, the ideal envelope of $w$ is the same, which proves the assertion. $\square$

The same coordinatewise computation applies to arbitrary curve potentials, to curve backgrounds that need not be positive, and to smooth obstacles that are sums of functions of the separate coordinates. Keep the curves $C_ i$, the product $Y$ and its projections $\pi _ i$. Let $\sigma _ i$ be smooth real $(1,1)$-forms on $C_ i$ with $\mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma _ i)\ne \varnothing $, that is, with $\int _{C_ i}\sigma _ i\geq 0$ by lemma 1.8.14, and choose $u_ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma _ i)$ and $F_ i\in C^\infty (C_ i,\mathbb R)$. Put

\[ \gamma =\sum _ i\pi _ i^*\sigma _ i,\qquad u=\sum _ i u_ i\circ \pi _ i,\qquad F=\sum _ i F_ i\circ \pi _ i. \]

Proposition 16.6.30. For the preceding data,

16.27
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} P_{\gamma ,F}[u] & =\sum _ i P_{\sigma _ i,F_ i}[u_ i]\circ \pi _ i,\\ P_{\gamma ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} & =\sum _ i P_{\sigma _ i,F_ i}[u_ i]_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

These identities also hold when some of the curve backgrounds have zero area.

Proof. Write $e_ i=P_{\sigma _ i,F_ i}[u_ i]$. Sums of ordinary competitors in the separate coordinates are ordinary competitors on the product, and their regularized supremum is $\sum _ i e_ i\circ \pi _ i$. This gives the lower bound in the first identity.

For the upper bound we use induction on the number of factors, the case of one factor being trivial. Let $v\leq F$ be an ordinary competitor, so that $v\leq u+C$ for some constant $C$. Choose a constant $K$ with $u-K\leq F$ and replace $v$ by $\max (v,u-K)$. This preserves both bounds and ensures that the restrictions of $v$ to the slices used below are not identically $-\infty $.

Write $Y=Y'\times C_ m$, and on $Y'$ put $u'=\sum _{i<m}u_ i\circ \pi _ i$ and $F'=\sum _{i<m}F_ i\circ \pi _ i$. The regularized partial supremum

\[ a(t)=\left(\sup _{y'\in Y'}\bigl (v(y',t)-F'(y')\bigr )\right)^* \]

is $\sigma _ m$-plurisubharmonic by the local upper-envelope theorem of chapter 1: the functions $t\mapsto v(y',t)-F'(y')$ have the common upper bound $F_ m$, and at least one of them is not identically $-\infty $, by the lower bound $v\geq u-K$. The bound $v\leq u+C$ gives

\[ a\leq u_ m+C+\sup _{Y'}(u'-F'),\qquad a\leq F_ m. \]

Thus $a$ is a competitor for $e_ m$, and $a\leq e_ m$. If $u_ m(t)$ is finite, so is $e_ m(t)$, since a sufficiently low translate of $u_ m$ is a competitor for $e_ m$. The function $v(\cdot ,t)-e_ m(t)$ is then a potential in the product background on $Y'$, not identically $-\infty $, lying below $F'$ and at least as singular as $u'$. By induction, $v(\cdot ,t)\leq \sum _{i<m}e_ i\circ \pi _ i+e_ m(t)$. At a pole of $u_ m$, the bound $v\leq u+C$ already gives $v=-\infty $. Every competitor is therefore bounded by $\sum _ i e_ i\circ \pi _ i$, and taking the regularized supremum proves the ordinary identity.

For the ideal identity, put $B_ i=\int _{C_ i}\sigma _ i\geq 0$ and $\widehat\eta _ i=\eta _ i/A_ i$. By lemma 1.8.14, choose smooth functions $\chi _ i$ with

\[ \sigma _ i=B_ i\widehat\eta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _ i. \]

Let $E_ i$ be the atomic part of $\sigma _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i$. If $B_ i>0$, apply lemma 19.5.2 to $u_ i+\chi _ i$ in the positive background $B_ i\widehat\eta _ i$. Subtracting $\chi _ i$ from the resulting atomic Green potential gives a $\sigma _ i$-plurisubharmonic function $g_ i$ such that

\[ \begin{gathered} u_ i\sim _{\mathcal I}g_ i,\qquad P_{\sigma _ i}[u_ i]_{\mathcal I}\sim g_ i,\\ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ i=E_ i-E_ i(C_ i)\widehat\eta _ i-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _ i; \end{gathered} \]

the second relation uses the smooth gauge bounds of proposition 16.3.1. If $B_ i=0$, lemma 1.8.14 gives $u_ i=-\chi _ i+c_ i$ for a constant $c_ i$, and $E_ i=0$; taking $g_ i=-\chi _ i$ gives the same conclusions. In either case,

\[ \sigma _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ i =E_ i+(B_ i-E_ i(C_ i))\widehat\eta _ i\geq 0. \]

Put $g=\sum _ i g_ i\circ \pi _ i$. Pullback and addition, lemma 1.16.6, lemma 1.16.4, give $g\sim _{\mathcal I}u$. The complex Hessian of $g$ is a countable sum of divisor currents supported on coordinate fibers, minus a smooth form. This divisor current has finite mass, because each coordinate fiber has constant mass for a fixed product metric and each $E_ i$ has finite mass. Thus lemma 1.17.4 applies, even when some $B_ i$ vanish. Every ideal competitor $v\leq F$ for $u$ therefore satisfies $v\preceq g$, and is bounded by $P_{\gamma ,F}[g]$.

Set $r_ i=P_{\sigma _ i,F_ i}[u_ i]_{\mathcal I}$. Invariance of the ordinary envelope under ordinary equivalence and proposition 16.6.8 give

\[ P_{\sigma _ i,F_ i}[g_ i] =P_{\sigma _ i,F_ i}[P_{\sigma _ i}[u_ i]_{\mathcal I}] =r_ i. \]

By the ordinary identity already proved, applied to $g$, the ideal envelope of $u$ is therefore at most $\sum _ i r_ i\circ \pi _ i$. Conversely, each $r_ i$ lies below $F_ i$ and is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $u_ i$, again by proposition 16.6.8. Their sum is a $\gamma $-plurisubharmonic ideal competitor for $u$ below $F$, which proves the reverse inequality. No strict positivity of the backgrounds is needed in this comparison. $\square$

The proof of (16.27) uses that the obstacle is a sum of functions of the separate coordinates; for an arbitrary smooth obstacle on $Y$, the coordinatewise bounds are not available. Equality of the two envelopes below such mixed obstacles is treated in 16.6.11.

We now return to the positive product background $\beta $ and the normalized curve models $q_ i$, and add a divisorial factor as in 16.6.7. Let $Z$ be a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $k\geq 1$, and let $D\subset Z$ be a nonempty smooth reduced divisor, with a Kähler form $\kappa $ and a normalized potential $\psi $ satisfying $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$. On $M=Y\times Z$, write $p,r$ for the projections, set $h_ i=\pi _ i\circ p$, and put

\[ \Theta =p^*\beta +r^*\kappa ,\qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r,\qquad \Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m)=\Psi +\sum _ i q_ i\circ h_ i. \]

Let $\mathcal S_\Psi ^{(m)}$ be the set of these potentials. For the polar curvatures $\mu _ i=\mathbf1_{\{ q_ i=-\infty \} }(\eta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_ i)$ and the analogous measures $\nu _ i$ of models $b_ i$, define

16.30
\begin{equation} \rho _\Psi ^{(m)}(\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m),\Phi (b_1,\ldots ,b_ m)) =\sum _ i\| \mu _ i-\nu _ i\| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \end{equation}

Proposition 16.6.31. The set $\mathcal S_\Psi ^{(m)}$ consists of normalized ordinary models and is a complete geodesic metric space for $\rho _\Psi ^{(m)}$. Its ideal models form a closed geodesically convex subspace, equal to the metric closure of its models with analytic singularities. Ideal projection is the unique nearest-point retraction onto this subspace and is $1$-Lipschitz, and the distance to the subspace is the sum of the nonatomic polar masses. Metric convergence implies $L^1(M)$ convergence for every smooth positive volume form. The upper-volume comparison $d_\Theta $ vanishes on every pair of points of this locus.

Proof. The preceding lemma and proposition 16.6.13 give

\[ \begin{gathered} P_\Theta [\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m)]=\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m),\\ P_\Theta [\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m)]_{\mathcal I} =\Phi (q_{1,\mathcal I},\ldots ,q_{m,\mathcal I}), \end{gathered} \]

since the induced zero obstacle is zero; independence of the factors gives normalization. Restricting to sections on which the other potentials are finite shows that if two product potentials are equal, then each coordinate difference is constant, and normalization makes every such constant zero. Thus $\Phi $ identifies this locus with the finite product of the spaces of curve models, equipped with the sum of their metrics.

Completeness follows coordinate by coordinate from corollary 30.12.3. Interpolating all polar measures linearly with the same parameter gives a constant-speed geodesic for the sum metric. Ideal projection takes the atomic part in each coordinate, so the assertions on closedness, convexity, unique nearest points and the Lipschitz bound follow coordinate by coordinate from corollary 30.12.3, proposition 30.12.6, and the distances add. Finite atomic approximations with rational coefficients in each coordinate have local logarithmic coefficients whose denominators, together with the unit coefficient of $D$, can be cleared in a product monomial. They give analytic models that are dense in the ideal subspace. Conversely, every ordinary model with analytic singularities is its own ideal projection by proposition 16.2.7, so the closure of these models is exactly the ideal subspace.

By theorem 30.12.4, metric convergence gives $L^1$ convergence on each curve. Off the pole sets, the difference of two product potentials is the sum of the pulled-back coordinate differences. Integrating each term along the compact fibers of $h_ i$ bounds its $L^1$ norm on $M$ by a constant times its $L^1$ norm on the curve, as in proposition 16.6.26, and the finite sum tends to zero.

Finally, since $\dim Y=m<\dim M$, proposition 16.6.13 shows that every model in this locus has zero upper singularity volume. A pairwise maximum is still $\Psi $ plus the pullback of a $\beta $-plurisubharmonic function on $Y$, so the same proposition gives zero upper volume for it. All three terms of $d_\Theta $ therefore vanish. $\square$

As in corollary 16.6.27, corollary 16.6.28, an auxiliary curve factor with a variable coefficient makes the background nonclosed, and lets it degenerate, without changing this metric locus.

Keep $Y,\beta ,\pi _ i$ and $Z,\kappa ,D,\psi $ from above. Let $C_0$ be another connected compact curve with a smooth positive form $\tau $. On $M=Y\times C_0\times Z$, write $p,s,r$ for the projections. For a smooth real function $f\geq 0$ on $Y$, put

\[ \Theta _ f=p^*\beta +(f\circ p)s^*\tau +r^*\kappa , \qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r, \]

and set $\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m)=\Psi +\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p$.

Corollary 16.6.32. For every tuple of normalized ordinary curve models, one has

\[ \begin{gathered} P_{\Theta _ f}[\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m)]=\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m),\\ P_{\Theta _ f}[\Phi (q_1,\ldots ,q_ m)]_{\mathcal I} =\Phi (q_{1,\mathcal I},\ldots ,q_{m,\mathcal I}). \end{gathered} \]

On the locus of these potentials, use the sum of polar distances (16.30). All the metric conclusions of proposition 16.6.31 hold, and the diameter is $2\sum _ i A_ i$. The locus, its metric, ideal projection and the analytic closure are independent of $f$, including at $f=0$. The background is positive and not closed when $f$ is positive and nonconstant. The background $\Theta _0$ obtained for $f=0$ is not big.

Proof. On $B=Y\times C_0$, let $\pi ,s_0$ be the projections and put $\beta _ f=\pi ^*\beta +(f\circ \pi )s_0^*\tau $. For $w=\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i$, an ordinary competitor $v\leq 0$ for $w\circ \pi $ may be replaced by its maximum with $w\circ \pi $. Its restriction to every section $Y\times \{ z\} $ is then a $\beta $-plurisubharmonic competitor for $P_\beta [w]=w$, not identically $-\infty $, by lemma 16.6.29. Thus

\[ P_{\beta _ f}[w\circ \pi ]=w\circ \pi . \]

Take the atomic Green potentials $g_ i$ from the proof of the lemma and put $g=\sum _ i g_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $. Its complex Hessian is a closed countable divisor current of finite mass minus a smooth form. It is $\beta _ f$-plurisubharmonic for every $f\geq 0$, and it is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $w\circ \pi $. Put $w_{\mathcal I}=\sum _ iq_{i,\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i$. Since $g\sim w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi $, every ideal competitor $v\leq 0$ is bounded above by

\[ P_{\beta _ f}[g] =P_{\beta _ f}[w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi ] =w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi . \]

Here lemma 1.17.4 gives $v\preceq g$, and the ordinary identity applies to $w_{\mathcal I}$. Conversely, $w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi $ is a normalized ideal competitor, because ideal projection preserves the ideal type on each curve and the ideal type is compatible with pullback and addition. This computes the ideal envelope on $B$ without requiring $\beta _ f$ to be positive.

Apply proposition 16.6.13 to $M\to B$ and the divisor $B\times D$. Its induced zero obstacle is zero, which gives the two identities upstairs and normalization. Restriction to a fixed section of the auxiliary curve identifies this locus isometrically with the finite product of the spaces of curve models. Completeness, geodesics and the nearest-point property of ideal projection therefore follow as in proposition 16.6.31, and the same rational atomic monomials give analytic density. For $f=1$, the background is positive and proposition 16.2.7 shows that every analytic model lies in the ideal subspace. Analytic singularities and the computed ideal projection are independent of $f$, so this analytic-closure assertion holds for every nonnegative coefficient. Fiber integration, now including the factor $C_0$, gives the same $L^1$ convergence upstairs. Each model and each pairwise maximum is $\Psi $ plus a pullback from $B$, whose dimension is smaller than that of $M$, so their upper volumes vanish by proposition 16.6.13; the upper-volume comparison of definition 30.6.1 therefore vanishes for every $f$.

Each polar measure $\mu _ i$ has mass at most $A_ i$, so the sum distance is at most $2\sum _ iA_ i$. By lemma 30.12.1, this bound is attained by placing mass $A_ i$ at two distinct points of each $C_ i$. Hence the diameter does not change, even at the limiting coefficient.

Finally, $\mathrm d\Theta _ f=p^*(\mathrm df)\wedge s^*\tau $ is nonzero for nonconstant $f$, and $\Theta _ f$ is strictly positive when $f>0$. To see that $\Theta _0$ is not big, put $n=m+1+k$ and use the smooth closed positive $(n-1,n-1)$-form $T$ and the Hermitian form $\Omega $ given by

\[ T=p^*\left(\bigwedge _ i\pi _ i^*\eta _ i\right)\wedge r^*(\kappa ^ k), \qquad \Omega =p^*\beta +s^*\tau +r^*\kappa . \]

Then $\int _ M\Theta _0\wedge T=0$, whereas $\int _ M\Omega \wedge T>0$. By Stokes' theorem, a lower bound $\Theta _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \varepsilon \Omega $ with $\varepsilon >0$ would contradict these identities. Thus the background $\Theta _0$ is not big. $\square$

16.6.9 Varying the curve coefficients

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The coefficient $f$ of the auxiliary curve in corollary 16.6.32 does not affect the metric on the model locus. Scaling the curve forms themselves has a different effect: a positive coefficient rescales the polar distance in its coordinate, whereas a zero coefficient removes the coordinate altogether, so the diameter depends on the remaining curve areas. We describe the resulting family of model spaces and then compare its members.

Keep the product $M=Y\times C_0\times Z$, its projections and the forms of that corollary. For $a=(a_1,\ldots ,a_ m)\in [0,\infty )^ m$ and a smooth function $f\geq 0$ on $Y$, put

\[ J(a)=\{ i:a_ i>0\} ,\qquad \beta _ a=\sum _ i a_ i\pi _ i^*\eta _ i,\qquad \Theta _{a,f}=p^*\beta _ a+(f\circ p)s^*\tau +r^*\kappa . \]

The coordinates $i\in J(a)$ are called active. For normalized ordinary models $q_ i$ in the fixed curve backgrounds $\eta _ i$, define

\[ \Phi _ a(q)=\Psi +\sum _{i\in J(a)}a_ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p, \qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r. \]

Terms with zero coefficient are omitted, also on their pole sets, so $\Phi _ a(q)$ depends only on the active coordinates. Write $\mathcal S_ a$ for this locus of potentials, and $q_{i,\mathcal I}=P_{\eta _ i}[q_ i]_{\mathcal I}$.

Lemma 16.6.33. For every $a$ and $f$ as above,

\[ P_{\Theta _{a,f}}[\Phi _ a(q)]=\Phi _ a(q),\qquad P_{\Theta _{a,f}}[\Phi _ a(q)]_{\mathcal I}=\Phi _ a(q_{\mathcal I}). \]

Both potentials have supremum zero. In particular, $\mathcal S_0=\{ \Psi \} $.

Proof. For $a_ i>0$, scaling the ordinary competitors shows that $a_ iq_ i$ is an ordinary model for $a_ i\eta _ i$. Its polar curvature is $a_ i\mu _ i$, where $\mu _ i$ is the polar curvature of $q_ i$. Taking atomic parts and applying theorem 30.13.3, we see that its ideal model is $a_ iq_{i,\mathcal I}$.

Put $w_ a=\sum _{i\in J(a)}a_ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i$. If there are at least two active factors, lemma 16.6.29 makes the corresponding sum an ordinary model on the product of the active curves, with its positive product background. For one active factor, this is the identity on the curve. The identity is preserved under pullback to $Y$, by proposition 1.22.3: every competitor in the pulled-back background descends, and so do its obstacle bound and its ordinary order, since the projection is surjective. If there are no active factors, the normalized model is zero by the maximum principle. Hence $P_{\beta _ a}[w_ a]=w_ a$ in all cases.

On $B=Y\times C_0$, use the background $\beta _{a,f}=\pi ^*\beta _ a+(f\circ \pi )s_0^*\tau $. As in corollary 16.6.32, replace an ordinary competitor by its maximum with $w_ a\circ \pi $ and restrict it to each section $Y\times \{ z\} $. This gives $P_{\beta _{a,f}}[w_ a\circ \pi ]=w_ a\circ \pi $.

For the ideal identity, take $g_ a=\sum _{i\in J(a)}a_ i g_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $, with the atomic Green potentials $g_ i$ of lemma 19.5.2. Scaling, addition and pullback preserve multiplier-ideal equivalence by lemma 1.16.4, lemma 1.16.6. The complex Hessian of $g_ a$ is a closed countable divisor current of finite mass minus a smooth form, and $g_ a$ is $\beta _{a,f}$-plurisubharmonic. It is ordinarily equivalent to $w_{a,\mathcal I}\circ \pi $, where $w_{a,\mathcal I}=\sum _{i\in J(a)}a_ iq_{i,\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i$. By lemma 1.17.4, every ideal competitor is an ordinary competitor for $g_ a$, so by the ordinary identity applied to the models $a_ iq_{i,\mathcal I}$ it is bounded by $w_{a,\mathcal I}\circ \pi $. The latter is itself an ideal competitor, so the ideal envelope equals it. When $J(a)$ is empty, both envelopes on $B$ are zero directly. Finally, proposition 16.6.13, applied to $M\to B$ and the divisor $B\times D$ with weight one, gives the identities upstairs, and independence of the factors gives normalization. $\square$

For two tuples $q,\widetilde q$, let $\mu _ i,\nu _ i$ be their polar curvature measures in the fixed backgrounds $\eta _ i$, and set

16.31
\begin{equation} \rho _ a(\Phi _ a(q),\Phi _ a(\widetilde q)) =\sum _{i\in J(a)}a_ i\| \mu _ i-\nu _ i\| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \end{equation}

On the singleton $\mathcal S_0$ this distance is zero.

Proposition 16.6.34. For every $a$, the locus $\mathcal S_ a$ is a complete geodesic metric space for $\rho _ a$, independently of $f$. Its ideal subspace is closed, geodesically convex and equal to the metric closure of its models with analytic singularities. Ideal projection is the unique nearest-point retraction onto this subspace and is $1$-Lipschitz, with distance

\[ \rho _ a\bigl (\Phi _ a(q),\Phi _ a(q_{\mathcal I})\bigr ) =\sum _{i\in J(a)}a_ i\mu _ i^{\mathrm{na}}(C_ i), \]

where $\mu _ i^{\mathrm{na}}$ is the nonatomic part of $\mu _ i$. The diameter is $2\sum _ i a_ iA_ i$, and metric convergence implies $L^1(M)$ convergence for every smooth positive volume form. The upper-volume comparison vanishes on this locus.

If $a,b$ have the same nonempty active set $J$, the correspondence $T_{ba}\Phi _ a(q)=\Phi _ b(q)$ is well defined, commutes with ideal projection and satisfies

\[ \min _{i\in J}\frac{b_ i}{a_ i}\, \rho _ a(u,v) \leq \rho _ b(T_{ba}u,T_{ba}v) \leq \max _{i\in J}\frac{b_ i}{a_ i}\, \rho _ a(u,v). \]

For a fixed smooth positive volume form on $M$, there are constants $K_ i$, independent of $a,b$ and of the normalized model tuple $q$, such that

\[ \| \Phi _ a(q)-\Phi _ b(q)\| _{L^1(M)} \leq \sum _ i K_ i|a_ i-b_ i|. \]

Consequently, as all $a_ i$ tend to zero, the diameter tends to zero and every potential in $\mathcal S_ a$ tends to $\Psi $ in $L^1(M)$, uniformly over the normalized model tuples.

Proof. Restriction to coordinate sections on which the other potentials are finite determines every active term $a_ iq_ i$ up to a constant, and normalization makes these constants zero. Thus (16.31) is well defined, and it identifies $\mathcal S_ a$ with the product of the spaces of curve models in the active coordinates, with their distances multiplied by $a_ i$. Coordinates with $a_ i=0$ do not appear, and for $J(a)=\varnothing $ the empty product is a single point.
