The completeness, geodesic and atomic-projection results of corollary 30.12.3, proposition 30.12.6 apply coordinate by coordinate, and the weighted distances add; this proves the metric assertions and the distance formula. The diameter bound is attained by choosing polar measures $A_ i\delta _{x_ i}$ and $A_ i\delta _{y_ i}$ with $x_ i\ne y_ i$ on each active curve. Fiber integration and theorem 30.12.4 give $L^1$ convergence upstairs. The preceding lemma shows that ideal projection does not depend on $f$. Since every model and every pairwise maximum is $\Psi $ plus a pullback from $B$, proposition 16.6.13 gives zero upper volumes, so the upper-volume comparison vanishes.

For analytic density, approximate the atomic part $a_ i\mu _ i^{\mathrm{at}}$ of each scaled polar measure, where $\mu _ i^{\mathrm{at}}=\mu _ i-\mu _ i^{\mathrm{na}}$, by finite atomic measures with rational coefficients and mass at most $a_ iA_ i$. These approximations converge in total variation. Their curve models in the background $a_ i\eta _ i$ have finitely many logarithmic poles with rational coefficients, whose denominators, together with the unit coefficient of $D$, can be cleared in local product monomials. They therefore give analytic models in $\mathcal S_ a$ converging to every ideal model. Conversely, suppose that $\Phi _ a(q)$ has analytic singularities. Restrict it to a section along an active curve on which all other potentials are finite and whose $Z$-coordinate lies outside $D$. By proposition 1.13.1, the restriction, which is $a_ iq_ i$ plus a constant and not identically $-\infty $, has analytic singularities. It is an ordinary model in the positive background $a_ i\eta _ i$, so proposition 16.2.7 shows that it equals its ideal projection. The preceding lemma now shows that $\Phi _ a(q)$ is an ideal model, and closedness gives the analytic-closure assertion. The argument uses rational coefficients for the scaled poles $a_ iq_ i$ themselves. With the rational-coefficient convention of section 1.13, an irrational scaling need not preserve an analytic curve model, since it can give a pole an irrational Lelong number.

For a common active set, the normalized tuple is determined by the potential. The two bounds for $T_{ba}$ follow by comparing each positive weight $b_ i$ with $a_ i$, and atomic projection in each coordinate commutes with this correspondence.

Finally, normalized compactness, theorem 1.4.2, bounds the $L^1(C_ i)$ norms of all the $q_ i$ uniformly. Integration along the compact fibers of $\pi _ i\circ p$ gives constants $K_ i$ bounding the $L^1(M)$ norms of their pullbacks. Off the pole sets, which have smooth volume zero, the difference of the two potentials is the sum of the functions $(a_ i-b_ i)q_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p$. The triangle inequality proves the uniform estimate, also when some coefficients vanish. Taking $b=0$ gives uniform convergence to $\Psi $, and the diameter formula shows that the diameters tend to zero. $\square$

Thus letting the auxiliary coefficient vanish leaves the metric of corollary 16.6.32 unchanged, whereas letting a curve coefficient vanish removes its coordinate and decreases the diameter. These computations concern only the specified product loci, not general ordinary models in higher dimension.

Comparing bounded model spaces

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When the active sets differ, the coordinatewise correspondence $T_{ba}$ no longer defines a bijection between $\mathcal S_ a$ and $\mathcal S_ b$. We compare them as abstract metric spaces. They are bounded, but they are not compact as soon as some coefficient is positive. The Gromov–Hausdorff distance $d_{\mathrm{GH}}$ of bounded metric spaces from definition 14.18.8, together with its estimate through correspondences in lemma 14.18.9, applies without a compactness hypothesis.

Write $\mathcal I_ a\subset \mathcal S_ a$ for the ideal subspace of proposition 16.6.34, with the restricted metric $\rho _ a$.

Proposition 16.6.35. For all nonnegative coefficient vectors $a,b$, including vectors with different active sets,

16.32
\begin{equation} \left|\sum _ i(a_ i-b_ i)A_ i\right| \leq d_{\mathrm{GH}}(\mathcal S_ a,\mathcal S_ b) \leq \sum _ i|a_ i-b_ i|A_ i. \end{equation}

The same bounds hold for $d_{\mathrm{GH}}(\mathcal I_ a,\mathcal I_ b)$. If $b_ i\leq a_ i$ for every $i$, both distances equal $\sum _ i(a_ i-b_ i)A_ i$. In particular, the distance from $\mathcal S_ a$ to the singleton $\mathcal S_0=\mathcal I_0=\{ \Psi \} $ equals $\sum _ i a_ iA_ i$.

For $b\leq a$, the map $T_{ba}\Phi _ a(q)=\Phi _ b(q)$ is a surjective $1$-Lipschitz map and commutes with ideal projection. Every fiber has diameter

\[ 2\sum _{\{ i:a_ i>0,\ b_ i=0\} }a_ iA_ i. \]

If some $a_ i>0$, then $\mathcal S_ a$ and $\mathcal I_ a$ are not totally bounded, and hence not compact.

Proof. Use the correspondence

\[ R_{ab}=\{ (\Phi _ a(q),\Phi _ b(q)):q_ i \text{ are normalized ordinary curve models}\} . \]

Every potential in either locus arises from such a tuple, with arbitrary normalized models in its coordinates of zero coefficient, so both projections of $R_{ab}$ are surjective. For any two tuples, put $d_ i=\| \mu _ i-\nu _ i\| _{\mathrm{TV}}$. By (16.31), the difference of their two distances is $\sum _ i(a_ i-b_ i)d_ i$, with $0\leq d_ i\leq 2A_ i$. Thus the distortion of this correspondence satisfies $\operatorname {dis}R_{ab}\leq 2\sum _ i|a_ i-b_ i|A_ i$, and lemma 14.18.9 gives the upper bound in (16.32). The diameter formula in proposition 16.6.34 and the same lemma give the lower bound.

Restricting the correspondence to tuples of ideal curve models, we obtain a correspondence between the two ideal subspaces with the same distortion bound. The atomic measures $A_ i\delta _{x_ i}$ and $A_ i\delta _{y_ i}$ show that the ideal subspaces have the same diameters as the full spaces, so the lower bound also holds for them. When $b\leq a$, the upper and lower bounds coincide, including when $b=0$.

For the transition map, every active coordinate of $b$ is active for $a$, and uniqueness of the normalized active tuple makes the map well defined. Every $b$-tuple extends to an $a$-tuple, so the map is surjective, and $\sum _ i b_ i d_ i\leq \sum _ i a_ i d_ i$ proves the Lipschitz bound. Atomic projection in each coordinate commutes with discarding and rescaling coordinates. In a fiber, every coordinate with $b_ i>0$ is fixed, while the coordinates with $a_ i>0$ and $b_ i=0$ vary freely. The diameter of the fiber is therefore the sum above, by the calculation of the diameter on each curve.

Finally, fix an active coordinate $i$ and all other coordinates at their zero models. Let $q_ i$ have polar measure $A_ i\delta _ x$, with $x$ ranging over infinitely many distinct points of $C_ i$. The resulting potentials are ideal models, and any two of them are at distance $2a_ iA_ i$. This infinite separated set excludes total boundedness of both spaces. In particular, the comparison above is between complete bounded spaces that are not compact. $\square$

16.6.10 Arbitrary curve backgrounds

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Scaling by coefficients is a special case of changing the curve backgrounds. By proposition 16.6.30, the coordinatewise computation of the envelopes holds for arbitrary smooth curve backgrounds of nonnegative area, so the product metric depends on the areas of the curve backgrounds rather than on their positivity. A curve background of area zero has a unique normalized potential, which is smooth. Its coordinate no longer contributes to the metric, but its smooth potential remains a summand of the product potentials.

Keep $M=Y\times C_0\times Z$, the projections $p,s,r$ and $\Psi $ from corollary 16.6.32. On each $C_ i$, choose a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\sigma _ i$ with $\mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma _ i)\ne \varnothing $, and put $B_ i=\int _{C_ i}\sigma _ i\geq 0$. For a smooth function $f\geq 0$ on $Y$, set

\[ \gamma _\sigma =\sum _ i\pi _ i^*\sigma _ i,\qquad \Theta _{\sigma ,f}=p^*\gamma _\sigma +(f\circ p)s^*\tau +r^*\kappa . \]

For normalized ordinary models $q_ i$ in the curve backgrounds $\sigma _ i$, write

\[ \Phi _\sigma (q)=\Psi +\sum _ i q_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p, \qquad q_{i,\mathcal I}=P_{\sigma _ i}[q_ i]_{\mathcal I}, \]

and let $\mathcal S_\sigma $ be the locus of these potentials. All coordinates are included: when $B_ i=0$, $q_ i$ is the unique normalized potential $u_{\sigma _ i}$ of lemma 1.8.14, which is smooth.

Lemma 16.6.36. Every $\Phi _\sigma (q)$ is a normalized ordinary model, and

\[ P_{\Theta _{\sigma ,f}}[\Phi _\sigma (q)]_{\mathcal I} =\Phi _\sigma (q_{\mathcal I}). \]

The background is not big if $f=0$ or some $B_ i=0$. If $f>0$ everywhere and all $B_ i>0$, it is big. It is not closed whenever $f$ is nonconstant.

Proof. On $B=Y\times C_0$, let $\pi ,s_0$ be the projections and put

\[ \gamma _{\sigma ,f}=\pi ^*\gamma _\sigma +(f\circ \pi )s_0^*\tau , \qquad w=\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i. \]

The case of the zero obstacle in proposition 16.6.30 gives $P_{\gamma _\sigma }[w]=w$. Replace an ordinary competitor for $w\circ \pi $ by its maximum with $w\circ \pi $. Its restriction to every section $Y\times \{ z\} $ is then a competitor for $P_{\gamma _\sigma }[w]$ that is not identically $-\infty $, so

\[ P_{\gamma _{\sigma ,f}}[w\circ \pi ]=w\circ \pi . \]

Use the atomic Green representatives $g_ i$ constructed in the proof of proposition 16.6.30, which are smooth in the coordinates of zero area. Their sum $g=\sum _ i g_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $ is $\gamma _{\sigma ,f}$-plurisubharmonic and multiplier-ideal equivalent to $w\circ \pi $, and its complex Hessian is a closed countable divisor current of finite mass minus a smooth form. If $w_{\mathcal I}=\sum _ iq_{i,\mathcal I}\circ \pi _ i$, then $g\sim w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi $. By lemma 1.17.4, every ideal competitor is ordinarily dominated by $g$, hence bounded by

\[ P_{\gamma _{\sigma ,f}}[g] =P_{\gamma _{\sigma ,f}}[w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi ] =w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi . \]

Here the last equality holds because each ideal projection $q_{i,\mathcal I}$ on a curve is an ordinary model, so the argument with sections just given applies to $w_{\mathcal I}$. By pullback and addition, $w_{\mathcal I}\circ \pi $ is itself an ideal competitor, which proves the ideal identity on $B$. Now apply proposition 16.6.13 to $M\to B$ and the divisor $B\times D$ with weight one, whose induced zero obstacle is zero. The two identities upstairs and the independence of the factors give the assertion and normalization.

For the statements on bigness, put $n=m+1+k$ and $\Omega =p^*\beta +s^*\tau +r^*\kappa >0$, with the positive product form $\beta $ fixed earlier. For $f=0$, the closed positive test form

\[ T_0=p^*\left(\bigwedge _ j\pi _ j^*\eta _ j\right)\wedge r^*(\kappa ^ k) \]

satisfies $\int _ M\Theta _{\sigma ,0}\wedge T_0=0$ and $\int _ M\Omega \wedge T_0>0$. If $B_ i=0$, use instead

\[ T_ i=p^*\left(\bigwedge _{j\ne i}\pi _ j^*\eta _ j\right) \wedge s^*\tau \wedge r^*(\kappa ^ k). \]

Then $\int _ M\Theta _{\sigma ,f}\wedge T_ i=0$ for every $f$, since the only nonzero term contains the factor $\int _{C_ i}\sigma _ i=B_ i$, while $\int _ M\Omega \wedge T_ i>0$. In either case, Stokes' theorem against the closed test form shows that no current $\Theta _{\sigma ,f}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ with $u$ quasi-plurisubharmonic is bounded below by a positive multiple of $\Omega $, so the background is not big.

Finally, write $\sigma _ i=B_ i\eta _ i/A_ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _ i$ by lemma 1.8.14. The smooth potential $-\sum _ i\chi _ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p$ has strictly positive current when every $B_ i>0$ and $f>0$, which proves bigness in that case. The identity $\mathrm d\Theta _{\sigma ,f}=p^*(\mathrm df)\wedge s^*\tau $ shows that the background is not closed for nonconstant $f$. $\square$

For the polar curvatures $\mu _ i$ of $q_ i$ and $\nu _ i$ of another tuple $\widetilde q$, taken in the backgrounds $\sigma _ i$, set

16.33
\begin{equation} \rho _\sigma (\Phi _\sigma (q),\Phi _\sigma (\widetilde q)) =\sum _ i\| \mu _ i-\nu _ i\| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \end{equation}

Write $\mathcal I_\sigma \subset \mathcal S_\sigma $ for the ideal subspace and $\mu _ i^{\mathrm{na}}$ for the nonatomic part of $\mu _ i$.

Proposition 16.6.37. The metric space $(\mathcal S_\sigma ,\rho _\sigma )$ is complete and geodesic, with diameter $2\sum _ i B_ i$. The ideal subspace is closed, geodesically convex and equal to the metric closure of the models with analytic singularities. Ideal projection is the unique nearest-point retraction onto it and is $1$-Lipschitz, and the distance to the subspace is $\sum _ i\mu _ i^{\mathrm{na}}(C_ i)$. Metric convergence implies $L^1(M)$ convergence for every smooth positive volume form. These assertions are independent of $f$, and the upper-volume comparison vanishes on this locus.

Let $\sigma '_ i$ be another tuple of smooth real forms with $\mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma '_ i)\ne \varnothing $, with areas $B'_ i$. Then

16.34
\begin{equation} \left|\sum _ i(B_ i-B'_ i)\right| \leq d_{\mathrm{GH}}(\mathcal S_\sigma ,\mathcal S_{\sigma '}) \leq \sum _ i|B_ i-B'_ i|. \end{equation}

The same bounds hold for the ideal subspaces. If $B'_ i\leq B_ i$ for every $i$, both distances equal $\sum _ i(B_ i-B'_ i)$. In this case, rescaling the polar measures by the area ratios, with the unique normalized potential in each target coordinate of zero area, gives a surjective $1$-Lipschitz map that commutes with ideal projection. Its fibers have diameter $2\sum _{\{ i:B_ i>0,\ B'_ i=0\} }B_ i$.

Both $\mathcal S_\sigma $ and $\mathcal I_\sigma $ are noncompact if some $B_ i>0$. If all $B_ i=0$, they coincide with the singleton $\{ \Psi +\sum _ i u_{\sigma _ i}\circ \pi _ i\circ p\} $.

Proof. Restriction to coordinate sections on which the other potentials are finite determines each $q_ i$ up to a constant, and normalization fixes that constant. The preceding lemma and proposition 30.13.1 therefore identify the locus with the finite product of the spaces of curve models, with the sum of their polar metrics; factors of zero area are single points. Completeness, geodesics and the diameter formula follow coordinatewise. Under this identification, corollary 30.12.3, proposition 30.12.6 give closedness, geodesic convexity and the unique nearest-point retraction onto the ideal subspace, and the distances add. Fiber integration transfers $L^1$ convergence on the curves to $M$.

For analytic density, approximate each atomic polar measure by finite atomic measures with rational coefficients and mass at most $B_ i$. By the atomic Green construction and transport under smooth gauge changes, their models in background $\sigma _ i$ have the corresponding finitely many poles with rational coefficients, up to bounded remainders. Coordinates of zero area are smooth. Together with the unit coefficient of $D$, the poles in the coordinates of positive area allow the denominators to be cleared in local product monomials, which gives analytic models in the locus. They converge for the metric (16.33). Conversely, by proposition 1.13.1, a product model with analytic singularities restricts, on every coordinate curve section on which the other potentials are finite and whose $Z$-coordinate lies outside $D$, to a function with analytic singularities that is not identically $-\infty $. Each $q_ i$ therefore has polar curvature with finitely many atoms, and it is an ideal model by the classification of curve models. Closedness proves the analytic-closure assertion.

By proposition 16.6.13, applied to the divisor $B\times D$ with weight one, every model and every pairwise maximum in this locus has zero upper volume; for a maximum, use $\Psi $ plus the pullback of the maximum of the two potentials on $B$. Thus all three terms of the upper-volume comparison vanish, even in the backgrounds that are not big by the lemma.

To compare different backgrounds, put $a_ i=B_ i/A_ i$, and for a positive polar measure $\mu $ on $C_ i$ of mass at most $B_ i$ write $p_{\sigma _ i}(\mu )$ for the ordinary model in background $\sigma _ i$ with polar curvature $\mu $, as in proposition 30.13.1. Sending a curve model for $\eta _ i$ with polar measure $\mu _ i$, of mass at most $A_ i$, to $p_{\sigma _ i}(a_ i\mu _ i)$, which for $a_ i=0$ is the unique normalized potential, defines an isometry from the weighted locus $\mathcal S_ a$ of proposition 16.6.34 onto $\mathcal S_\sigma $ that maps the ideal subspaces onto each other. Applying proposition 16.6.35 with $a_ i=B_ i/A_ i$ and $b_ i=B'_ i/A_ i$ gives (16.34), the exact value for ordered areas, the contractions and the diameters of their fibers. In curve coordinates the contraction is

\[ p_{\sigma _ i}(\mu )\longmapsto \begin{cases} p_{\sigma '_ i}((B'_ i/B_ i)\mu ),& B_ i>0,\\ p_{\sigma '_ i}(0),& B_ i=0. \end{cases} \]

In the second case $B'_ i=0$, because $B'_ i\leq B_ i$. Atomic projection commutes with both operations. As in theorem 30.13.3, irrational area ratios need not preserve individual analytic models, but the ideal subspaces are preserved for every ratio.

If $B_ i>0$, the models with polar measure $B_ i\delta _ x$ for infinitely many distinct points $x\in C_ i$, with all other coordinates fixed at their models with zero polar curvature, form an infinite set of ideal product models with mutual distances $2B_ i$. This excludes total boundedness of both loci. If all areas are zero, every curve coordinate is fixed at its unique normalized smooth potential, which proves the last assertion. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.38. For each $i$, let $\sigma _{i,j}$ be smooth real forms on $C_ i$ of nonnegative area that converge uniformly, as $j\to \infty $, to a smooth form $\sigma _{i,0}$ of area zero, and write $\sigma _ j=(\sigma _{1,j},\ldots ,\sigma _{m,j})$. For any nonnegative smooth auxiliary coefficients $f_ j$, the diameters of $\mathcal S_{\sigma _ j}$ tend to zero. For every fixed smooth positive volume form on $M$, the potentials in $\mathcal S_{\sigma _ j}$ converge in $L^1(M)$ to

\[ \Psi +\sum _ i u_{\sigma _{i,0}}\circ \pi _ i\circ p, \]

uniformly over all normalized model tuples.

Proof. The areas $B_{i,j}$ tend to zero, so the diameter formula applies. By theorem 1.8.15, all normalized $\sigma _{i,j}$-plurisubharmonic potentials converge in $L^1(C_ i)$ to $u_{\sigma _{i,0}}$, uniformly over this family. Integration along the fixed compact fibers bounds each pulled-back norm by a constant times the norm on the curve, and the finite sum gives the asserted uniform limit on $M$. The auxiliary coefficients do not enter these potentials or their metrics. $\square$

Thus the metric comparison sees only the areas of the curve backgrounds, while the limiting potential still records their smooth gauges. These statements concern the separated product locus; extending the metric to general ordinary models is part of 16.6.79.

16.6.11 Equality below all obstacles on separated products

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to the question raised in 16.6.4: does equality of the two envelopes below zero imply their equality below every smooth obstacle? For sums of curve potentials the answer is yes, even for obstacles that mix the coordinates. By proposition 16.6.8, membership in the relative envelope class of the ideal model implies equality below every obstacle, and this membership can be tested coordinatewise, because stretching competitors can be added before any obstacle is imposed.

Lemma 16.6.39. Let $N\geq 1$, let $X_ j$, $1\leq j\leq N$, be connected compact complex manifolds with smooth real $(1,1)$-forms $\theta _ j$, and let $u_ j,\varphi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X_ j,\theta _ j)$. On $X=\prod _ jX_ j$, with projections $\operatorname {pr}_ j$, put

\[ \vartheta =\sum _ j\operatorname {pr}_ j^*\theta _ j,\qquad u=\sum _ j u_ j\circ \operatorname {pr}_ j,\qquad \varphi =\sum _ j\varphi _ j\circ \operatorname {pr}_ j. \]

Then

\[ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\vartheta ,\varphi ) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad u_ j\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X_ j,\theta _ j,\varphi _ j) \quad \text{for every }j. \]

Proof. The ordinary order between the sums is equivalent to the ordinary order in each coordinate. Indeed, the latter follows from the former by fixing the other coordinates at points where their potentials are finite and restricting the inequality, and the converse follows by adding the coordinate inequalities.

Suppose that each $u_ j$ belongs to its relative class. For $b>1$, choose stretching competitors $h_ j$ as in definition 13.4.1. The sum $h=\sum _ jh_ j\circ \operatorname {pr}_ j$ is $\vartheta $-plurisubharmonic and satisfies

\[ b^{-1}h+(1-b^{-1})\varphi \leq u. \]

This convex inequality is meaningful at poles. For $b=1$, use $h=u$. Thus $u$ belongs to the relative class on the product.

Conversely, fix $b>1$ and a stretching competitor $h$ on the product. Choose a point $x=(x_1,\ldots ,x_ N)$ at which $h$ and all the coordinate potentials are finite; such points have full smooth volume. For each $j$, let $h^{(j)}$ be the restriction of $h$ to the slice through $x$ in the $j$th factor. It is $\theta _ j$-plurisubharmonic and not identically $-\infty $, since it is finite at $x_ j$. Put

\[ c_ j=\sum _{\ell \ne j}u_\ell (x_\ell ),\qquad d_ j=\sum _{\ell \ne j}\varphi _\ell (x_\ell ). \]

Restricting the product inequality to the slice gives

\[ b^{-1}\bigl (h^{(j)}-bc_ j+(b-1)d_ j\bigr ) +(1-b^{-1})\varphi _ j\leq u_ j. \]

This gives a stretching competitor in the $j$th coordinate for every $b>1$. Together with the ordinary order and the competitor $u_ j$ for $b=1$, it proves the converse. $\square$

Keep the curves $C_ i$ and the smooth forms $\sigma _ i$ with $\mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma _ i)\ne \varnothing $ from 16.6.10, and choose arbitrary $u_ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma _ i)$. Put

\[ u=\sum _ i u_ i\circ \pi _ i,\qquad q_ i=P_{\sigma _ i}[u_ i],\qquad r_ i=P_{\sigma _ i}[u_ i]_{\mathcal I},\qquad Q=\sum _ i r_ i\circ \pi _ i. \]

Write $\mu _ i$ and $E_ i$ for the polar and the atomic parts of the positive curvature measure $\sigma _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i$, respectively.

Proposition 16.6.40. For these data, $Q=P_{\gamma _\sigma }[u]_{\mathcal I}$. The following are equivalent:

\[ \begin{gathered} P_{\gamma _\sigma }[u]=P_{\gamma _\sigma }[u]_{\mathcal I}, \qquad \mu _ i=E_ i\quad \text{for every }i,\\ u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(Y,\gamma _\sigma ,Q),\\ P_{\gamma _\sigma ,F}[u]=P_{\gamma _\sigma ,F}[u]_{\mathcal I} \quad \text{for every smooth real }F\text{ on }Y. \end{gathered} \]

The obstacle in the last condition may mix the curve coordinates. Curve backgrounds of zero area are allowed, and the models may have zero volume.

Proof. For the zero obstacle, proposition 16.6.30 identifies the two envelopes with $\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i$ and $Q$, respectively. Since the coordinates are independent and all these functions have supremum zero, the two sums are equal if and only if $q_ i=r_ i$ for every $i$. By corollary 30.13.2, this is equivalent to $\mu _ i=E_ i$, and also to $u_ i\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(C_ i,\sigma _ i,r_ i)$, for every $i$, including backgrounds of zero area. The preceding lemma identifies these memberships with membership in the relative class on the product. Finally, proposition 16.6.8 gives equality below every smooth obstacle, and taking $F=0$ gives the reverse implication. This proves all four equivalences. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.41. On $M=Y\times C_0\times Z$ with the background $\Theta _{\sigma ,f}$, for any smooth $f\geq 0$, put $U=\Psi +u\circ p$. Then

\[ \begin{aligned} P_{\Theta _{\sigma ,f}}[U]& =\Psi +\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p,\\ P_{\Theta _{\sigma ,f}}[U]_{\mathcal I}& =\Psi +Q\circ p. \end{aligned} \]

The following are equivalent: equality of these two envelopes; $\mu _ i=E_ i$ for every $i$; $U\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta _{\sigma ,f},\Psi +Q\circ p)$; and equality of the ordinary and ideal envelopes of $U$ below every smooth real obstacle on $M$. These statements include the case $f=0$ and curve backgrounds of zero area.

Proof. On $B=Y\times C_0$, with projection $\pi $ to $Y$, use the background $\gamma _{\sigma ,f}$ of the proof of lemma 16.6.36. Sums of ordinary competitors in the separate coordinates give $P_{\gamma _{\sigma ,f}}[u\circ \pi ]\geq \sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $. For the reverse inequality, replace an ordinary competitor by its maximum with a sufficiently low translate of $u\circ \pi $. Its restriction to every section $Y\times \{ z\} $ is then not identically $-\infty $, nonpositive and at least as singular as $u$, so the separation for the zero obstacle bounds it by $\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i$. The reduction of proposition 16.6.13, applied to the divisor $B\times D$ with weight one, proves the ordinary identity upstairs.

By corollary 16.2.4, each $q_ i$ has the multiplier ideals of $u_ i$, so pullback and addition give $U\sim _{\mathcal I}\Phi _\sigma (q)$. These two potentials therefore have the same ideal envelope, which lemma 16.6.36 identifies with $\Psi +Q\circ p$.

If $\mu _ i=E_ i$ for every $i$, then $u_ i\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(C_ i,\sigma _ i,r_ i)$ by corollary 30.13.2, and we choose stretching competitors in these curve classes. The sum of their pullbacks plus $\Psi $ is $\Theta _{\sigma ,f}$-plurisubharmonic: its current is the sum of the pulled-back positive curve currents, the form $(f\circ p)s^*\tau $ and the current of the divisor with weight one. Adding the stretching inequalities gives the one for $U$ relative to $\Psi +Q\circ p$, also at poles, and the ordinary order follows in the same way. Thus $U$ belongs to that relative class, and proposition 16.6.8 gives equality below all obstacles. Taking the zero obstacle and using the two identities above recovers $q_ i=r_ i$, hence $\mu _ i=E_ i$. This closes the chain of equivalences. $\square$

These criteria concern sums of curve potentials and their extensions to the product $M$. They give neither coordinatewise formulas for the envelopes below mixed obstacles nor the implication for general potentials of zero volume.

16.6.12 Auxiliary compact complex factors

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

So far, the auxiliary factor that makes the background nonclosed has been a curve. The arguments only used that the auxiliary term in the background is semipositive and vanishes on the slices parallel to the curve product. The auxiliary factor can therefore be any compact complex manifold of positive dimension, Kähler or not, and the potentials and their polar metric still depend only on the curve coordinates and the fixed divisor of weight one.

Keep $Y$, $\sigma _ i$, $B_ i$ and $\gamma _\sigma $, as well as the data $(Z,\kappa ,D,\psi )$ of the divisor of weight one. Let $T$ be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $\ell \geq 1$, and put $B=Y\times T$, with projections $\pi $ to $Y$ and $\operatorname {pr}_ T$ to $T$. Choose a smooth semipositive real $(1,1)$-form $\alpha $ on $B$ whose restriction to every slice $Y\times \{ t\} $ is zero. By semipositivity, the tangent spaces of these slices lie in the kernel of $\alpha $, so in product coordinates $\alpha $ has only coefficients in the $T$-directions. On $M=B\times Z$, let $\varpi ,r$ be the projections, and put $p=\pi \circ \varpi $ and

\[ \Gamma _\alpha =\pi ^*\gamma _\sigma +\alpha ,\qquad \Theta _\alpha =\varpi ^*\Gamma _\alpha +r^*\kappa , \qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r. \]

We assume neither that $\alpha $ is closed nor that $M$ has the bounded mass property.

Lemma 16.6.42. For $u_ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(C_ i,\sigma _ i)$ put

\[ u=\sum _ i u_ i\circ \pi _ i,\qquad U=\Psi +u\circ p, \qquad q_ i=P_{\sigma _ i}[u_ i],\quad r_ i=P_{\sigma _ i}[u_ i]_{\mathcal I}. \]

Then

\[ \begin{aligned} P_{\Theta _\alpha }[U]& =\Psi +\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p,\\ P_{\Theta _\alpha }[U]_{\mathcal I} & =\Psi +\sum _ ir_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p. \end{aligned} \]

Every ordinary representative of either of these envelopes has zero extended mass, so their upper and lower singularity volumes are zero.

Proof. On $B$, sums of ordinary competitors on the curves give the lower bound $P_{\Gamma _\alpha }[u\circ \pi ]\geq \sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $. For the upper bound, replace a competitor by its maximum with a sufficiently low translate of $u\circ \pi $. Its restriction to every slice $Y\times \{ t\} $ is then not identically $-\infty $, and since $\alpha $ restricts to zero there, it is a $\gamma _\sigma $-plurisubharmonic ordinary competitor for $u$. The case of the zero obstacle in proposition 16.6.30 bounds it by $\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i$. This proves the identity for the ordinary envelope on $B$.

Take the atomic Green representatives $g_ i$ of the proof of proposition 16.6.30. The pullback $g$ of their sum to $B$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(B,\Gamma _\alpha )$, is multiplier-ideal equivalent to $u\circ \pi $, and is ordinarily equivalent to $\sum _ ir_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $. Its complex Hessian is a countable current of divisors along coordinate fibers, minus a smooth form; this divisor current has finite mass, as one sees with a fixed product Hermitian metric and the finite atomic masses on the curves. By lemma 1.17.4, every ideal competitor is therefore at least as singular as $g$; if the divisor current is zero, $g$ is smooth and this is immediate by compactness. Thus every such competitor is bounded by $P_{\Gamma _\alpha }[g]=P_{\Gamma _\alpha }[\sum _ ir_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi ]$, which equals $\sum _ ir_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ \pi $ by the ordinary identity just proved and $P_{\sigma _ i}[r_ i]=r_ i$. This sum is itself an ideal competitor, which proves the identity for the ideal envelope on $B$.

Apply proposition 16.6.13 to $M\to B$ and the divisor $B\times D$ with weight one. Its induced zero obstacle is zero because $\sup _ Z\psi =0$, which gives both identities upstairs. The mass assertion of the same proposition, applied to each of the two envelopes, shows that every representative of its ordinary type has zero mass, so both singularity volumes are zero. $\square$

For normalized ordinary curve models $q_ i$, write

\[ \Phi _\alpha (q)=\Psi +\sum _ iq_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p, \]

let $\mathcal S_\alpha $ be the locus of these potentials, and give it the metric

\[ \rho _\alpha (\Phi _\alpha (q),\Phi _\alpha (\widetilde q)) =\sum _ i\| \mu _ i-\widetilde\mu _ i\| _{\mathrm{TV}}, \]

where $\mu _ i,\widetilde\mu _ i$ are the polar curvatures of $q_ i,\widetilde q_ i$.

Proposition 16.6.43. The map $\Phi _\alpha (q)\mapsto \Phi _\sigma (q)$ is an isometry from $\mathcal S_\alpha $ onto the locus $\mathcal S_\sigma $ of proposition 16.6.37, and it maps the ideal subspaces and the models with analytic singularities onto each other. Consequently $\mathcal S_\alpha $ is complete and geodesic, with diameter $2\sum _ iB_ i$. Its ideal subspace is closed, geodesically convex and equal to the metric closure of the models with analytic singularities. Ideal projection is the unique nearest-point retraction onto this subspace and is $1$-Lipschitz, and the distance to the subspace is the sum of the nonatomic polar masses.

Changing $T$ or $\alpha $ does not change this abstract metric space. All the area comparisons and the contractions commuting with ideal projection of proposition 16.6.37 hold, even for different auxiliary data. The upper-volume comparison vanishes on the locus, and metric convergence implies $L^1(M)$ convergence for every smooth positive volume form. If $T$ and $Z$ are fixed and the curve backgrounds converge uniformly to backgrounds of zero area, then, for any choice of the semipositive terms $\alpha $, the potentials converge in $L^1(M)$, uniformly over the model tuples, to the sum of $\Psi $ and the pullbacks of the normalized smooth potentials of the limiting curve backgrounds, as in corollary 16.6.38.

Proof. Applied to model tuples, the lemma shows that every $\Phi _\alpha (q)$ is a normalized ordinary model and computes its ideal projection coordinatewise. Restrictions to curve sections on which the other potentials are finite determine the tuple up to constants, and the normalization of the coordinates determines these constants. Thus the map is well defined and bijective, and the formulas for the polar curvatures make it an isometry preserving ideal projection.

Each potential in either locus is pulled back from $Y\times Z$. Analytic presentations pull back under the projection that forgets the auxiliary factor. Conversely, by proposition 1.13.1, the restriction of a potential with analytic singularities to a section with fixed auxiliary coordinate has analytic singularities and is not identically $-\infty $. Hence a potential in either locus has analytic singularities exactly when the common potential on $Y\times Z$ does, and the isometry maps the analytic models onto each other. The statements on the complete metric, the ideal closure, geodesics, nearest points and area comparisons now follow from proposition 16.6.37.

A pairwise maximum is $\Psi $ plus the pullback of the maximum of two $\Gamma _\alpha $-plurisubharmonic functions on $B$. The reduction along the divisor of weight one gives zero upper volume for it and for both models, so the upper-volume comparison vanishes. For $L^1$ convergence, integration along the fixed compact fibers bounds the norm of each pulled-back curve potential by a constant times its norm on the curve. The same estimate and theorem 1.8.15 give the asserted uniform limit at zero area. Neither estimate involves $\alpha $. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.44. For the arbitrary curve potentials $u_ i$ of the lemma, put $R=\Psi +\sum _ ir_ i\circ \pi _ i\circ p$. The following are equivalent: $P_{\Theta _\alpha }[U]=P_{\Theta _\alpha }[U]_{\mathcal I}$; for each $i$, the polar part of the curvature measure of $u_ i$ equals its atomic part; $U\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta _\alpha ,R)$; and the ordinary and ideal envelopes of $U$ agree below every smooth real obstacle on $M$.

Proof. By the lemma and the normalization of the coordinates, equality of the envelopes below zero is equivalent to $q_ i=r_ i$ for every $i$. By corollary 30.13.2, this is equivalent to equality of the polar and atomic measures, and in that case $u_ i\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(C_ i,\sigma _ i,r_ i)$. Choose stretching competitors in these curve classes. The sum of their pullbacks plus $\Psi $ has positive current, since the contributions of the curves, the form $\varpi ^*\alpha $ and the current of the divisor are all positive. Adding the convex stretching inequalities, which is possible also at poles, gives a stretching competitor relative to $R$ for every finite stretch, and adding the ordinary bounds gives $U\preceq R$. Thus $U\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(M,\Theta _\alpha ,R)$, and proposition 16.6.8 gives equality below every smooth obstacle. Taking the zero obstacle recovers the first condition, which proves the equivalence. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.45. If $\alpha =0$ or some $B_ i=0$, the background $\Theta _\alpha $ is not big. If all $B_ i>0$ and $\alpha $ is positive on every nonzero vector tangent to the factor $T$, it is big. In particular, for a Hermitian form $\omega _ T$ on $T$ and a positive nonconstant smooth function $h$ on $Y$, the choice $\alpha =(h\circ \pi )\operatorname {pr}_ T^*\omega _ T$ gives a big background that is not closed whenever all $B_ i>0$.

Proof. For $\alpha =0$, the background is pulled back from $Y\times Z$, and by proposition 1.22.3 every potential in it descends to $Y\times Z$. The corresponding currents have no component in the $T$-directions, so they cannot dominate a positive multiple of a Hermitian form on $M$. Thus the background is not big.

If $B_ i=0$, choose any Hermitian form $\omega _ T$ on $T$ and write $s:M\to T$ for the projection. The positive $(m+\ell +k-1,m+\ell +k-1)$-form

\[ L_ i=p^*\left(\bigwedge _{j\ne i}\pi _ j^*\eta _ j\right) \wedge s^*(\omega _ T^\ell )\wedge r^*(\kappa ^ k) \]

is closed, because $\omega _ T^\ell $ has top degree on $T$. Its pairing with $\Theta _\alpha $ is zero, since the only possible contribution contains the integral of $\sigma _ i$, whereas its pairing with a positive product Hermitian form is strictly positive. By Stokes' theorem, no current in the background is bounded below by a positive multiple of a Hermitian form.

When all areas are positive, write $\sigma _ i=B_ i\eta _ i/A_ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _ i$. Subtracting the smooth pullback of $\sum _ i\chi _ i\circ \pi _ i$ gives the current $p^*\sum _ i(B_ i/A_ i)\pi _ i^*\eta _ i+\varpi ^*\alpha +r^*\kappa $. Positivity of $\alpha $ in the $T$-directions makes this form strictly positive, which proves bigness. For the stated choice of $\alpha $, the exterior derivative of $\Theta _\alpha $ has the component $\mathrm dh\wedge \omega _ T$, with one direction in $Y$ and two in $T$. It is nonzero for nonconstant $h$ and cannot be canceled by the component with three directions in $T$. Hence $\mathrm d\Theta _\alpha \ne 0$. $\square$

The proofs use no bound on the ambient masses, so the auxiliary manifold may fail the bounded mass property; an explicit example follows. The complete metric and the criterion for all obstacles concern only the specified product locus.

16.6.13 A polar metric in a background with unbounded mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Taking the Hopf threefold of theorem 8.0.1 as the auxiliary factor makes the ambient masses unbounded, while the complete metric on the product models is unchanged. Letting the coefficient of the Hopf factor vary over the curve product also makes the background nonclosed.

Example 16.6.46. There is a compact complex sixfold with a smooth positive background $\Theta $ that is not closed and has infinite upper mass $v_+(\Theta )=+\infty $ in the sense of definition 6.5.1, while the locus of separated product models of proposition 16.6.43, along a divisor of weight one, has a complete geodesic polar metric of diameter $4$. The ideal subspace is closed and is the metric closure of the models with analytic singularities, and ideal projection is the unique nearest-point retraction onto it and is $1$-Lipschitz. The upper-volume comparison vanishes on this locus. The locus contains distinct ideal models with analytic singularities at polar distance $1$.

Proof. Take $Y=C_1\times C_2$ with $C_1=C_2=\mathbb P^1$ and normalized Fubini–Study forms $\omega _ i$ of area one, and write $\pi _ i:Y\to C_ i$ for the projections. Let $(T,\omega _ T,V_ j)$ be the Hopf threefold, its Gauduchon form and the smooth potentials of theorem 8.0.1. Take $Z=\mathbb P^1$ with its normalized Fubini–Study form $\kappa $, a point $D$ and its normalized potential $\psi $ satisfying $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =[D]$. On $M=Y\times T\times Z$, write $p,s,r$ for the projections. Choose a positive nonconstant smooth function $h$ on $Y$ and put

\[ \Theta =p^*(\pi _1^*\omega _1+\pi _2^*\omega _2) +(h\circ p)s^*\omega _ T+r^*\kappa , \qquad \Psi =\psi \circ r. \]

This form is strictly positive. The component $\mathrm dh\wedge \omega _ T$ of its exterior derivative, with one direction in $Y$ and two in $T$, is nonzero and cannot be canceled by the component with three directions in $T$. Thus $\Theta $ is not closed.

Put $c=\min _ Yh>0$. The smooth functions $W_ j=cV_ j\circ s$ are strictly $\Theta $-plurisubharmonic, since their current in the $T$-directions is

\[ h\omega _ T+c\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j =c(\omega _ T+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)+(h-c)\omega _ T>0. \]

Only the part of degree three in the $T$-directions contributes to the top-degree product. The three curve forms have integral one, so positivity of smooth mixed products and Fubini's theorem give

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _ M(\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W_ j)^6 & =120\int _ Y\pi _1^*\omega _1\wedge \pi _2^*\omega _2 \int _ T(h(y)\omega _ T+c\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)^3\\ & \geq 120c^3\int _ T(\omega _ T+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)^3 \longrightarrow +\infty . \end{aligned} \]

The factor $120=6!/3!$ counts the ways of choosing one factor for each curve direction and three for $T$. Each $W_ j$ is bounded, so definition 6.5.1 gives $v_+(\Theta )=+\infty $, and the bounded mass property fails.

Apply proposition 16.6.43 to this product, with both curve areas equal to one. On the locus of potentials $\Psi +q_1\circ \pi _1\circ p+q_2\circ \pi _2\circ p$, where each $q_ i$ ranges over the ordinary $\omega _ i$-models with supremum zero, it gives a complete metric $\rho $ of diameter $4$, the analytic closure of the ideal subspace, the nearest-point retraction and the vanishing of the upper-volume comparison. These conclusions use no bound on the ambient masses.

For $x\in C_1$, let $g_ x$ be the normalized Green potential of the point $x$, with $\omega _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ x=[x]$. By lemma 16.6.42, the potentials

\[ \Phi _0=\Psi ,\qquad \Phi _ x=\Psi +g_ x\circ \pi _1\circ p \]

are ordinary and ideal models, and their divisorial poles with coefficient one give analytic singularities. Their tuples of polar curvatures are $(0,0)$ and $(\delta _ x,0)$, so $\rho (\Phi _0,\Phi _ x)=\| \delta _ x\| _{\mathrm{TV}}=1$. Nevertheless, by the same lemma, every representative of either ordinary type has zero extended mass; in particular, all their upper and lower singularity volumes are zero. $\square$

16.6.14 Models over a curve

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The polar metric on a curve also describes models on a compact manifold that maps onto it with connected fibers, including critical fibers. A background pulled back from the curve has no vertical curvature, so every potential descends. Gauduchon volumes of the fiber divisors are constant, with their multiplicities included. The normalized polar trace therefore recovers the curve metric without choosing a product structure.

Let $p:X\to C$ be a surjective holomorphic map with connected fibers, where $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 2$ and $C$ is a connected compact Riemann surface. Fix a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ on $C$, put $A=\int _ C\beta $ and $\theta =p^*\beta $, and choose a smooth positive area-one form $\eta $ on $C$. For $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$ write

\[ T_ v=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v,\qquad \mu _ v=\mathbf1_{\{ v=-\infty \} }T_ v. \]

Thus $\mu _ v$ is the polar curvature of the curve potential.

Lemma 16.6.47. The class $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is nonempty if and only if $A\geq 0$. For $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$ and $U=v\circ p$, one has

\[ P_\theta [U]=P_\beta [v]\circ p,\qquad P_\theta [U]_{\mathcal I}=P_\beta [v]_{\mathcal I}\circ p. \]

Pullback is a bijection between the ordinary model spaces and between their ideal subspaces. Every potential in this background has zero extended Monge–Ampère measure, and every ordinary type has zero upper and lower singularity volumes.

If $A=0$ and $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$, the only normalized potential is $-h\circ p+\min _ Ch$. It is smooth and is the unique ordinary and ideal model.

Proof. By 1.22.4, every potential descends, even for this possibly indefinite background. Thus nonemptiness is equivalent to the curve condition in lemma 1.8.14. Descent preserves the ordinary order, and 1.22.6 preserves and reflects the all-weight ideal order. At each $x\in X$, choose coordinates centered at $x$ and $p(x)$. A complex line detects the first nonzero homogeneous term of $p$. Its restriction to that line is open, so $p$ is open. Upper regularization therefore commutes with pullback, proving the envelope identities and the model bijections.

Write $\beta =A\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ with $h$ smooth, using lemma 1.8.14. For $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$, put $w=v+h\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,A\eta )$. Then

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v\circ p) =A p^*\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w\circ p). \]

When $A>0$, put $W=w\circ p$. Its bounded truncations are $\max (W,-j)=\max (w,-j)\circ p$. Locally choose a smooth $a$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=A\eta $. The bounded subharmonic function $a+\max (w,-j)$ has smooth decreasing local approximants. Their pullbacks have complex Hessian rank at most one, including at critical points, so their $n$th powers vanish. Bounded product continuity makes each truncation have zero top product, and hence $W$ has zero extended measure in the positive background. The current invariance in theorem 6.1.2 gives zero extended measure in the original decomposition as well.

When $A=0$, the curve lemma gives $v=-h+c$. Its pullback is smooth with zero curvature current, so its top product is zero. Surjectivity gives the stated normalization. Every potential differs from this one by a constant, and both envelopes fix its normalized representative. Since descent applies to every representative of every ordinary type, both singularity volumes are zero in all cases. $\square$

Henceforth assume $A\geq 0$. Fix a Gauduchon form $\gamma $ on $X$. For a finite positive measure $\mu $ of mass $M$, let $q_\mu $ be the solution normalized by $\int _ Cq_\mu \, \eta =0$ to

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_\mu =\mu -M\eta . \]

The curve Green kernel of section 19.3 gives this quasi-subharmonic solution. Define the positive current

\[ p^*\mu =M p^*\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(q_\mu \circ p),\qquad L_\gamma (\mu )=p^*\mu \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}. \]

Locally this is the pullback of a subharmonic potential of $\mu $; changing that potential adds a harmonic function and does not change the current. Extend both operators to signed measures by linearity. Write $F_ s=p^{-1}(s)$ for the fiber divisor with its scheme multiplicities.

Lemma 16.6.48. There is a positive constant $\kappa _\gamma $ such that, for every finite signed measure $\mu $ and every Borel set $B\subset C$,

\[ \begin{gathered} p_*L_\gamma (\mu )=\kappa _\gamma \mu ,\qquad \| L_\gamma (\mu )\| _{\mathrm{TV}} =\kappa _\gamma \| \mu \| _{\mathrm{TV}},\\ L_\gamma (\mathbf1_ B\mu ) =\mathbf1_{p^{-1}(B)}L_\gamma (\mu ),\qquad \int _{F_ s}\gamma ^{n-1}=\kappa _\gamma \quad (s\in C). \end{gathered} \]

The last integral includes the multiplicities of the fiber.

Proof. The positive $(0,0)$-current $S=p_*(\gamma ^{n-1})$ on $C$ satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}S=p_*\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\gamma ^{n-1})=0$. A harmonic distribution is smooth, so $S$ is a constant $\kappa _\gamma $. On the complement of the critical values, smooth fiber integration identifies it with the positive integral of $\gamma ^{n-1}$ on a regular fiber. Thus $\kappa _\gamma >0$.

First let $\mu $ be positive with mass $M>0$. Choose smooth positive measures $\mu _ j$ with the same mass and $\mu _ j\rightharpoonup \mu $. Their mean-zero solutions $q_ j$ have a common lower curvature bound $-M\eta $. Normalized compactness bounds $\sup _ Cq_ j$: their mean-zero condition makes this supremum the negative mean of their supremum-zero normalization. Hence $(q_ j)$ is precompact in $L^1(C)$. Every limit has mean zero and Hessian $\mu -M\eta $. Two such limits differ by a harmonic function, hence by a constant, which their means force to be zero. Therefore $q_ j\to q_\mu $ in $L^1(C)$.

By 1.22.7, $q_ j\circ p\to q_\mu \circ p$ in $L^1(X)$. Thus $p^*\mu _ j\to p^*\mu $ as currents and their smooth Hermitian traces converge weakly. For smooth $\mu _ j$, the definition of $S$ gives $p_*L_\gamma (\mu _ j)=\kappa _\gamma \mu _ j$. Passing to the limit proves the identity for $\mu $. The case $M=0$ is zero, and linearity proves the signed identity.

For a positive $\mu $, split it into $\mathbf1_ B\mu $ and $\mathbf1_{C\setminus B}\mu $. Their traces are positive; the pushforward identity shows that they are respectively carried by $p^{-1}(B)$ and its complement. Additivity then gives the restriction identity. Apply the same argument to the positive and negative Jordan parts of a signed measure. Their disjoint Borel carriers pull back to disjoint carriers, so their traces are the Jordan parts upstairs. The pushforward identity gives the asserted total-variation norm.

Finally the Poincaré–Lelong formula in the book normalization gives $p^*\delta _ s=[F_ s]$, including every zero multiplicity of a local equation of the fiber. Its trace has total mass $\kappa _\gamma $, by the pushforward identity with $\mu =\delta _ s$. $\square$

Let $\mathcal M(\theta )$ be the ordinary model space. For $U=v\circ p\in \mathcal M(\theta )$, its polar trace measure is

\[ \lambda _ U^\gamma =\mathbf1_{\{ U=-\infty \} } (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =L_\gamma (\mu _ v). \]

The last identity follows from the Borel restriction formula of 16.6.48, including at critical fibers and for a nonatomic polar measure. Define

\[ \rho _\theta (U,V)=\kappa _\gamma ^{-1} \| \lambda _ U^\gamma -\lambda _ V^\gamma \| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \]

Proposition 16.6.49. The metric $\rho _\theta $ is independent of the Gauduchon form $\gamma $. Pullback is an isometry from the complete geodesic curve model space onto $(\mathcal M(\theta ),\rho _\theta )$, which has diameter $2A$. The ideal models form a closed geodesically convex subspace equal to the metric closure of the models with analytic singularities. Ideal projection is its unique nearest-point retraction and is $1$-Lipschitz. If $U=v\circ p$ is a model, its distance to that subspace is the nonatomic polar mass of $v$.

Metric convergence implies $L^1(X)$ convergence for every smooth positive volume form. The upper-volume comparison on $\mathcal M(\theta )$ vanishes identically. At $A=0$ this is the one-point metric space of the unique smooth model.

Proof. The definition of pullback measure gives $p^*T_ v=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v\circ p)$: in the smooth decomposition $\beta =A\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$, both sides are $A p^*\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((v+h)\circ p)$. Apply the Borel restriction and total-variation identities of 16.6.48 to get

\[ \lambda _{v\circ p}^\gamma =L_\gamma (\mu _ v),\qquad \rho _\theta (v\circ p,w\circ p) =\| \mu _ v-\mu _ w\| _{\mathrm{TV}}=d_\beta (v,w). \]

The envelope bijection and proposition 30.13.1 give the isometry, completeness and geodesics. Polar measures have mass at most $A$, so their mutual distance is at most $2A$. For $A>0$, the models of $A\delta _ s$ and $A\delta _ t$, $s\ne t$, attain this bound. For $A=0$, 16.6.47 gives the singleton.

Ideal projection corresponds to the atomic part of the polar measure by the same curve classification. If $\mu =\mu _{\mathrm{at}}+\mu _{\mathrm{diff}}$ and $\nu $ is atomic, the measures $\mu _{\mathrm{diff}}$ and $\mu _{\mathrm{at}}-\nu $ are mutually singular. Therefore

\[ \| \mu -\nu \| _{\mathrm{TV}} =\mu _{\mathrm{diff}}(C) +\| \mu _{\mathrm{at}}-\nu \| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \]

The minimum is attained exactly at $\nu =\mu _{\mathrm{at}}$. Taking the atomic part does not increase total variation, giving the Lipschitz estimate. Atomic polar measures of mass at most $A$ form a closed convex subset of the polar-measure space.

For $A>0$, the smooth-gauge model bijection in the proof of proposition 30.13.1 preserves analytic singularities in both directions: its envelope differs by a bounded function from the smoothly transported analytic model, by proposition 16.3.1. Thus the analytic density in corollary 30.12.3 carries from $A\eta $ to $\beta $. The analytic descent equivalence of 1.22.8 and the envelope bijection then give the stated closure upstairs. At zero area the unique smooth model is already analytic and ideal.

Curve metric convergence implies $L^1(C)$ convergence by proposition 30.13.1, and 1.22.7 gives convergence upstairs. Finally all top-degree masses, including those of pairwise maxima, vanish by 16.6.47. The upper-volume comparison therefore vanishes. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.50. Let $U=v\circ p$ and $R=P_\theta [U]_{\mathcal I}$. The following are equivalent: $P_\theta [U]=P_\theta [U]_{\mathcal I}$; $\mu _ v$ is atomic; $U\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\theta ,R)$; and the ordinary and ideal envelopes of $U$ agree below every smooth real obstacle on $X$. At zero area all four conditions hold.

Proof. Put $r=P_\beta [v]_{\mathcal I}$. The envelope bijection gives $R=r\circ p$. By corollary 30.13.2, base envelope equality is equivalent to atomic polar curvature and to $v\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(C,\beta ,r)$. For each stretching parameter $b\geq 1$, descent and surjectivity identify competitors satisfying $b^{-1}h+(1-b^{-1})r\leq v$ with their upstairs counterparts. This uses only convex combinations and remains valid at poles. Thus the relative-class conditions correspond. Apply proposition 16.6.8 for equality below every smooth obstacle on $X$; the converse includes the zero obstacle. The zero-area assertion follows from the smooth singleton. $\square$

The top-degree measure is zero for every potential, yet the first polar trace separates every pair of models. Here the one-dimensional quotient supplies all the singularity data.

16.6.15 Finite curve factors

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Keep $X$ and $C$ as above, but now allow $p:X\to C$ to have disconnected fibers. By 1.22.9, it has the component factorization

\[ X\xrightarrow {q}C'\xrightarrow {r}C, \qquad p=r\circ q, \]

where $q$ is surjective and holomorphic with connected fibers, $C'$ is a connected compact Riemann surface, and $r$ is finite and holomorphic of degree $d\geq 1$. Let $\beta $ be any smooth real form on $C$, put $A=\int _ C\beta $, and set $\sigma =r^*\beta $ and $\theta =p^*\beta =q^*\sigma $.

Proposition 16.6.51. The class $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is nonempty exactly when $A\geq 0$. For $A\geq 0$, the entire ordinary $\theta $-model space is isometric, using the $q$-fiber normalization, to the curve model space in background $\sigma $, whose area is $dA$. Its diameter is $2dA$; it has the analytic closure, ideal retraction, convergence and all-obstacle properties above. Every potential has zero extended top-degree measure and every ordinary type has zero upper and lower singularity volumes. At $A=0$ the model space is a smooth singleton.

If $\kappa _ q^\gamma $ and $\kappa _ p^\gamma $ are the constants defined by the pushforwards of $\gamma ^{n-1}$ to $C'$ and $C$, then

\[ \kappa _ p^\gamma =d\kappa _ q^\gamma . \]

For $A\geq 0$, normalizing the same polar-trace variation by $\kappa _ p^\gamma $ gives $d^{-1}$ times the $q$-normalized metric, with diameter $2A$. This rescaled metric is also independent of $\gamma $. For a model $U=v\circ q$ with polar measure $\mu _ v$,

\[ p_*\lambda _ U^\gamma =\kappa _ q^\gamma r_*\mu _ v. \]

Proof. Away from the finitely many critical values, $r$ is a covering with $d$ sheets. Change of variables on these sheets gives

\[ \int _{C'}r^*\alpha =d\int _ C\alpha \]

for every smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\alpha $; the branch points and their inverse images have smooth area zero. In particular $\int _{C'}\sigma =dA$. The envelope lemma 16.6.47, applied to $q$, gives nonemptiness exactly for $dA\geq 0$, equivalent to $A\geq 0$, and proves the zero-mass and zero-area assertions. In this nonempty case, apply 16.6.49, 16.6.50 to $q$ and the smooth background $\sigma $, which may vanish at branch points or be indefinite.

The Gauduchon trace lemma for $q$ gives $q_*(\gamma ^{n-1})=\kappa _ q^\gamma $. For a smooth test form $\alpha $ on $C$, the projection formula and the covering calculation yield

\[ \int _ Xp^*\alpha \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\kappa _ q^\gamma \int _{C'}r^*\alpha =d\kappa _ q^\gamma \int _ C\alpha . \]

Thus $p_*(\gamma ^{n-1})$ is the constant $d\kappa _ q^\gamma $. The ratio of the two normalizations is $d^{-1}$, giving every metric assertion. Finally $q_*\lambda _ U^\gamma =\kappa _ q^\gamma \mu _ v$, so composing pushforwards proves the last identity. $\square$

Example 16.6.52. Let $X=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, let $q$ be its first projection and let $r:\mathbb P^1\to \mathbb P^1$ be $r([z_0:z_1])=[z_0^2:z_1^2]$. Put $p=r\circ q$ and $\theta =p^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$, with $\int _{\mathbb P^1}\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}=1$. There are two distinct analytic ideal $\theta $-models $U_+,U_-$ which do not descend through $p$. Their $q$-normalized polar distance is four and their $p$-normalized distance is two, yet

\[ p_*\lambda _{U_+}^\gamma =p_*\lambda _{U_-}^\gamma \]

for every Gauduchon form $\gamma $. Thus the pushforward of polar trace to the original base does not separate the models.

Proof. The finite map $r$ has degree two and is branched at zero and infinity. In the affine coordinate it is $r(z)=z^2$, so the smooth semipositive form $\sigma =r^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ has area two. For $s\in \{ 1,-1\} $, set

\[ v_ s(z)=2\log |z-s|^2-\log (1+|z|^4)-\log 8. \]

This extends smoothly across infinity and has curvature $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ s=2\delta _ s$. The inequality $|z-s|^4\leq (|z|+1)^4\leq 8(1+|z|^4)$, with equality at $z=-s$, shows that its supremum is zero. The model of $2\delta _ s$ from proposition 30.13.1 has precisely this total curvature, since its polar measure already has the full mass two. Harmonic uniqueness and normalization identify it with $v_ s$. Write $v_+=v_1$ and $v_-=v_{-1}$. Both have analytic singularities, hence are ideal by proposition 16.2.7. The model pullback bijection makes $U_\pm =v_\pm \circ q$ analytic ideal $\theta $-models.

The function $U_+$ is $-\infty $ on $q^{-1}(1)$ and finite on $q^{-1}(-1)$, although both divisors are mapped by $p$ to the same base point $1$. It therefore cannot descend through $p$; the same argument with the divisors exchanged applies to $U_-$. The variation of $2\delta _1-2\delta _{-1}$ is four. The finite factor proposition gives the two distances and

\[ p_*\lambda _{U_\pm }^\gamma =2\kappa _ q^\gamma \delta _1. \]

The upstairs polar traces are distinct, while this common pushforward loses which component carries the pole. $\square$

16.6.16 Models pulled back by a finite curve map

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $r:C'\to C$ be a finite surjective holomorphic map of degree $d$ between connected compact Riemann surfaces. Fix a smooth real form $\beta $ of area $A\geq 0$ on $C$ and put $\sigma =r^*\beta $. The models pulled back from $C$ occupy a proper subspace of the full model space on $C'$ in general. The fiber maximum of 1.22.10 compares envelope competitors on the two curves. The weighted measure pullback then identifies the metric on this subspace and gives a retraction from the full space.

Proposition 16.6.53. For $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$ and every smooth real obstacle $F$ on $C$,

\[ \begin{aligned} P_{\sigma ,F\circ r}[v\circ r] & =P_{\beta ,F}[v]\circ r,\\ P_{\sigma ,F\circ r}[v\circ r]_{\mathcal I} & =P_{\beta ,F}[v]_{\mathcal I}\circ r. \end{aligned} \]

In particular, $J(v)=v\circ r$ embeds the ordinary model space $\mathcal M(\beta )$ into $\mathcal M(\sigma )$ and preserves ideal projection.

Proof. Pullback sends every base competitor to an upstairs competitor: it preserves positivity, the obstacle inequality, the ordinary order and, by the Lelong formula and the curve valuative criterion, the all-weight ideal order. Conversely, if $h$ is an upstairs competitor, its fiber maximum $m_ h$ is a base competitor by 1.22.10, since $h\leq F\circ r$ implies $m_ h\leq F$. Also $h\leq m_ h\circ r$. Thus the two unregularized suprema are equal after pullback. A finite nonconstant holomorphic map of curves is open; upper regularization commutes with its pullback, as is seen in the coordinates $t=z^ e$. This proves both identities. Take $F=0$. An ordinary model is fixed by its ordinary envelope, so its pullback is a model; surjectivity of $r$ gives injectivity. Applying the ideal identity to a model proves preservation of ideal projection. $\square$

Let $d_\beta $ and $d_\sigma $ be the polar metrics from proposition 30.13.1. If $q$ is a model on $C'$, denote its polar curvature by $\mu _ q=\mathbf1_{\{ q=-\infty \} }(\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)$. Write $p_\beta (\nu )$ for the base model with polar measure $\nu $, using the same classification. Write $U_\beta (v)=\int _ C\mathrm{MA}_\beta (v)$ and $U_\sigma (q)=\int _{C'}\mathrm{MA}_\sigma (q)$ for their non-pluripolar volumes.

Proposition 16.6.54. For $u,v\in \mathcal M(\beta )$,

\[ d_\sigma (J(u),J(v))=d\, d_\beta (u,v),\qquad U_\sigma (J(u))=d\, U_\beta (u). \]

The image $J(\mathcal M(\beta ))$ is closed and contains the linear polar-measure geodesic between each pair of its members. The map

\[ R(q)=J\! \left(p_\beta \! \left(d^{-1}r_*\mu _ q\right)\right) \]

is a $1$-Lipschitz retraction onto this image. Its polar measure is

\[ \mu _{R(q)}=d^{-1}r^*r_*\mu _ q. \]

It preserves the polar mass and the non-pluripolar volume, and commutes with ideal projection on $\mathcal M(\sigma )$. At $A=0$ both model spaces are smooth singletons.

Proof. Change of variables off the finite branch sets gives $\int _{C'}\sigma =dA$. By 1.22.11, the polar measure of $J(v)$ is $r^*\mu _ v$, with total mass $d\mu _ v(C)$. The curve classification gives

\[ \begin{aligned} d_\sigma (J(u),J(v)) & =\| r^*(\mu _ u-\mu _ v)\| _{\mathrm{TV}} =d\| \mu _ u-\mu _ v\| _{\mathrm{TV}},\\ U_\sigma (J(v)) & =dA-d\mu _ v(C)=d\, U_\beta (v). \end{aligned} \]

Completeness of the base model space proves closedness of the image: a convergent sequence in the image comes from a Cauchy sequence downstairs. Linear interpolation of polar measures commutes with $r^*$, giving the stated geodesics.

For any $q\in \mathcal M(\sigma )$, its polar measure has mass at most $dA$. Its pushforward is polar by the image assertion of 1.22.11, so $d^{-1}r_*\mu _ q$ has mass at most $A$ and defines a base model. This proves that $R$ is well defined and gives its displayed polar measure. The identity $r_*r^*=d$ makes $R\circ J=J$. For any finite signed measure $\tau $ on $C'$, pushforward does not increase total variation, hence

\[ \| d^{-1}r^*r_*\tau \| _{\mathrm{TV}} =\| r_*\tau \| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq \| \tau \| _{\mathrm{TV}}. \]

Apply this to the difference of two polar measures to obtain the Lipschitz estimate. The averaging operator preserves their total mass, so the formula $U_\sigma (q)=dA-\mu _ q(C')$ gives volume preservation. Both measure operations commute with taking the atomic part, which is ideal projection by the curve classification. This proves the commutation assertion. At zero area all polar measures vanish and both spaces are singletons. $\square$

Corollary 16.6.55. Let $p:X\to C$ be a surjective holomorphic map from a connected compact complex manifold of dimension at least two, with component factorization $p=r\circ q$, $q:X\to C'$. For $\theta =p^*\beta $, the ordinary models that are constant on each $p$-fiber form a closed complete subspace. With the $p$-fiber normalization of 16.6.51, the map $v\mapsto v\circ p$ is an isometry from $(\mathcal M(\beta ),d_\beta )$ onto this subspace.

Every model has the form $U=u\circ q$, and

\[ R_ X(U)=R(u)\circ q \]

is a $1$-Lipschitz retraction onto the subspace. It commutes with ideal projection. The subspace contains the linear polar-measure geodesic between each pair of its members.

Proof. The full model correspondence for $q$ is 16.6.47. A model $u\circ q$ is constant on $p$-fibers exactly when $u$ is constant on $r$-fibers. In that case $u=m_ u\circ r$, and 1.22.10 makes $m_ u$ a base potential. The envelope identity and injectivity of $J$ then show that $m_ u$ is a model. Thus this subspace is exactly the pullback image of $\mathcal M(\beta )$.

By 16.6.51, the $p$-normalized metric is $d^{-1}$ times the polar metric on $C'$. The preceding proposition proves the isometry, closedness, completeness, retraction and geodesic assertions. The ideal-envelope correspondence for $q$ transfers the commutation with ideal projection. $\square$

This retraction averages the singularity data over a fiber. Unlike the ideal retraction, it need not select a nearest model.

Example 16.6.56. For $r:\mathbb P^1\to \mathbb P^1$, $r(z)=z^3$, and the Fubini–Study background $\beta $ of area one, the retraction $R$ above is not a nearest-point retraction, even on ideal models with analytic singularities.

Proof. Put $\sigma =r^*\beta $, of area three, and let $q$ be its model with polar measure $\delta _1$. This is an analytic ideal model: in the affine coordinate, the global function

\[ h(z)=\log |z-1|^2-\tfrac 13\log (1+|z|^6) \]

extends smoothly across infinity and satisfies $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\delta _1+\tfrac 23\sigma $. Thus $h$ is $\sigma $-plurisubharmonic with one logarithmic pole, and the curve classification identifies its envelope with $q$; proposition 16.2.7 preserves its analytic type. Writing $\zeta =\exp (2\pi \mathrm i/3)$, one has

\[ \mu _{R(q)}=\tfrac 13(\delta _1+\delta _\zeta +\delta _{\zeta ^2}). \]

The three atoms are distinct, so

\[ d_\sigma (q,R(q))=\tfrac 23+\tfrac 13+\tfrac 13=\tfrac 43. \]

The zero model is $J(0)$ and has zero polar measure, giving $d_\sigma (q,J(0))=1$. Hence the averaged model is strictly farther from $q$ than a member of the same pullback subspace. $\square$

16.6.17 Hopf surface fibers and unbounded ambient mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The complete model space of a curve submersion can occur on a manifold whose ambient Hermitian masses are unbounded. Blowing up the elliptic base locus of the projection $[z_0:z_1]$ on the scalar Hopf threefold turns this projection into a submersion with Hopf surface fibers. The rank-one background then retains only the curve singularities, while the smooth potentials of the Hopf threefold still force unbounded mass in a positive ambient background.

Example 16.6.57. There is a connected compact complex threefold $Z$ with a surjective holomorphic submersion $p:Z\to \mathbb P^1$ whose fibers are scalar Hopf surfaces. The manifold $Z$ does not have the bounded mass property. For the pullback $\theta =p^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ of the Fubini–Study form of area one, the entire ordinary model space has the complete geodesic polar metric of 16.6.49, with diameter two. Its ideal subspace is the analytic closure, with the unique nearest-point, $1$-Lipschitz ideal retraction. All potentials in this background have zero extended Monge–Ampère measure, yet it contains analytic ideal models at polar distance one.

Proof. Put $\lambda =\mathrm e^{-1}$ and consider the vector bundle

\[ \mathcal V=\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1) \oplus \mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}. \]

Let $\mathcal V^\times $ be its total space with the zero section removed, and define

\[ Z=\mathcal V^\times /\langle v\mapsto \lambda v\rangle . \]

The bundle projection descends to a holomorphic map $p:Z\to \mathbb P^1$. A Hermitian norm on the bundle satisfies $\| \lambda ^ kv\| =\lambda ^ k\| v\| $. The action is free and proper: on compact subsets of $\mathcal V^\times $ the norms have positive lower and finite upper bounds, so only finitely many powers can move one such subset to meet another. Thus the quotient is a complex threefold. The annulus bundle $\{ \lambda \leq \| v\| \leq 1\} $ is compact over $\mathbb P^1$ and meets every orbit, so $Z$ is compact. Bundle charts identify $p^{-1}(U)$ with

\[ U\times \bigl ((\mathbb C^2\setminus \{ 0\} )/ \langle w\mapsto \lambda w\rangle \bigr ). \]

Hence $p$ is a holomorphic submersion with connected compact Hopf surface fibers, and $Z$ is connected.

The tautological inclusion into $\mathbb C^2$ identifies $\mathcal V^\times $ with the incidence manifold

\[ \left\{ ([s:t],z)\in \mathbb P^1\times (\mathbb C^3\setminus \{ 0\} ):\ t z_0=s z_1\right\} . \]

The last coordinate $z_2$ comes from the trivial summand. This is the blowup of $\mathbb C^3\setminus \{ 0\} $ along $L=\{ z_0=z_1=0,\ z_2\ne 0\} $: in the chart $s\ne 0$, write $a=t/s$ and $z_1=az_0$, while in the chart $t\ne 0$ write $b=s/t$ and $z_0=bz_1$. These are the two usual blowup charts of the ideal $(z_0,z_1)$. The scalar contraction preserves the incidence relation and commutes with projection to $z$. It therefore descends to a modification

\[ \mu :Z\longrightarrow H=(\mathbb C^3\setminus \{ 0\} )/ \langle z\mapsto \lambda z\rangle . \]

The map is an isomorphism away from the elliptic curve $L/\langle \lambda \rangle $. Local quotient charts show that it is its blowup; in particular it has degree one.

Take the Gauduchon form $\omega $ and smooth potentials $V_ j$ on $H$ from theorem 8.0.1. Since $Z$ is compact, a sufficiently large multiple $\Omega $ of any Hermitian form satisfies $\Omega -\mu ^*\omega >0$. Put $W_ j=V_ j\circ \mu $. Then

\[ \Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W_ j =\Omega -\mu ^*\omega +\mu ^*(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)>0. \]

Positivity of smooth mixed products and the degree-one change of variables off the exceptional divisor give

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _ Z(\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W_ j)^3 & \geq \int _ Z\mu ^*(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)^3\\ & =\int _ H(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)^3\longrightarrow +\infty . \end{aligned} \]

The exceptional divisor and its image have zero measure for smooth top-degree forms, so they do not affect the equality. Every $W_ j$ is smooth and bounded, so $v_+(\Omega )=+\infty $. By proposition 6.5.2, $Z$ does not have the bounded mass property.

Apply 16.6.47, 16.6.49 to $p$ and the area-one form $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$. This gives all the assertions about the full model space. To see the stated distance explicitly, fix $x\in \mathbb P^1$ and let $g_ x$ be its normalized Green model, with $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ x=\delta _ x$. The models $0$ and $g_ x\circ p$ are ordinary and ideal, and the latter has a logarithmic pole of coefficient one along the smooth reduced fiber $p^{-1}(x)$. Thus both have analytic singularities. Their base polar measures are $0$ and $\delta _ x$, so their polar distance is one, although their top-degree measures vanish. $\square$

16.6.18 Complete model topologies

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now compare the complete metrics obtained so far and ask how they might extend to all ordinary models. On a curve, the total variation of polar curvature is a complete metric on ordinary models, ideal projection keeps the atomic part of the polar curvature, and the distance to the closure of the analytic models is exactly the diffuse polar mass, by corollary 30.12.3, proposition 30.12.6. This topology is stronger than $\tau _{\omega ,0}$ on ordinary models: proposition 30.12.9 exhibits a concentration of diffuse polar curvature that is lost in the latter topology.

Adding the smooth curve background as a further coordinate gives the complete space of theorem 30.14.1. Its analytic closure is the locus of ideal models, also over backgrounds of zero area, and metric convergence controls the normalized potentials in $L^1$ and in capacity. This construction keeps the positive-volume topology but imposes a different Cauchy condition at the zero-volume boundary.

For a smooth big background with bounded mass, theorem 30.11.3 gives a complete topology on ordinary models of positive lower volume. Its locus of ideal models is the completion of the analytic models of positive volume, by corollary 30.11.4.

At zero volume, the product constructions of the preceding subsections keep information that top-degree mass cannot detect. A fixed divisor with unit coefficient forces every ordinary representative of the induced models to have zero mass, while polar curvature on the curve factors still distinguishes these models and gives a complete metric whose analytic closure is exactly the locus of ideal models, with ideal projection as the unique nearest-point retraction. These conclusions hold on the product loci of proposition 16.6.26, proposition 16.6.43, including the nonclosed background with unbounded ambient mass in example 16.6.46.

Changing the curve areas rescales the polar distances, and a zero area removes the corresponding coordinate. The comparisons in proposition 16.6.35 quantify this change even when different sets of coordinates survive. If the curve backgrounds converge uniformly to forms of area zero, the diameters tend to zero and the potentials converge in $L^1$, uniformly over the model tuples, and the limit still contains the normalized smooth potentials of the limiting backgrounds, by corollary 16.6.38. These loci thus describe explicit degenerations beyond the positive-volume theory.

A curve quotient can also describe the entire model space. If $p:X\to C$ is a surjective holomorphic map with connected fibers, including critical fibers, and $\beta $ is a smooth real form of nonnegative area on the compact curve $C$, every potential in the background $\theta =p^*\beta $ descends to $C$. For $\dim X\geq 2$ all top-degree masses vanish, but the polar trace, normalized by the constant Gauduchon volume of the fiber divisor with its multiplicities, gives the complete curve metric on all ordinary $\theta $-models by 16.6.49. Its ideal subspace is the analytic closure, and 16.6.50 characterizes equality of the ordinary and ideal envelopes below every smooth obstacle. For an arbitrary surjective map to a compact curve, 1.22.9 supplies the finite factor used in 16.6.51, so disconnected fibers have the same complete model-space description. The polar trace must be kept upstairs: in 16.6.52, two distinct analytic models have the same trace pushforward to the original base curve.

We do not know how to extend the complete topology to general ordinary models while keeping analytic closure and compatibility across backgrounds. Since polar curvature is the coordinate that achieves this on a curve, it is natural to test polar curvature on all modifications in higher dimension. The rest of this subsection shows that this candidate fails in three ways: the first polar current misses isolated poles unless a modification exposes them; normalizing polar traces by Gauduchon forms makes the topology depend on the chosen forms; and diffuse poles of product type are invisible to polar curvature on every modification, although they lower the volume.

Polar curvature in higher dimension

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a curve, the polar curvature records every logarithmic pole. In higher dimension, the restriction of the curvature current to the pole set no longer does: it vanishes for an isolated logarithmic pole, although such a pole changes the multiplier ideals. The curve metric therefore cannot be extended simply by replacing the polar measure with this restricted current.

Here $X$ has dimension $n\geq 2$, and $\omega $ is a Hermitian form on $X$. For $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, the first polar current of $p$ is

\[ \operatorname {Pol}_1(p) =\mathbf1_{\{ p=-\infty \} }(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p), \]

where the restriction to the pole set is applied to the coefficient measures of the positive current. In the notation of proposition 1.25.6, $\operatorname {Pol}_1(p)=\operatorname {Pol}_\omega (p)$; it is a closed positive current.

Proposition 16.6.58. For every $x\in X$, there is $a_0>0$ such that, for each rational $a\in (0,a_0)$, there is an ideal model $p_ a$ with analytic singularities and pole set $\{ x\} $ satisfying

\[ p_ a=a\log |z|^2+O(1)\quad \text{near }x,\qquad \operatorname {Pol}_1(p_ a)=0. \]

The models $p_ a$ are pairwise ordinarily inequivalent and have pairwise distinct families of multiplier ideals $\mathcal I(tp_ a)$, $t>0$, whereas also $\operatorname {Pol}_1(0)=0$. More precisely, with $\mathfrak m_ x$ the maximal ideal at $x$,

16.49
\begin{equation} \mathcal I(tp_ a)_ x =\mathfrak m_ x^{\max \{ \lfloor ta\rfloor -n+1,0\} } \qquad (t>0). \end{equation}

Proof. Choose the cutoff potentials $u_ a$ of lemma 1.16.2 for the single point $x$, with $a_0$ smaller than the reciprocal of the constant in that lemma, and put $p_ a=P_\omega [u_ a]$. By proposition 16.2.7, $p_ a$ is an ideal model with analytic singularities, ordinarily equivalent to $u_ a$. It is locally bounded away from $x$, and its only pole has the stated logarithmic coefficient. Distinct coefficients give unbounded differences near $x$. Formula (16.49) follows from lemma 1.16.1 with codimension $n$, and its exponents distinguish any two coefficients at sufficiently large weights.

It remains to show that the current has no coefficient mass at $x$. In coordinates centered at $x$, let $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ and let

\[ \nu =(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_ a)\wedge \beta ^{n-1}. \]

This is a positive measure. Choose smooth cutoff functions $0\leq \chi _\varepsilon \leq 1$, equal to one on the ball of radius $\varepsilon $ and supported in the ball of radius $2\varepsilon $, with $-C\varepsilon ^{-2}\beta \leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _\varepsilon \leq C\varepsilon ^{-2}\beta $; their complex Hessians are supported in the annulus between the two balls. Since $\beta $ is closed, distributional integration by parts gives

\[ \int \chi _\varepsilon \, \mathrm d\nu =\int \chi _\varepsilon \omega \wedge \beta ^{n-1} +\int p_ a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _\varepsilon \wedge \beta ^{n-1}. \]

The logarithmic bound for $p_ a$ on the annulus and the Euclidean volume of the annulus therefore give

\[ 0\leq \nu (\{ x\} )\leq \int \chi _\varepsilon \, \mathrm d\nu \leq C_ a\bigl (\varepsilon ^{2n} +\varepsilon ^{2n-2}(1+|\log \varepsilon |)\bigr ) \longrightarrow 0. \]

This is where $n\geq 2$ is used. By positivity, the coefficient measures of $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_ a$ are bounded by its trace measure $\nu $, so they have no mass at $x$. Hence the restriction to the pole set is zero, as asserted. The zero potential is an ordinary and an ideal model because $\omega >0$, and its pole set is empty. $\square$

Consequently, no comparison that depends only on the fixed background and on $\operatorname {Pol}_1$ separates these analytic ideal models, and a complete topology in higher dimension must involve singularity data of higher codimension.

A modification can turn the isolated pole into a divisor. For a modification $\pi :Y\to X$, pullback maps $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ to $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\omega )$ by proposition 1.22.12, and we define

\[ \operatorname {Pol}_\pi (p) =\mathbf1_{\{ p\circ \pi =-\infty \} } (\pi ^*\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p\circ \pi )). \]

Corollary 16.6.59. Fix $x$ and the models $p_ a$ of proposition 16.6.58. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be the blowup at $x$, with exceptional divisor $E$, and let $\eta $ be a Hermitian form on $Y$. Then

\[ \operatorname {Pol}_\pi (p_ a)=a[E]. \]

For $\mu _ a=\operatorname {Pol}_\pi (p_ a)\wedge \eta ^{n-1}$ and any two admissible coefficients $a,b$,

\[ \| \mu _ a-\mu _ b\| _{\mathrm{TV}} =|a-b|\int _ E\eta ^{n-1}. \]

Proof. On relatively compact blowup charts, with $z=(s,sw)$, one has $p_ a\circ \pi =a\log |s|^2+O(1)$ along $E=\{ s=0\} $. Thus the pole set is $E$ and the generic Lelong number along it is $a$. Add a local smooth function $\rho $ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \pi ^*\omega $. By the argument of lemma 1.17.1, the restriction of the closed positive current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p_ a\circ \pi +\rho )$ to $E$ is $a[E]$, and subtracting the smooth correction does not change the coefficient mass on $E$. Consequently $\mu _ a=a[E]\wedge \eta ^{n-1}$. The positive measure $[E]\wedge \eta ^{n-1}$ has mass $\int _ E\eta ^{n-1}>0$, which proves the formula for the total variation. $\square$
